Second edition · release candidate v2.0.0-rc2 · 2026-10-19

Generation ZX Developers

GenAI Startup Edition

Dinis Cruz

Release candidate 2 of the second edition: not signed off, not an official version. The first edition (2018) is the only official version. This candidate was written by the book's agents from the ideas register and is for reading and marking up. Its text is frozen as v2/versions/v2.0.0-rc2.md; the title is Dinis's decision of 19 October 2026 (decision 014). Text: CC BY 4.0.

About this release candidate

This is release candidate 2 of Generation ZX Developers (GenAI Startup Edition), the second edition of Generation Z Developers (2018). It was not written the way the first edition was, and it is not the same book that release candidate 1 was, a few hours earlier.

In 2018 Dinis wrote the chapters himself, in a public repository, and readers sent fixes as GitHub issues. In 2026 the book is talked into existence: voice memos, written up with a journalist agent as articles in his voice, each ending with a brief for the roles that finish a book. A librarian reads every article, every decision and every one of the 2018 chapters and outlines, and writes one idea per thing the book would say: a sentence the book stands behind, with where it came from, down to the section of the article, and the 2018 file it answers. The ideas are placed on a chapter map, and each chapter is written from the ideas placed in it, by an agent that reads the articles and the 2018 text behind them and nothing else. The rule is simple: every number, every quotation and every story has to be in a source the chapter names. Where the sources run out, the chapter says so, in a note addressed to Dinis.

Release candidate 1 was the first end-to-end draft under that method: thirty chapters, written in a day. Dinis read it and arrived at the founder. The reader he wants to give this book to is the person who wants to start a GenAI company, and that reader is two generations: Generation Z, entering the workforce and becoming the entrepreneurs, and Generation X, the subject-matter experts he sees starting companies, often their parents. Together, Gen ZX, which goes back to his origins, the ZX Spectrum. The book got a title, an objective (by the end of it you have started a company), a new part at its centre, Start the company, a part rebuilt around shipping without a server, four more hands-on chapters, and a measure: the companies created from it. The articles the newsroom already had on pricing, selling, funding, zero-knowledge vaults, constraints and control came into the register as the first round's batch, and this candidate was rewritten under that frame: forty-one chapters in ten parts.

Some chapters are honest placeholders. The newsroom has no article yet on creating a limited company in the UK, on Lithuania, on Portugal, on the European Union's programmes, on the first customers, or on how a reader tells us about their company. Those chapters stand as their titles, their ideas and what is missing, until Dinis records the memos; the notes to him say which.

This is a candidate, not a version. The first edition is still the only official one. The candidate's working copy lives in the book's vault; this text is a frozen snapshot of it, so that what Dinis reads and marks up cannot change behind him, and his feedback produces the next candidate rather than an edit of this one. From here on, every word, change and idea that wants to come in goes before a review board, an advocate and a cutter and the readers and a checker and the editor, and Dinis decides; every verdict is kept with its reason and becomes a precedent. The diff between this candidate and the last is on the website, block by block, because Dinis reads the diffs.

The voice is Dinis's throughout, in the first person, because the material is his. Where a chapter sounds like him, that is the material doing its work. Where it does not, that is the agents', and it is what the review is for. The verbs he answers with are the ones the process gives him: keep, rewrite, merge, cut, article first, later, not doing it.

Read it as a reader first. Then, if you are Dinis, read it with a pen. And if you started a company because of something in it, tell us: that is the first entry in the measure.

Part 1

Before you start

Five chapters before the argument starts. Who this book is for and what it is for, why I think you are lucky to be here, one chapter on graphs because everything after it is one, how to find out which kind of developer you are, and why the path only makes sense afterwards. Read these first; then go where your map says.

1Gen ZX: who this book is for, and what it is for

The first line of the 2018 book was this: "Hi Generation Z Developer, if you are one and want to learn as much as you can about your craft, this is the book for you." One generation, one reader, one line.

This edition needs a longer first line, because it is for two generations, and because it has an objective the first one did not have. So before anything else, let me say who I am talking to, and what I want to have happened by the time you close the book.

Two generations

In 2018 I wrote that Generation Z was "the generation born after 1996", "the first real 'born online' generation", and "the generation that is entering the job market right now". I focused on them because I had two daughters in it, because I had spent time teaching UK school kids to hack in the ethical sense, and because I worried they had missed the history behind the technology they took for granted. Eight years later they are entering the workforce for real, and a lot of them are not looking for a job. They are hungry, they are focused on this, and they are the new entrepreneurs. They are still the first reader.

The second reader is my own generation. The more I thought about who I want to give this book to, and what I want them to do with it, the more I arrived at the people I actually work with. The founders I see starting companies now are, a lot of them, Generation X. They are the subject-matter experts: twenty years inside a problem, in a pharmacy, a clinic, a legal practice or a security team, who have described the tool they need to vendor after vendor and never got it.

In The expert founders I put the change in three words: "Execution just got cheap." When agents do most of the building, the person who knows the problem can build the first version of the answer themselves, instead of explaining it to a team they could never afford. And the data was always on the experts' side, which is worth hearing before somebody tells you it is too late. In the largest study I know, of 2.7 million US founders, the mean age at founding of the fastest-growing one in a thousand new companies was 45, and founders with three or more years in the same industry reached that top tier at twice the rate of those with none. What held the experts back was not knowledge. It was access: to capital, to engineers, to the long and expensive build before anything could be shown. That is the part that just got cheap. A third of new US startups now have a single founder.

Often the two readers are in the same family. Generation Z are, by the dates, the children of Generation X, which is an interesting gap: one grew up with the agents, the other grew up with the problem. In the storyboard that goes with this chapter, the mother asks whether she can just build it now, after twenty years of explaining it, and the daughter says yes, and shows her the agent. That is the book, in two lines.

The dates I use are the Pew Research Center's: Generation X born 1965 to 1980, millennials 1981 to 1996, Generation Z 1997 to 2012. Boundaries differ between sources, and like all generation boundaries they generalise; the 2018 book said so too, and I still mean it. That leaves the millennials in the middle. They can read this, and they will get a lot from it, but theirs is a different story, and I would rather write the two I know from the inside: I am Generation X, and my daughters are Generation Z.

Generation ZX

Put the two letters together and you get Generation ZX, which is a plot twist I did not plan and now cannot give up, because it goes back to my origins. The ZX Spectrum was launched by Sinclair Research in the UK in April 1982, when Generation X was between two and seventeen. If you are my age, you may have typed a program from a magazine into one, line by line, and watched it run, or not run, and gone looking for the typo. That machine is where a lot of us learnt that a computer was something you could own, open and program. The storyboard has the whole language of it on a board: two lines of BASIC, and the second one is GOTO 10.

One more thing about the name, and then I will leave it alone. It is Gen ZX, not Gen XZ. XZ is also the name of a compression library that had a backdoor planted in it in 2024, the most famous supply-chain attack of recent years, and in a book that covers security the order of the letters matters.

And they are developers. The title keeps the word on purpose. The entrepreneurs I am talking about are coding: with agents, in the way the newsroom calls explorer coding, which is a better name for what most people call vibe coding. Explorer coding says it in one line: "What people call vibe coding is exploration, and the word should say so." The explorer builds to find out, and what the exploration produces is the brief for the version other people will rely on. A founder needs both halves, the exploring and the handover, and Explorer coding, not vibe coding, and the handover is the chapter on them. Before it, Everyone who can brief an agent is a developer says why the word now includes you, even if you have never written a line of code and do not intend to read the ones the agent writes.

What it is for

The 2018 book had a chapter called Be a founder. What it meant was attitude: "act like one of the founders of the business" you work for. Imagine you were employee number four and cared deeply about the company. Ask what you would do differently, what needs to be done now that would make a big difference, what values you would fight for. Then ask the question the chapter ended on: "what is stopping you from doing just that?" It is still good advice, and the part of this book about getting a job keeps it as the then of a then-and-now. But in 2026 the advice is literal. You can be the founder.

So this book has an objective, which the first edition did not. I said it to the team making the book in one sentence: "Our job is to empower the reader to create a startup by the end of the book." This is the book you give somebody who wants to start a GenAI company. If that is you, here is what it has to contain, for real: the company itself, how to create a limited company in the UK, the case for Lithuania, Portugal, because that is where I am from, and the European Union and what exists there for a founder; the cloud providers and the startup programmes that exist right now; and the first sale.

Two honest clauses about that list. The chapters on the company are written from experience, mine and the people I work with, and they are not legal or tax advice. And the programmes and the credits change every year, so they live in the hands-on part at the end of the book, dated, to be renewed each year while the argument in the rest of the book stays put.

The shape of the middle follows from the objective. Start the company is the centre of the book: be a founder, ship it and see if they miss it, price it and then give it away, sell, fund it or do not, and the company itself. The whole thing is about shipping now. As the newsroom's The question is whether they miss it puts it: "Not to plan, not to pitch, not to architect. Ship." You ship it, and it is painful. If you give it away and they do not miss it, you try again.

Then Build it without a server: ship your startup serverless. No servers of yours with logic in them, no database of yours, the work done on the user's side. A founder with no servers has nothing to run at three in the morning and nothing to pay for when nobody is using it. SG/Send and sgit come in there, because they give you data privacy without a server that can read the data, and RiskMandate's techniques come in as the way to control the agents your company runs on. Each has its chapter, and the hands-on part ends with a product shipped that way in a weekend.

The measure

A book that is a book to read is fine. A book with an objective is a different thing: it can fail, and it can be measured. So I will say plainly how I measure this one: by how many companies have been created from it.

That means asking you to tell us what you are doing, and building a community around the people who do. The last chapter, How this book was made, and what you made, says how. If you started a company because of something you read here, that is the first entry in the measure, and I would like to hear about it before I hear about anything else.

For Dinis the measure. How does a reader tell us about their company, and is the list public? The article keeps it as your question, and the last chapter needs the answer before it can do more than ask.

For Dinis: is this the second edition of Generation Z Developers, or a new book that grew out of it? Decision 014 leaves it open. This chapter says "edition" throughout, and the publisher's brief waits on the same answer.

That is who you are, as far as this book is concerned, and what I want you to have done by the last page. The next chapter says why, whichever of the two generations you belong to, you are lucky to be starting now.

From the register: The reader this book is for is the founder, and its objective is that by the end you have started a company, Two generations, Z and X, together Gen ZX: back to the ZX Spectrum. Answers: Generation Z, Introduction.

2You are lucky to be here

I discover things I can do. That is the sentence I keep coming back to when someone asks what it is like to work the way I work now. I discover workflows. I follow an idea, and I can execute it, often the same afternoon. The feeling is not relief, and it is not fear. It is closer to wonder, and now that you know who this book is for, I want it to be the first thing in it, because it is the first thing I would want you to feel.

I heard a woman on a podcast describe the same thing: the wonder that happens with generative AI, the feeling of discovering what you can do. I recognised it at once. I cannot now say who she was, so I will not put a name to it.

For Dinis who was the podcast? If you can find it, the book should credit her here; the article left her unnamed.

How the first edition started

The 2018 book did not start with wonder. It started with a slide I had shown to a room of young people in 2017, and a question the book repeats on its first page: "Here is the slide that started it all in 2017, how many logos do you recognize?" Very few recognised them, and nobody had told them the story behind each one.

The history still matters. But the book that followed went too technical, too deep, too soon. Parts 2 and 3 were nineteen chapters of technologies to know. The argument was at the two ends, and the middle was a list.

What I had actually wanted is in that book's introduction, under the heading "Overwhelming curiosity": moments when you "follow link after link about a particular topic" and think it is awesome. The 2018 book called that place the zone: "If you are lucky enough to find yourself in this place, congratulations! You just found 'the zone'." The second edition starts there. Not with what you should know, but with what you can now do, and the wonder of discovering it.

In 2018 I also asked, of those logos, "Why is the why important?", and I had an outline for a chapter called Start with why, after Simon Sinek's golden circle: why before how before what. It was never written. So let me start with the why of this book, in one sentence.

You are lucky to be here

Whoever is reading this in 2026 or 2027 is ridiculously privileged to be living at this moment in time, in the middle of a huge transition in society, while the pieces are still landing.

I know that is not what you hear. If you are starting out, what you hear is that AI took the jobs before you got there. If you are my age, what you hear is that it is too late for you. I tried to read one of the first kind of essays this month, in Business Insider, under the headline "I thought working in AI would put me ahead of the curve. Two years in, I've never felt more behind and tired of the topic." I could not get past the paywall, so I cannot tell you what it argued. In Europe in particular, I think young people are being fed a lot of fear about AI, and are not embracing it the way they should. That is my reading, not a measurement, and this edition still owes you a proper answer to it.

I am not going to tell you that none of the fear is right. Projects will be cancelled and money will be lost; every transition has had that. But the fear is what you hear. It is not where this book starts, and I do not think it is where you should start either.

Here is my position, not a forecast: ignore the doom, because nobody knows what is going to happen. Every time we had one of these technological transformations, people said the same things, and they always said this time is going to be different. And each time the paths evolved: industries changed completely, jobs disappeared and appeared, and the people who learned the new tools early did well out of it.

The transformations I have lived through

I have seen several in my own lifetime, each a revolution that changed whole industries, and if you are my generation you have seen most of them too. The dates are the commonly recorded ones.

  • The home computer, my ZX Spectrum, in the early 1980s. A computer became something you could own, open and program.
  • The BBS, in the 1980s and early 1990s. Text messages and chat between strangers, over a phone line.
  • The internet, in the 1990s. Everything published, linked and searchable.
  • The mobile phone, in the late 1990s, and the smartphone from 2007. The network in your pocket.
  • The cloud, from the mid 2000s. Servers you rent by the hour instead of buy.
  • The apps, from 2008, with the app stores. Software delivered to everyone, instantly.
  • Generative AI, from November 2022. Software you can talk to, and that can build software.

The 2018 book had an outline for the second of those, BBS and modems, with a story I still have to tell properly: blowing up my home's telephone system trying to connect to one.

The only difference this time, I sometimes joke, is that this one is televised: far more visible, because of the money, the players, and how much of the stack it touches at once. It will also take longer to land than the headlines say; it just feels as if it is happening all at once.

I lived through the dot-com crash: enormous hype, then a crash. The Nasdaq peaked in March 2000 and had lost roughly four fifths of its value by October 2002, and the internet still changed everything. We might have a crash or a correction this time too; nobody can say when, or whether. None of that makes the transformation less real. A crash would change who makes money; it would not undo what people can now do.

For Dinis does the dot-com crash belong here, on the first pages, or later in the book? The article left it as your question. It is here for now because part of the reader's fear is a fear of exactly this crash.

My own last two years are the counter-story to the burnout essay. In Two years in, the opposite I put it this way: "I have been doing AI for the last two years and a bit, and I have never felt more productive." I have never felt happier at work, or more empowered. There is an anti-AI mood at the moment, and stories of burnout feed it; stories like mine should be told too. It is one person's account, not a study.

A couple of years ago I wondered whether we had missed the boat; it felt as if all the big things had been done. Now the pieces of the puzzle are landing again. This is the biggest opportunity of your career, whether that career is starting or is twenty years in: the moment when you can still be a player, before things stabilise, as they always do, until the next big thing.

Technology that finally works for you

For a long time technology has not worked for you. If you are Generation Z, you in particular were handed a technology stack designed to hook you, and to make you a passive user. There was some creating, but mostly you were a consumer, living in a world of advertising, attention and dopamine. I believe that will one day be treated the way tobacco is today; that is my belief, and the evidence is still being gathered. The 2018 book had a draft chapter on dopamine that already called phones' attention-grabbing activities "the new smoking".

My generation was privileged in a different way: we could build things. A whole generation grew up programming, building computers, mixing parts. A computer was not something that just arrived; you could take it apart. And then, somewhere along the way, most of us stopped, and became users of software that other people made for our fields, and not very happy ones. One of you was handed technology to use, not technology to build; the other put the building down. Now you can both build again.

Here is what that looks like in practice. For twenty years the instinct in software was that a custom interface is expensive, so everyone gets the generic one: the inbox, the ticket, the form. In Custom UIs are not the exception I argued that this is now backwards: "a custom interface per message, per thread, per topic, per question is not an exception. It is how interfaces get made." When an interface costs an afternoon, you stop asking whether it is worth building and start asking which moment it is for.

The wonder underneath that has a name in my influences. Bret Victor's talk Inventing on Principle, from 2012, says that a creator needs to see what they are making, as they make it. Every step between you and the thing you are making, a compile, a deploy, a refresh, is a place where an idea can die. Generative AI removes a lot of those steps. The 2018 book had an outline for that talk too, with a note that is very much me: "print this and read it".

None of this is only for the creative ones. It is not that you have to be creative, and if you are not, you are left out. It is that you can now scale the things you are not good at. You can have technology work for you.

And it is why I am an optimist about where technology goes. In 2018 I recommended Kevin Kelly's The Inevitable, because of the way he maps what is likely to happen next: technology evolves with tendencies of its own. Which of his books I would hand you first is a question I have not settled. The frame of this book, which Where do you land? introduces, is built on that kind of seeing: things move in a direction, and the people who can see it can be early.

How to read this book

Read it on paper, with a pen. Books, and narratives, are still the best way we have to take in information, and your brain takes it in much better from a page you can mark. If you are reading this as a PDF, print it. The 2018 book had a chapter called Pen and Paper that said "analogue techniques like the pen and paper are actually better technologies for creating and capturing ideas." I still think so, though that is my experience rather than a finding.

Read it somewhere you can give it your undivided attention. That is a skill, and the best professionals in the world have it. The phone you were handed was designed to take it from you.

Then use the rest of the book the way Where do you land? describes: find your centre of gravity first, and read the chapters marked for it before the others. If you are here to start a company, know where you are heading: the centre of the book is the part called Start the company, and the hands-on part at the end is where you form it, price it and ship it. The chapters before them are the ground those stand on, so do not skip them. Connect the dots backwards is about how to learn, and its first lesson is that your path will only make sense looking back, so you do not have to see it now. You have to start.

That, in the end, is why the title says developer. Craftspeople made their own tools. In the past they did it out of necessity; now you can do it again, because the tools can be made to fit you, and because, as Everyone who can brief an agent is a developer argues, the bar has dropped. The person who has spent twenty years inside a problem is the craftsperson here, and the first tool they make may be the first version of their company. It is still your name on the work, and it is still your thinking. You think with the AI; you do not delegate your thinking to it.

You are lucky to be here. Not because it will be easy, but because the pieces are still landing, and you can still be the one who places some of them.

From the register: The book opens with wonder, not fear and not technologies, Whoever reads this now is lucky to be here, Ignore the doom: nobody knows what happens next, Seven transformations, lived through: the Spectrum, the BBS, the internet, the mobile phone, the cloud, the apps, AI, Technology can finally work for you, Read it on paper, with a pen, The fear, impostor syndrome and anxiety about AI among young people, The immediate connection between a creator and what they make is the wonder the book opens with, Purpose, and optimism: Sinek for why, Kevin Kelly for what technology wants. Answers: Introduction, Generation Z, The Zone, BBS and Modems, Dopamine.

3Everything is connected: graphs

Think of the board on the wall in a detective story. Photographs pinned up, notes under them, and red string running from one to another. The detective stands in front of it and asks one kind of question, every time: what connects A to B? Who knew whom, who was where, what links these two. Everything on that board is either a thing or a connection between two things.

That board is a graph. If you have followed one of those stories to the end, you already know how to read one.

I have met people for whom a graph is a picture with boxes, or a chart with bars. So let me say early what this book means by the word, because it is one of the most fundamental ideas in it, and it comes back in almost every chapter.

A graph is a network, not a diagram

A graph is a network: nodes, and the edges that connect them. The first of the graphs books, written with agents on graphs.sgit.ai, puts it in one line: "In this book a graph is a network: nodes and the edges that connect them. Never a chart, never a figure." And its opening sentence says why the edges matter more than the nodes: "A node connected to nothing is literally meaningless."

People mean three things when they say graph. They mean networks, for analysis: who is connected to whom. They mean graph databases, for storing and fetching things fast. And they mean semantics: what something means, which in a graph comes from what it is connected to and how. That is the graphs book's way of dividing the word; others divide it differently. This book uses the first and the third senses, and almost never needs the second.

In 2018 I left an outline for a chapter called Graphs and never wrote it. It had four bullets: "Graphs are everywhere", "I've been thinking in graphs for years", "very hard paradigm shift to make", "requires an ability to think in nodes and edges". That is this chapter in miniature. Eight years later the shift still feels hard to explain and easy to show, which is why the storyboards in this book draw graphs, the detective's board among them.

For Dinis the 2018 outline's first bullet says "See my presentation on Graphs". Which presentation did you mean? If it still exists, the chapter should name it and take from it.

The whodunit

The detective's board is the easiest way in, and it is why I think a reader of Generation Z will find this chapter easier than my generation did. In Follow the rabbit holes I said that people underestimate the young: they are very good at connecting the dots, very good at finding things, very good detectives. That is my experience, from my family and the young people I have worked with, not a study. But it is what thinking in graphs is: tracing what an unfamiliar thing connects to, trusting a claim because of where it came from, asking what else breaks if this one fails. The graphs book has a line for anyone who thinks this sounds exotic: "you may already think in graphs without ever having called it that."

If you are my age and twenty years inside a field, that line is for you too. You carry a graph of your field in your head, every rule and every exception and what each one touches; you have just never been asked to draw it, and this book will ask.

The board is a teaching device. Real investigations are messier than red string. But the question stays the same, from A to B, and once you see that the question is about edges and not about the boxes, you can ask it of anything.

A map is a graph with positions

Take the same nodes and the same edges, and now give every node a place on the page that means something. You have a map.

In my talk on why Wardley maps changed how I think, the first two shifts were exactly that: "Its a graph with context", and "When a graph becomes a Map". A Wardley map is a graph where each node has a position on two axes: how visible it is to the user at the top, and how evolved it is, from a brand-new idea to something everyone takes for granted. The positions are what make it a map and not only a graph; the edges are what make it a graph and not only a picture.

The 2018 book said the same thing from the other side, in the chapter Dot Language, where I complained that most diagrams in software "are usually not maps": they have components and context but no anchor, no position relative to the anchor, and no consistency of movement. That chapter was about drawing diagrams as text, so that software could draw them and a team could version them like code. Its real complaint was that we build "large interconnected and complex structures without accurate maps", and its best line is one I still believe: "What is crazy is to make decisions based on shadows of reality."

That is why this chapter and Where do you land? sit next to each other. One gives you the graph; the other gives it positions and asks where you are on it.

For Dinis graphs before Wardley maps, or the other way round? The chapter map puts this one first because a map is a graph with positions; the article leaves the order as your question.

Graphs of graphs

Here is the part that took me longest to see, and that I now cannot unsee. Graphs nest. Zoom into a node and you find another graph inside it, with its own kinds of nodes and its own kinds of edges. Zoom out and the whole graph is one node in a bigger one.

The newsroom's introduction to what I call fractal semantic graphs puts the consequence in two sentences: "There is no top and no bottom. There is only the altitude you happen to be looking from, and how much definition you choose to load at it." A law is a node in a graph of laws. Open it and it is a graph of articles. Open an article and it is a graph of the terms it uses. Each level has its own vocabulary, and none of them was made to match the others. What connects them is that each one is still a graph.

In a way, you can see nature like this too: a system, the molecules in it, the particles in those, everything connected to everything else, and groups of groups forming the layers we think in. That is a way of seeing, not a claim in physics. But it is how I view the world: it is made of connections. Groups of groups are the natural abstraction layers, and abstraction layers are how we think.

It is also why provenance is a graph. Where a claim came from is a chain of edges back to its source, and the newsroom's rule for a record is that it should be "not a pile of articles, but a graph whose every edge has a date and a source". A chain that ends in a rumour is a finding too. Facts, evidence and the record is built on that.

Code is already a graph

The 2018 book had a second chapter that was a graph without saying so. AST opened by calling an abstract syntax tree "a graph representation of source code", and it told you that if you could "see code or raw data as a graph", you would have made a paradigm shift that would help you across your career. I still think so, and I now think it goes further than I said then.

Source code is the best example of a fractal graph that I know, because the layers are not an interpretation; they are a fact about the thing. At the top is a person trying to do something. Below that, the features and flows. Below that, the components people draw when you ask for the architecture. Then the classes, then the methods and the calls between them, then the syntax tree, and on down if you want to. Each layer is a graph with its own vocabulary, and you change universe when you step from one to the next.

What changed since 2018 is that naming the nodes and the edges is now cheap. A language model can read a function and say what it does, and propose the edge between two nodes faster than a person can type it. So a change to the code can be read at every layer, not only as lines. A refactor is a change that moves the bottom layers and leaves the stories at the top still. A bug is a story that stopped holding, and its fix is visible at the top as that story holding again. The blast radius of a change is the climb from the methods it touched up to the stories that can reach them, and you can read it before anyone runs anything.

This is the only honest answer I have to the characteristic failure of AI-generated code: the change made in one place that breaks something three packages away. In a text diff that is invisible; in the layered graph it is a changed node that reaches a command the author never looked at. In the newsroom I worked this through on a real tool, the sgit command line, read as layered graphs with nothing run: one commit of 75 lines added and 7 removed in three files turned out to reach nine of the tool's 72 commands and six of its eleven user stories. The stories were hand-written, the example runs nothing, and it is published as a vault with the read key on the page, so you can check it. The technique underneath, following the call tree from one method and writing out only that code, is one I used for code review on the O2 Platform more than a decade ago and described in the 2018 AST chapter as method streams. It is back, and it is one script now.

If you want to own code in production and answer for it, this is your chapter as much as the detective's board is. And if you are a founder whose agents wrote most of the product, it is yours too: this is how you read what they made at the altitude you need, the stories at the top, without pretending you have read every line.

Start anywhere

Here is the thing about graphs that I most want you to take from this chapter, because it is about how you feel when you look at everything there is to learn, or at everything a company needs before it exists.

A graph has no front page. It has no top, so there is no right place to begin. Whatever node you start from, the edges take you to the rest. It does not matter where you start; it matters that you start. It does not matter, in a way, whether you are going straight in the right direction; it matters that you get movement. You start to learn, you start to connect the dots, and the connections do the rest.

So when it feels like too much, and it will, pick a node. Any node. Start there and follow one edge. Then another. Connect the dots backwards says that your path will only make sense afterwards, and this is why: a path is a walk through a graph, and the graph does not care where the walk began.

The hands-on part has a chapter, Your first graph, built by hand, where you draw one of your own. You do not need it to read on. You need only to remember, when the word comes up, that a graph is nodes and the edges between them, that everything in this book connects to something else in it, and that you can start wherever you are.

From the register: A graph is a network of nodes and edges, not a diagram, The whodunit is the easiest way in: the detective's board is a graph, A map is a graph with positions; a Wardley map is a graph with context, Graphs nest, and so does the world: groups of groups are the abstraction layers we think in, You can start anywhere: it matters that you start, and get movement, The 2018 book was already there: Dot Language and AST were graphs without saying so, Source code is a fractal semantic graph, and a review should read every layer of it. Answers: graphs, Dot Language, AST (Abstract Syntax Tree).

4Where do you land?

Take someone's first job, or the first product of a company that does not exist yet, and draw what it depends on. Put the person at the top, with the thing they need to get done. Under that, the things that serve the need: the way they work, the tools, the models those tools call, the computers the models run on. Now order it twice. Down the page, from what the person sees every day to what they never see. Across the page, from the brand new and badly understood on the left to the everyday thing everyone takes for granted on the right.

You have just drawn a map. I have not named it yet, on purpose. Look at it first.

The models are on the right, moving further right: a product becoming a commodity. The way of working with agents is on the far left, new and rough. Everything on the map is moving, and all of it the same way: from left to right, from rare to ordinary. Once you see that movement you cannot stop seeing it, and that is the point of this chapter.

Maps everywhere, shown before they are named

The map you just looked at is a Wardley map, after Simon Wardley, and they run through this whole book. You will meet them in every part, sometimes with their names on and sometimes, as above, without. I want you to feel the pattern before you learn the vocabulary: the user, the need, the chain of things that serve it, and the movement.

A Wardley map is a graph with two axes of context. Everything is connected: graphs gave you the graph. The first axis is vertical: a value chain, anchored at the top on a user and a need, each component drawn under the components that need it. The second is horizontal: evolution, how far each component has travelled from an idea nobody has built to something you buy by the unit without thinking. Wardley's four stages, genesis, custom built, product and commodity, are the scale on every map in this book.

Every dot is a claim. The positions are judgements, and the judgements are the argument. A map you cannot be wrong about is a diagram.

Why do I care so much? In 2022 I gave a talk for the Open Security Summit on why Wardley maps changed how I think, and listed eleven shifts they caused in me. The eleventh was: "Wardley Maps gives me inner peace (allowed me to understand why things happen)". Once a thing is on a map, placed and connected, I stop carrying it in my head. The map also tells you what to build and what to buy, and whether a market exists at all: a commodity layer is a hard sell, and the custom building on top of it is where the market is. If you are about to start a company, that last sentence is the first thing the map does for you.

The four stages are not only for activities

When people start with maps, I see a lot of them stop at the four stages most examples use, and treat them as a scale for activities only. The movement is everywhere. For that talk I made a table of it. The first four rows are from Wardley's published material; the rows below the line are my own explorations.

  • Activity: genesis, custom build, product, commodity.
  • Practice: novel, emerging, good, best.
  • Data: unmodelled, divergent, convergent, modelled.
  • Knowledge: concept, hypothesis, theory, accepted.
  • Openness of content: confidential, published with ordinary copyright, CC BY, CC0.
  • Openness of software: proprietary, GPL, open source, public domain.
  • Backup restore time: infinity (no backups), a week, a day, real time.
  • Data, by shape: unstructured raw text, spreadsheet, graph, map.

There are more rows in the original, on incident response, security logs, risk, attacker motivation, concepts and ideas. Evolution is in how open a piece of writing is, how fast a team can recover, whether your data is a pile of text or a map. Once you see it, you see it in your own work and your own career.

Where do you fit?

Now the question I most want this book to ask you, early and often: where are you on it?

Wardley named them: pioneers, settlers and town planners in 2015, and since 2023 explorers, villagers and town planners. Explorers "explore never-before-discovered concepts, the uncharted land"; "They show you wonder but they fail a lot." Villagers "turn the half baked thing into something useful for a larger audience". Town planners "take something and industrialise it taking advantage of economies of scale." The 2018 book had this as an outline, never written: explain the concept, show how everybody is different, connect it to Agile, Lean and Six Sigma. This chapter does the first two.

I think these are personalities, and I think Wardley nailed them. In business we keep finding the same thing over and over, in Myers-Briggs and in whatever test is in fashion, and in my experience the three do a better job of describing the people I have worked with. That is my experience, not research; the business tests have a contested evidence base of their own. What I can tell you is what I have seen.

The explorer is bursting with ideas. They love to experiment, to try things out. Once it works, it gets boring, and they build on top of it. They are not the person playing with the Lego; they want to invent the bricks. Failure is exciting, because failure is just an experiment. Change is natural to them, and the immediate connection with what they are making, the instant feedback, matters to them more than to anyone. What they make only works for early adopters, because it is going to break: full of wires, half-baked, a prototype.

The villager loves the user. They are emotionally attached to the user being happy, almost the user themselves; they put themselves in the middle of the problem and solve it. They thrive on packaging, releasing, going to market, taking something and refining it. They do not like failure that much, because for them the product has to work. Often it is about trimming it down, about simplicity, which is where design comes in: the first version just has to work; the second version has to be designed. They are the ones who let things scale; without them, everything would die before it reached the other side.

The town planner geeks out on the details. A good friend of mine says boring is good, and that is them: the nice hum of effectiveness, marginal gains, scale, bigger volumes. They like to be invisible, because they know they have done the job right when nothing goes wrong. Their work is making something a commodity, which is still hard. They are the people who pay attention to the letters, the words, the dotted i's, the grammar, the code. That is craftsmanship, the obsession with getting it right, and it needs less recognition than the product people need, which is fine.

If the third one is you, and you are a little proud of doing things by hand and sceptical of shortcuts, hold on to that. The newsroom's argument in Explorer coding is that what people call vibe coding is explorer work, and that an explorer-coded app "is not ready to be in line": once other people depend on it, it is mission critical. Villagers make it safe to depend on; town planners own the code in production and are accountable for it. The further towards commodity a thing moves, "the fewer agents in line, and the more deterministic code". Your centre of gravity is not out of date. It is where the explorers' work has to end up.

And if you are the founder, you need both halves. The explorer in you builds to find out, and what the exploration produces is the brief for the version other people will rely on; the handover to the people who maintain it and own it is where a company starts to exist. Explorer coding, not vibe coding, and the handover takes both halves up.

A centre of gravity, not a box

Everyone has a centre of gravity. Some can play at several levels, but there is a centre, and knowing it helps. The test is simple. What do you care about? What makes you lose your sense of time? What is the thing that, when you do it, time just flies? That is where you are.

It moves. As your career changes, your centre can move with it. But it matters to recognise where you are now, because the skills you need as an explorer are different from the skills you need as a villager, which are different again from a town planner's. All three are brilliant. They are brilliant at different things, and they should be learning different things.

This is a lens, not a label. A software vendor has spent thirty years trying to be all three for its users, and in Software vendors should become villagers and town planners I argued that users are now better explorers of their own needs than the company will ever be, so the company should let them explore on top and sell them the maintenance of what they build: "What stops me is not building it; it is maintaining it." The same handover, from the one who explores to the ones who maintain and own, is how a team works, how a career moves, and how a one-person company grows into one with a second person in it.

How to use this book

Every chapter carries a badge, in black and white, because the book will be printed that way. A compass marks a chapter that leans to the explorer. A house marks one for the villager. A grid marks one for the town planner. A chapter for everyone carries all three. The badges are a first attempt, drawn for the storyboards, and whether they work is still open.

Work out your centre of gravity first, using the test above. Then read the chapters for everyone, and the chapters with your badge, before the others. An explorer will want the chapters on explorer coding and on building fast with agents early; a villager the ones on shipping, on selling and on your own newsroom; a town planner the ones on controlling your agent, on the record, on serverless for real, and on reading code as a graph. Then come back for the rest; you will work with the other two every day, and if the company is yours, you will hire them.

The last part, dated 2026, is hands on: workflows you can set up this week, and the company you can form, price and ship this year. It will be renewed as the tools and the programmes change; the argument in the rest of the book should not need to be. Whether the book should one day be grouped by centre of gravity, rather than only marked, is a question I have not settled; for now, the badges carry it.

The jobs that never scaled

One more thing, because the map is also about where the work is.

The opportunity is not only in new jobs. It is in jobs we have been doing for a very long time, sometimes for millennia, that never really scaled and never had good technology, done one person at a time. We can now scale those jobs with agents. The map shows you where: a layer where everyone is custom building their own version of the same thing, on top of commodities, is a market, and each of those builders would rather buy.

If you have spent twenty years inside one of those jobs, you are no longer waiting for somebody to build the technology it never had. You can build the first version yourself, with agents, and the map tells you whether anyone else would buy it. If you are starting out, the same map tells you which of those jobs is still waiting for someone to try.

So when you ask where you land, ask it twice. Where on the spectrum, explorer to town planner, does time fly for you? And which old job, now that it can finally have good technology, would you want to be the one to scale?

For Dinis three examples of jobs that never scaled, and now can. The article asks for them and the chapter needs them here; without them this section is a claim without a case.

From the register: The opportunity is in old jobs too: the ones that never scaled, and now can, Wardley maps run through the whole book, shown before they are named, The four stages of evolution are not only for activities, Explorer, villager, town planner: personalities, each with a centre of gravity, Each centre of gravity needs different skills and a different learning path. Answers: Are you a pionner, Settlers or Town Planners.

5Connect the dots backwards

Steve Jobs said it in his commencement address at Stanford on 12 June 2005: "you can't connect the dots looking forward; you can only connect them looking backwards." His own example was a calligraphy class he took after dropping out of college. Nobody would have put it on a programmer's syllabus. Ten years later it came back as the typography of the first Macintosh.

If you are coming to this from somewhere else, from running a team, from a job that never involved code, or from twenty years in a field whose software you are about to build yourself, that sentence is for you. You cannot see where this goes from here. Nobody can. So you have to trust that the dots will connect, and trust your gut.

Connect the dots backwards

Trusting your gut is not the same as having no method. The newsroom already has one for walking a path you cannot see ahead of you, and the book will use it. The path is not a straight line. What matters is knowing which decisions are one-way doors and which are two-way doors, so that you go through the two-way ones quickly and think hard only about the others. It matters that you can iterate very fast. It matters that you play the game of constraints, and that you work where the smallest move shifts the most.

I have a small dot of my own from this year. I took SG/Send, a file-sending service where the server holds nothing, to market end to end: a checkout live on 1 March, ten paying customers the next day, and by 5 March a brief saying "Users buy credits, test once, and don't come back." That is where I learnt Stripe, pricing and everything else a real product needs, so by the newsroom I already knew how; and the same technology is in use now because the newsroom, RiskMandate and the vaults need it. In From Send to sgit I called it "a good example of how exploring something early pays off later, in a way nobody plans." Explorers find things early; the use case decides when they matter.

Your future self will connect the dots. Your job now is to make dots worth connecting.

For Dinis your own dots, connected backwards. The article asks which three make the best story; SG/Send above, and The Cyber Boardroom and MyFeeds below, are three candidates. Are they the ones, or is the story elsewhere?

The edge is multidisciplinary

What I most want to stress is that, more and more, it is the multidisciplinary person who has the edge. Not someone going in a huge number of directions at once, and not chaos. The ability to learn, and to intersect disciplines.

If you have spent years doing something other than programming, that is not a gap in your CV. It is the discipline you are about to intersect. The 2018 chapter on learning said that "being a better musician, artist, athlete, car mechanic or philosopher will make you a better developer", and that is truer now, because the wall of syntax and setup has come down, and what is left is knowing what to build and for whom. That is what you bring, and for a founder it is the advantage.

That is why the humanities matter. And it is why, in a way, mathematics matters.

We teach maths as if to make people hate it

The reality is that we do a horrible job of teaching maths. One of the books I love is A Mathematician's Lament by Paul Lockhart, an essay from 2002 and a book in 2009, which says, in effect, that if we had set out to design a system to make people hate mathematics, we could not have done better. It opens with a nightmare in which music is taught the way maths is: notation and rules for years, and never a song.

The same is true of music, and of much else. We give the solution away. We do not teach the wonder, we do not teach the struggle, and we do not let people be creative. There is a story, often retold, that children start out very creative and school beats it out of them; the retellings are better known than the original data, so I will not lean on it until I know what was actually measured.

If you hated maths at school, you were probably taught it badly. You were not born without it. The 2018 outline for a chapter called Love maths had one idea I still like: get people to do the maths that sends a rocket to space, with real problems, the way the human computers did.

Learning, made for you

Our education system was designed for a very specific type of person, with a very specific set of skills. If you were very good at maths of the school kind, at memorising, at a certain kind of academic writing, you did well. That is a small subset of the population. I was not the best at that, and I think that is why I gravitated to computing: it let me be creative, and it let me find the zone.

You know the line about judging a fish by how fast it can climb a tree. It is usually attributed to Einstein, without evidence. Judged that way, the fish believes it is stupid. The academic model was generic, made for a mythical student, too high for some and too low for others.

That has changed. This is about you. How you like to learn, how you like to take in information, how you like to get lost in something. I learn more now than I ever have, because I can shape what I learn to what I am curious about and to how I like to learn, and go deep exactly where my gaps are. And you can now build the tools for that.

There is science behind this. Mihály Csíkszentmihályi's Flow, from 1990, describes the state the 2018 book called the zone: clear goals, immediate feedback, and a challenge matched to your skill. He named programming as one of the activities that can produce it. Anders Ericsson's Peak, from 2016, is the science behind learning as a skill: expertise comes from structured practice with feedback, at the edge of what you can do, not from the number of hours. Ericsson did the research behind the ten-thousand-hours number that someone else made famous, and the credit belongs with him. The 2018 chapter on learning said the same in Kathy Sierra's words from Badass: practise right, and consume large amounts of good examples. What agents change is that both halves, the right practice and the good examples, can now be made for you.

Think with it; do not delegate your thinking to it

A search engine that gives you information from anywhere in the world was already a ridiculous technology, when you think about it, and my generation was the first to have it.

But search gave you an answer, without the ability to question it, to go deeper, to explore. Generative AI gives you the ability to really go deep: to challenge, to ask the questions, and to ask for the evidence. That is the difference between using it to skip understanding and using it to understand. If you ask it for the answer and paste it in, you have learned nothing and put your name on something you cannot defend. If you ask it why, then where that came from, then what would show it was wrong, you are learning.

It is still your responsibility. It is still your name; the work is still done under you, and if the company is yours, so is everything the agents build in it. This is not you delegating your thinking to generative AI; it is you thinking with it. I do it when I write: I ask the agent helping me with this book to double-check my quotes, to make sure what I am saying is coherent, and to find the evidence behind every statement. The name of the author of A Mathematician's Lament, which I remembered wrongly in the memo behind this chapter, was corrected that way.

And sometimes, as I always say, you use generative AI so that you do not have to use generative AI: you use it to write the script or the tool that then does the job reliably, with no model in the loop.

Generative AI lets you explore again

Exploring is back, and so is communicating in ways you could not afford before.

Look at what happened while this book was being written. I wanted a better way to communicate the book, and to think about it, than infographics, which take too long and cost a new image for every change. So I asked for something programmatic instead. What came back was a cast of stick figures drawn by code, in the browser, every time the page loads, from a data file per storyboard. The renderer is about 400 lines. The storyboards in this book are those. That is how I like to think, and that is the beauty of it: you can now discover how you like to work, and build the thing that lets you.

When people tell me they do not see it, or that they are overwhelmed, my argument is: experiment, learn, try, start, and adjust. Let your questions guide you.

Learning is a lifelong skill

Learning is a skill. Take it seriously, because it is a lifelong one. The 2018 book opened its chapter on it with a question I would still ask you first: "Do you know how to learn?" And it said what I still think: learning is like a muscle, and the more you do it, the better you become.

Historically, the roles and opportunities open to people were limited by geography, money and power. Now you can be in the middle of nowhere, with an internet connection and, less and less, a bit of compute, and have the learning systems of the world in front of you. The future, as I see it, is decentralised and sovereign: local compute, local systems. That is my position, not a forecast.

And here is a point I want you to hear plainly: by reading this book, you are already in a very small minority. The numbers behind that are the newsroom's own arithmetic on other people's surveys, for sizing, not for adding up: professional developers are about 0.6% of the world's internet users, and in the OECD's tests of adult skills across 33 rich countries, 26% of adults could not use a computer at all, and only 5% reached the level of a skilled user. You are already ahead, and the gap between you and the people who have not started is the opportunity, and the responsibility, that Civic responsibility is about.

For Dinis what does multidisciplinary look like for an explorer, a villager and a town planner? The article asks it as an interview question; one example each would anchor this chapter for the three readers.

The thinking is never wasted

For a founder, connecting the dots backwards has a practical form: a pivot leaves assets, if you did the work in the open. Two of mine, summarised from memory; the proofs of concept are not all published.

The Cyber Boardroom was a GenAI platform to help boards with cyber security, with an advisor called Athena. It taught me what personas, and output shaped to the person reading it, are worth; there is a direct line from it to the personas in RiskMandate and the newsroom today. What it needed and did not have was somewhere to keep the data: nobody wants confidential board material in an environment somebody else runs. Ironically, what it needed is what I eventually built as sgit. It was open source, so I could pivot, into MyFeeds: semantic graphs of the news, personalised all the way to the reader. That is when I realised that language models were how graphs could scale. I built a good proof of concept, took it to the industry, and there was silence: it needed micropayments, paying for the graph and paying the people who write, and the industry was not there. So I paused it.

What stayed was the thinking. The pieces I wrote in 2025, on monetising trust, on fact provenance and on micro and nano payments, are the foundations the newsroom now stands on, and I can now go back to the publishers with an end-to-end example, all running, all open source. If it does not land in 2026, I will try again in 2027. As I put it in Not a straight line: "Because it was open and written down, it was there when the moment came."

That is the founder's version of the calligraphy class. You cannot know which experiment will pay. You can make sure that when one pauses, what it taught you is written down where you, and other people, can find it.

Know where your ideas come from

One last thing, and it is an exercise.

Nothing in this book came from nowhere. The frame is Wardley's; the zone is Csíkszentmihályi's. I keep a register of my influences, as claims you can check rather than a page of warm sentences; its own first article says that "An entry in this register is a falsifiable claim about my codebase." Each entry names the one work that did the shaping, the principle it gave me in a sentence, where that principle shows up in my work, and where it is still absent. The "where this comes from" boxes in this book, and the appendix at the end, are made from it.

Do the same. Pick the three people or works that have shaped how you think. For each, write the one work that did it, the principle you took from it in one sentence anyone could use, where that principle shows up in something you have made, and where it does not yet. That is a register. It is also a CV that shows how you think, and a reading list for the next person.

And write it on paper first. The 2018 chapter Pen and Paper said it in one line: "The reality is that you will forget the ideas you are having today!" The only way to make sure your future self has them is to capture them now. Then, when you look back, you will have the dots.

From the register: Learning can be made for you, not for a mythical average student, Think with the AI; never delegate your thinking to it, A path only makes sense looking back, The edge is now multidisciplinary, We teach maths, and music, as if to make people hate them, Generative AI lets you explore and communicate again, By reading this you are already in a small minority, and geography no longer decides, Flow and deliberate practice are the science of the zone and of learning, Know where your ideas come from: keep your own register, The thinking is never wasted: pause in the open and pick up when the moment comes. Answers: Learning, Books, Pen and Paper, Love Maths, Thinking as programmer.

Part 2

The frame

The frame the rest of the book hangs on. What generative AI is and is not, what happens between your prompt and the answer, why the word developer now means anyone who can brief an agent, and the founder's way of building: explore first, call it what it is, then hand it over.

6What generative AI is

In 2018 I wrote that one of the most important areas you needed to understand in the next five years was machine learning and artificial intelligence. I said it was not a Skynet scenario, and that the question was "whether you are going to be a player or a pawn in what is happening next". Then I pointed you at the machine learning services the cloud providers had started to offer, and at three books. That was the whole chapter.

I was right about the five years and wrong about where to look. What arrived was not the AI I was describing. So before this book says anything else about what you can do, or what you can build a company on, I want to be exact about what it is talking about.

AI is a huge bucket

AI has become a huge bucket, and to be honest the word gets abused a lot. Inside the bucket there is AI in the wider sense, there is machine learning, there is deep learning, and there is generative AI. They are not the same thing, and most of what you read online does not say which one it means.

The technology that is changing the world, and the technology that is driving my imagination and my capabilities right now, is the generative kind. So this book says "generative AI" when it means that, and "AI" only when it means the wider field.

There will be very few examples here of AI out in the field recognising faces, scoring loans or recommending films. That AI is huge and has a lot of interesting applications. It is not what this book is about. This book is about the AI that creates something from the input it is given.

How I understand it

The interesting property of generative AI is in the name: it generates the output. There are the transformers and all that jazz, but for me the interesting part is simpler. The data goes into a model. The model builds its own interpretation of it. Then it recreates an output from that interpretation. There is an abstraction layer between what goes in and what comes out: the input becomes an intermediate representation inside the model, and the output, an image or a text, is generated from that representation.

Inside a language model, what happens is that it predicts the next word. That sounds small. I wrote in the newsroom, in Who owns the words, that "predicting the next word" is accurate and demeaning at the same time. It is accurate because that is the mechanism. It is demeaning because of what the prediction is made from: an incredibly rich picture of the world, of how things connect, which the model narrows down based on your prompt.

That is why the better your prompt, the better the output. When I talk for ten minutes before I ask for anything, as I did in the memo this chapter comes from, it is not politeness. The model knows better which concepts I am talking about. The prompt pulls it towards the part of what it knows that I mean.

Where hallucinations come from

I think that is also where hallucinations come from. Just about everything is connected to everything, and from inside the model they are all just connections. It is very hard for a model to know where the lines are. Giving it text helps so much because the text in your prompt is like a short-term memory that says: these are the connections that actually hold here.

In a talk I gave in Lisbon in June 2024 I said hallucinations "Are a FEATURE when creating content and when we want creativity, But are a BUG and PROBLEM when we want facts, evident and trust". I still think so. If you want a story, you want the model to wander across those connections. If you want a fact, you want it held to the ones you gave it. Most of the trouble people have comes from asking for the second while set up for the first. If what you build for your customers needs facts, build for the second from the first day.

The image that made it click

Ironically, it was an image, in one of my presentations, that made me understand generative AI at this level. The keynote was Deterministic GenAI Output With Provenance, at OWASP Global AppSec EU in Lisbon on 28 June 2024, and slide 70 has the title "The image that caused my 'GenAI Paradigm Shift'". The image is a post by Lee Brimelow. He used Photoshop's Generative Fill and Adobe Firefly to extend Van Gogh's The Starry Night on every side, with the original shown in a frame at the centre. Outside the frame, the swirls, the stars, the hills and the brushwork carry on as if Van Gogh had painted a bigger canvas. The tools for this were new then: outpainting was announced in August 2022, and Generative Fill went into Photoshop in May 2023.

I remember thinking: for that to work, the model has to have learned all those concepts. How Van Gogh paints. The patterns, the structure, the positions, the palette. What we are actually looking at: the landscape, the sky, a character. If there is a mountain here, there will be a mountain there, and the new mountains have to be coherent with every other mountain it has seen. If the sun is there, the light falls a certain way, and so do the shadows. Once you see all the variables it has to hold, extending the image makes sense.

And then I took that to text, and thought: that is exactly what happens with text. Nine slides later, the same talk has the picture I still use: "Input", "Intermediate Representation", "Output", with "GenAI" in the middle. The slide after it says what matters about that black box is not the black box. It is the human verification and the human ownership of the answer. That is where provenance comes in, and Facts, evidence and the record picks it up.

For Dinis the book can only reproduce Lee Brimelow's image with his permission. The alternative the article names is to extend The Starry Night ourselves, with a named tool, and show that. Which one?

What the models found in our language

I think the models have discovered things about our language that we never found ourselves, because we were never able to rationalise them. We do not know what is happening in a child's head as they learn to speak, because they cannot put it into words. We are too close to our own language to see it. With many layers, and as they got bigger, the models found connections and relationships in it. That is my hypothesis, not a finding; the people who study the insides of these models are still working out what the structures they find mean.

We are still discovering what the models can do. In the early days of the chat assistants there were all these stories: it can count, it can do chemistry, it speaks all these languages. A model trained mostly on English turns out to work in a language it saw very little of. How does that work? That is the same sense of wonder this book started with.

Where I was corrected

I asked the agent that is helping me write this book to correct me where I was wrong. It did. The corrections belong here, because they are the kind of thing you should learn to ask for, and because my picture above is a working picture, not the inside of the machine.

  • Classic machine learning learns too. I had said the older AI was a sequence of steps or a formula, and generative AI learns. The rule-based AI was like that; machine learning in general learns from data, and generative AI is one branch of it. The real difference is what comes out. Most machine learning predicts a label or a number (is this spam, what will this cost). A generative model produces new content of the kind it was trained on.
  • Inside the model there are vectors, not a graph. A language model represents words and their contexts as long lists of numbers, and passes them through many layers; its attention mechanism connects every word in the text to every other word, with learned strengths. You can picture that as a graph, and I find the picture useful. But there is no stored graph of nodes and edges inside. My graph is a metaphor.
  • The prompt does not change the model. It conditions what the model computes. The weights stay the same; the prompt steers what it draws on. My "real-time fine-tuning" was also a metaphor.
  • It predicts the next token, then it is trained to be useful. Tokens are pieces of words. The prediction is the start; further training, including on human feedback, is what turns a model into an assistant that follows instructions.
  • Hallucinations have a second cause. A study published by OpenAI researchers in September 2025, Why language models hallucinate, argues that training and evaluation reward guessing over saying "I don't know": a guess is sometimes right, and an honest "I don't know" scores nothing. One explanation, from one model maker; not the only one.
  • Most image models work differently. The common kind are diffusion models: they start from noise and remove it step by step, guided by the text. My intuition that they learned the structure of images holds; but they learned statistical patterns, and they still get light, shadows and hands wrong in ways a person would not.
  • "Emergent abilities" are debated. The term comes from a 2022 paper by Jason Wei and others; a 2023 paper by Rylan Schaeffer and others argues that many of those jumps come from how they were measured. Models working across languages is real and studied; the stories of near-zero data and full fluency are anecdotes.
  • Whether models have a "world model" is an open question. Research shows they form internal representations of things like positions, places and dates; how far that goes is argued about.

Keep both halves. The picture is how I work with these models every day, and it has not let me down. The corrections are what is actually inside. You will need the first to be fast and the second to be honest, and asking for the second is a habit this book will keep coming back to.

A note on the name

Most of what this book says should say generative AI, not AI. The name of the edition says so too. For a while this edition was described as the AI edition; I preferred GenAI, because there is a play in it: Gen Z, Gen AI. Then the readers became two generations, Z and X, and the play got better: Gen ZX, which goes back to my first computer, and which Gen ZX: who this book is for, and what it is for explains. So the title is Generation ZX Developers (GenAI Startup Edition).

After Gen Z comes Gen Alpha. Mark McCrindle, who named them, puts them as born from 2010 to 2024, so the oldest are sixteen in 2026, and after them Gen Beta, from 2025. The boundaries are not official; no body sets them. The two generations in the title are who the book is written for first, not who may read it. If you are sixteen and reading this, read on.

From the register: This book is about generative AI, not AI in general, A language model predicts the next word from a rich picture of how things connect; that is why a better prompt helps, and where hallucinations come from. Answers: Machine Learning and AI.

7Lenses, roles and loops

What generative AI is explained what a language model is. This chapter is about what happens when you use one, from a single prompt to an agent that runs for hours. It is very important to understand this workflow, because once you understand it you see that it is not magic. And that is why you want this level of understanding: so that you can be the one who defines the constraints and how it works. Whether you are leaving university or leaving twenty-five years of a profession to start a company, this is the chapter to understand before you build anything on these tools.

A prompt is a lens

The power of a language model starts with the prompt. The prompt lets you customise the output, which is why a model can speak to so many audiences so easily, and why it can translate. From a model's point of view, translating from one language to another is no different from translating for a particular topic, region, audience, stakeholder or persona. Explain this to me as if I were a teenager. Explain it as if I were a musician. Explain it with medical references. Explain it in football terms.

The model can do that because it has the frame of reference. It holds all those worlds inside it, and the prompt puts the right lens in front of the idea. The analogy is the reason someone understands something.

Take one idea, recursion, and put it through five lenses. For a teenager, a meme of a meme. For a musician, a round, sung over itself. For a doctor, cells dividing into cells. For a football fan, the replay of the replay. In Portuguese, recursividade. Same idea, five ways in, and to the model the fifth is no different from the other four.

It is the same with people who say they are not good at maths. Watch them in their day-to-day and they do a huge amount of maths: rotas, budgets, stock levels. They are just not good at the kind of maths we decided was maths: sums, calculus, trigonometry, algebra. Mathematics is much more than that. It was the lens that was wrong, not the person, and that is why this book keeps showing multiple lenses. One idea, five lenses in the hands-on part lets you try it on an idea of your own.

A role is a model with a description

On the site that made this book there is an editor, a publisher, a journalist, a reviewer, and now a librarian; elsewhere I have had a developer and a designer. Each of those roles is, in essence, almost a raw model plus a role description and a question. That is it.

The role description is the lens: who you are, what you care about, what you refuse, how you work. Every role on the site has a role.md that says exactly that. In the newsroom, in How I work with Claude, I put it this way: an agent is a session with a particular focus, and in practice it is three things: a few documents that explain the project, a role.md for this agent, and a session that reads those before it does anything, named for the role. That is enough to start. The same model sits behind every role; what changes is the description and the question. If your company's first team is a set of agents, this is what each of them is, and Controlling your agent: RiskMandate is about what each of them is allowed to do.

An agent is a loop with a goal

And an agent is that role on a loop, with an objective. Fundamentally the agent is simply a task: do this; then, are we done yet, or do we need to continue? And rinse and repeat. They are not that difficult, which is why the better you understand them, the better your workflows will be.

"An agent is just a loop" is a simplification, and I want to be straight about that. Good agents add planning, checks and limits; the loop is the skeleton, not the whole animal. Made precise: an agent is a model plus instructions, tools, a loop and a rule for stopping. The "personality" is the instructions. "Are we done?" can be the model's own judgement, a test that passes, a budget that runs out, or a person. A tiny agent loop, in the hands-on part, is a page of that, so you can see the skeleton move.

Tools changed everything

In 2018 I told you that as a developer you would soon be asked to write code that integrates with machine learning services: voice, image recognition, domain-specific analysis. The direction was right and the picture was backwards. Today the model asks for the service.

The first chat assistants relied on their training, which is always fundamentally a compression of reality. Imagine having all of Google put inside the model: it was impossible for it to know everything, and what it knew stopped at the day its training ended. Compression is a metaphor, a useful one; what a model knows is learned patterns, not a stored copy. But the limit was real.

What changed is that we gave the models tools, and let them iterate with those tools. This is a very important concept to understand. When you ask a question, the model does not have to answer. It can return a tool call instead: can you make these calls for me? Can you call this weather service? Can you run this web search? Can you read this file? Those are the capabilities we gave that agent, or that session. The program around the model makes the calls, gets the answers, puts it all together, and sends it in the next request. The next request has your original question, plus the tool calls, plus their answers, and the model writes its answer from that, or asks for another tool, and it continues.

Picture it with a made-up question: is it raining in Lisbon? The model answers with a request: call the weather service. The program calls it and gets "light rain". It sends everything back: the question, the call, the result. Now the model answers: take an umbrella. Two requests for one answer, and the second holds everything the first did, plus the tool's result.
