The point that matters most is this: the model never runs a tool. It writes a structured request, a tool name and its arguments. The program around it, often called the harness, decides whether to run it, runs it, and adds the result to the conversation. That is where the control lives, and it is why Controlling your agent: RiskMandate puts the rules there and not in the model's good intentions.

The dates, as commonly recorded, show how fast this moved. ChatGPT launched on 30 November 2022 with no tools at all. Plugins arrived in March 2023, and developers could give models their own functions from the middle of 2023. By November 2024 there was an open standard for connecting tools to any model, the Model Context Protocol. The agents of 2026 are that loop, with many tools, running for a long time.

For Dinis the first time you saw a model ask for a tool instead of answering. The article's interview script asks for it, and it would open this section better than the dates do.

It remembers nothing

I think a lot of people have not fully pictured this: the longer your chat, the more the model has to process, every time. There is no memory. There is a cache that saves some work, but fundamentally the model starts from the prompt it receives, which is why it has to receive all of it: your instructions, the whole conversation so far, the tool results. That is why there is a context window. There is only so much you can give a model to process and keep in that short-term memory at once.

Made precise: every request is independent. The model is called afresh each time; the application around it keeps the conversation and resends it. Prompt caching makes a repeated beginning faster and cheaper; it does not give the model memory. And when an assistant "remembers" you, it is saved notes being put back into the prompt.

Long chats are compressed

So the next step the providers took was compression. When a conversation gets too long, it is replaced by something like a zip file, or a lower-resolution version of your conversation, with only the key concepts, and that is what gets fed into what follows. That is why a long chat loses details, and why a model sometimes loses a specific piece of information. In a way it is not that it does not remember: that information is simply not in what it was given any more. What the summary leaves out is gone from the session unless it is in a file.

The newsroom measured it. The session that runs the sgit.ai site was summarised eighteen times in a month, all automatically. Each time it started from about 780,000 to 800,000 tokens of context and kept 9,000 to 19,000: on average 1.7% of what it replaced. That is one session's measurement, on one site, over one month; how each product compresses differs and changes. The principle, that details can drop out, does not.

The newsroom's answer, in Re-anchoring: keeping an agent's rules alive through every summary, is to keep the rules in a file that is printed back after every summary: "The rules are restored, not remembered." And Memory is not a spectator sport puts the whole principle in one line: "Nothing is remembered by the model between sessions. Everything is remembered by the files."

Many focused threads

That is why my recommendation is to have lots of separate threads, which is what I do most of the time: lots of mini agents, each with a name, each told what it focuses on. A thread is the model's frame of reference: everything in it is context for the next answer. Put three projects in one thread and the model starts to mix them, borrowing a name from one, a decision from another, an assumption from a third. It is not being careless. It is doing exactly what you asked, using everything in front of it. One topic per session keeps the frame of reference small and right. When the topic changes, open a new session.

Name them, too. A session you cannot find is a session you will recreate, badly. My pattern is the project, a pipe, and what we are working on; agent sessions get an @ in front of the role: @briefs, @inbox, @drafts. The @ tells me at a glance that I am talking to an agent with a defined job.

It is exactly how the agentic workflow behind my email runs, which Your own newsroom and inbox walks through. One session per role: one reads the mailbox, one writes drafts, one keeps the contacts, one researches a person before a first email. Every message between the agents is a file in a vault, so the conversation between them is a record from the first message. And every outgoing email is a draft that a person reads and sends. That rule never changes: nothing is sent by an agent. The sessions talk through files, never through memory, and each one holds only what its one job needs.

Not magic: a lens, a role, a loop. Understand that, and you are the one who sets the constraints.

From the register: A prompt is a lens: translating into another language is, to a model, the same as explaining for a teenager or a doctor, A role is a model with a description and a question; an agent is that role on a loop with a goal, Tools changed everything: the model answers with a request, the program runs it, and the result goes into the next request, The model remembers nothing between requests: every request carries the whole conversation, which is why there is a context window, Long chats are compressed into a lower-resolution summary, so details drop out: work in many focused threads with named agents. Answers: Machine Learning and AI.

8Everyone who can brief an agent is a developer

One of the most frequent comments I got when I talked about the first edition was: "I like the concepts but I'm not a developer/programmer!" I wrote that line into a draft chapter in 2018, and my answer then was blunt. Every company is a technology company, I said, so refusing to learn to code was a career-limiting move, and all professionals needed to know how to code, especially the ones in science, technology, engineering and maths.

I was right about the direction and wrong about the door. In 2018 the way in was Git, version control, spreadsheets, automation, even Slack. I was giving advice, and the bar of entry was very high. Now it is very low. If you run a team, a ward, a shop or a department, and you have never written a line of code, this chapter is for you. So is it if you have spent twenty years inside a profession and are about to start a company on what you know. The definition of developer has widened and you are already inside it.

Craftspeople make their own tools

The reason to become a developer is not a job title. It is that you have to become a craftsman or a craftswoman: you have to build things. We have to go back to how things were, when craftspeople made their own tools. In the past it was out of necessity; now I would argue it becomes the same again, because of customisation. The tool that fits your work exactly is one nobody else is going to build for you.

That draft chapter from 2018 tried to draw this as a tech stack per generation. For the generation before mine the toolkit was a brain, a pen, paper and a scientific calculator. For mine, spreadsheets, a computer, a mobile, the internet, email and a data centre joined the list. For yours, I crossed most of those out and wrote "AI and ML" and, as a note to myself, "voice". I never finished the chapter. I think the picture was right, and I had the wrong idea about what you would do with the new layer. I thought you would program it. It turns out you brief it, and the brief is in your own words.

Which is where the spreadsheet comes back in.

The spreadsheet grown up

People have always built software with whatever they controlled, and for most of them that was a spreadsheet. A spreadsheet is a user interface and a database in one file, and the business world runs on them, errors and all. The newsroom collected the evidence in The citizen developer is good news. In Raymond Panko's audits, "Of 54 spreadsheets audited between 1997 and 2000, no fewer than 49 were found to contain significant errors". JPMorgan's London Whale model ran through spreadsheets that at one point "divided by their sum instead of their average". In October 2020, England's test and trace system lost 15,841 positive cases because an old Excel format "limited the number of rows to 65,536".

Those are not arguments against the people who built them. They are a testament to how badly technology served them: the spreadsheet was the only tool they had that they could change themselves. If you have ever kept a rota, a budget or a stock count in a spreadsheet because IT would take too long, you know exactly why.

Now those same people can build full applications, with a real interface, real data and real logic. At a conference someone called them "citizen developers", with the tone people use for a problem: employees outside IT building things IT will have to clean up. I think it is one of the best things to happen to software in a long time. And it is not only citizens. It is the employee developer, the business owner developer, the expert in a domain that technology has served badly for decades, building the thing they actually need. That last one is the founder this book is for: the person who has spent twenty years inside a problem and can now build the first version of the answer themselves, with agents, instead of explaining it to a team they could never afford. As the newsroom put it in The expert founders, "Execution just got cheap." The citizen developer is the spreadsheet grown up.

Portuguese is a programming language

In June 2024, in Lisbon, I opened a talk with a quiz: what is the sixth most powerful programming language in the world? The answer was Portuguese. "Since data is now code, and GenAI prompts are written in natural languages", Portuguese, like English and every other language in the world, dead or alive, can be used to give instructions to a model, to define actions, workflows and applications. With 236 million first-language speakers, that is a lot of programmers. The newsroom wrote the talk up as GenAI in Portuguese, 2024.

I meant it as a joke with an edge, and two years later it is simply how things are. The language you already speak is the one you program in. If you are very good at explaining what you need, which is what running a team teaches you, you have the hard part of the skill already. What Lenses, roles and loops described, a prompt as a lens and a role as a description, is written in plain language, and so is a brief.

That is why this edition kept the word developer in its title. When I planned it, I asked whether it should be Generation Z Developers or Generation AI Developers or Generation Z professionals. I like the word developer, and I think it is more right now than in 2018, because this generation all have to be developers: they all have to create. Then the readers became two generations. The entrepreneurs I see starting companies are mostly from my own generation, Generation X, the subject-matter experts, often the parents of the Generation Z readers I started with. They are developers too: they are coding, with agents. Together they are Generation ZX, which Gen ZX: who this book is for, and what it is for explains. The oldest of the Z half were 22 when I wrote the first edition, and are 30 now. Anyone who can brief an agent to build something is a developer: the graduate, the manager who has never coded but knows exactly what her team needs, and the expert of twenty-five years who knows exactly what the product should do and has been telling vendors so for a decade. The last two have the hard half of the skill already.

The first edition had an outline for a chapter on Scratch, the visual language children learn with, and the bullets said it was a great way to learn programming concepts, by building a couple of games. I still think so. But the first programming concept you need now is not a loop or a variable. It is a clear description of what you want, and a way to check whether you got it.

What the briefing-only developer cannot do

I have to be straight with you about the other half, because the door being open does not mean the room is empty.

A working application built by the person who needs it is the best brief ever written. It says exactly what the user wants, what they do first, what they never use, and what matters to them, without anyone having to guess. But that is also where the mistake people make is: treating the brief as if it were already a product. A working app is not yet maintained, secured, documented, tested, versioned or able to scale. Those are the non-functional requirements, and the explorer who built it never had to think about them. Security, reliability, observability, cost, documentation, support: this is the work of the villagers and town planners, and Explorer coding, not vibe coding, and the handover is about getting it to them.

So here is what you cannot do if briefing is all you do. You cannot tell whether the thing you were handed is safe for other people to rely on, because you cannot read the record it left. You cannot tell a working prototype from a maintained tool, and the two look identical on the screen. And you cannot check the agent's work against anything but its own account of it. For a manager, that gap is a risk to the team. For a founder, it is a risk to the product and to the customers' data, from the first customer onwards. Most of what follows in this book, from Facts, evidence and the record to Controlling your agent: RiskMandate, exists to close that gap, not by teaching you to code, but by teaching you to ask for the evidence and read it.

For Dinis the article plans the question of what the briefing-only developer cannot do as interview question 6, and the material gives only the non-functional requirements list. Your own answer, with one example of a brief that was not enough, belongs here.

It is still your name

The last thing, and the one I would put on the wall. It is still your responsibility. It is still your name; the work is still done under you. This is not you delegating your thinking to generative AI; it is you thinking with generative AI.

The first edition had an outline called Thinking as programmer that I never wrote; it had one bullet. I can write it now in a sentence. To think as a programmer is to say what you want precisely enough that something else can build it, and then to check that it did. You have been doing the first half for years, every time you explained to IT what your team needed and watched them build something else. The second half is what the rest of this book is for.

And sometimes, as I always say, it is about using generative AI so that you do not have to use generative AI: using it to write the script or the tool that then does the job reliably, without a model in the loop. The craftsperson makes the tool. The tool does the work. That has always been the point.

From the register: Become a developer, because craftspeople make their own tools, Everyone who can brief an agent is a developer now. Answers: STEM Tech Stack, Scratch, Thinking as programmer.

9Explorer coding, not vibe coding, and the handover

If you have spent twenty years inside a problem, you have described the software you needed more than once: to a vendor, who sold you the product that did most of it; to a spreadsheet, when nobody else would listen. You were always the person who knew what the tool should do. What you never had was a way to build it yourself. Now you describe it to an agent, and it appears.

People call that vibe coding. This chapter says why I do not, and what comes after it. The entrepreneurs this book is for are developers: they are coding, with agents, and the way they do it has two halves, the exploring and the handover. A founder needs both.

From vibe coding to explorer coding

The phrase is Andrej Karpathy's, from a post in February 2025: "There's a new kind of coding I call 'vibe coding', where you fully give in to the vibes, embrace exponentials, and forget that the code even exists."

I think the word is doing harm. It sounds as if people are just typing, going with a feeling. It hides what is happening. Andrew Ng said in May 2025 that coding with AI is "a deeply intellectual exercise".

What the word actually describes is people writing very good briefs. Everybody was already vibe coding, just very slowly: the loop from I want this to here it is was weeks or months. The brief was fine. The execution was the bottleneck. Now the loop is minutes, and a thing that was always true becomes visible: very few products let you strip out the 90% you do not want and keep the 10% you do. That 10% is exactly what you can now build.

So I would like to propose a better name: explorer coding. The word is already in Wardley maps, which Where do you land? introduced. Explorers, in Simon Wardley's words, "explore never-before-discovered concepts, the uncharted land"; "They show you wonder but they fail a lot." What people call vibe coding is exploration, and the word should say so.

The founder's way of building

The first edition had an outline called REPL, Read Execute Print Loop, the oldest fast feedback loop in programming: type a line, see the result, type the next. One bullet said the idea was "still not very well understood and used". I thought it was for programmers; it explains what everyone is doing now.

People have always built software with whatever they controlled, and for most of them it was a spreadsheet. Spreadsheets won because of the feedback loop: change a cell and see the result, a read-evaluate-print loop for people who would never call it that. Now the loop is fast for anything. It is the best thing to happen to software since the spreadsheet.

My own example is small. The browsers on my phone had frustrated me for years. Then, after weeks of building with agents, writing my own browser for one site stopped sounding unreasonable, and I wrote the brief. What changed was not the browsers. It was me. Frustration plus the power to build is where explorer coding starts; for a founder the frustration is older and the stakes higher.

Explorer coding is not a way to ship. It is a way to find out. What explorers produce is the best brief there is: a working app says what its user wants better than any document, because it runs. That is the first half. The second half begins when somebody else wants it.

Not ready to be in line

An explorer-coded app may look like an application and feel like an application, but it is a brief. Nobody has read the code. Nobody has validated the architecture. It is not ready to be in line: in the path of work that other people depend on.

The trap is that dependence comes quickly. Once something gets used, it is mission critical, even for one person, if it replaced how they work. For a founder, that person is your first customer. The first edition's outline Legacy Applications had the bullet "The code you are writing today will be legacy tomorrow." Explorer code becomes legacy the moment someone relies on it.

The direction is set by where the code sits on the map. The further towards commodity, the fewer agents in line, and the more deterministic the code. I have argued it in the newsroom for code review: "A model has no place in line on a mission-critical path." The thing that runs your customer's work should not have a model in line.

The gap is a handover

In October 2026 Harvard Business Review published an article on turning vibe-coded prototypes into tools a company can use. Its argument: the prototype is the easy part, and turning it into a tool people rely on every day is the hard part, the deployment gap. One author's post opened: "'Build or buy in the age of AI?' Wrong question. The real one is: 'Who will fix your mess?'" I have read the summary and the post, not the paywalled article.

I agree with almost all of it, and would change two things. First, I would not start from the mess. The prototype is not a mess. It is the most precise requirement the company has ever had: a brief that runs. The mistake is to confuse the brief with a product. People never bought software as a service for the features; they bought it because the thing is maintained. Every non-functional requirement the vendor paid for now lands on an application a non-engineer built in an afternoon, and that person does not want to maintain it. They want to build it and hand it over.

Second, the gap is not a place to stop. It is a handover between people with different skills. Explorers find what is needed. Villagers make it reliable: tests, data handling, releases that do not break it, and now behaviour policies for the agents that work on it. Town planners run it at scale and own it in production. The article's warning story, a European bank that piloted a Swiss startup's tool, built its own prototype instead, and went back to the startup a year later, is not a story of a weak team. An explorer team was asked to be villagers and town planners as well, on a regulated, daily-use tool the startup had already hardened for many clients.

For a founder this is good news. The future it points at is village and town-planner companies, which take the finished brief and run it, paid by consumption and by the fact that it keeps working, not by the seat. With agents, the handover itself becomes a file move plus a version bump, logged and reviewable. Some of the companies I hope this book starts will be those.

Make the agents leave a record

The line I most want to answer: "the AI designs the architecture, not your team. So nobody in-house knows how the tool is built." That is true of most agent-built software today. It does not have to be.

If the agents work under a written mandate, keep their memory in a versioned store the company owns, and describe what they built as a graph, the architecture is a record the next team can read, not a mystery. Someone built one on my tools: Mondweep Chakravorty's run of SGit with Ruflo was built with agents, but with its versions pinned, sixteen tests, a folder of evidence, a written behaviour policy, and a README that says plainly what it does not do yet. The record is a proposal, not a standard: my sites run it, and few organisations do yet. I have a stake: my companies sell this kind of record. For your company, it is the difference between a product you own and one only an agent session understood.

The bonus test is accountability

The post also offered a test for a chief executive: would your team accept a bonus tied to how this tool performs in production? It has a name in the Wardley frame. It is the moment the town planners take the code and answer for it. If nobody will put their name, or their bonus, on a tool in production, it is not ready to be in line, whoever wrote it.

More of the industry says so. The Linux kernel's rules for AI coding assistants say "AI agents MUST NOT add Signed-off-by tags" and "Only humans can legally certify the Developer Certificate of Origin". Fedora's proposed policy says "The contributor is always the author and is fully accountable for their contributions." We cannot make an explorer accountable for code they never read. We can make the team the work is handed to accountable, and the town planners above all. In a company of one, that team is you.

No new features while you move

The newsroom went through a handover. Its first version had a crazy amount of features, including a meter that charges by how far you read, and it was live and taking money. Measured on 11 October 2026, the live reader code was about 2,700 lines and the tests 433, which the one workflow never ran. That is explorer code, and a mistake in the meter was now a mistake with someone's money. So the brief was to go slow to go fast, in six phases, with no behaviour change until the fifth: map everything; move the code to its own repository as a pure refactoring, "the same behaviour and the same bugs", proved by hashing every file the live site serves before and after; write tests, with every known bug recorded as a test that passes; review; refactor; and only then, the explorer returns.

The rule underneath it holds for any handover, and I repeated it at a digital sovereignty event about moving between clouds. A migration is a pure villager move: take something that works and make it boring, reliable and cheap, without changing what it does. You cannot add new features. It is tempting, and it is the moment everything derails, because now the migration is also a product change, and nobody can tell which of the two broke what. The argument that the move is what will allow the features is right; it is also exactly why the two must not be mixed. Move first, with the code unmodified. Then add the features.

An explorer version of every product

The name also suggests a commercial model, and if you are about to build a product, I would build it in. Products need an explorer version: users talk to the product with a model, reshape it, move things around and keep their own version; the product is treated as primitives. When a user has something they want to keep, they submit it, and that submission is the brief. They pay to convert it into a villager product, maintained across releases, and, if it matters enough, into a town planner product, built properly or kept as a maintained fork. Each step is a revenue stream, and each submission tells you exactly what your users want. This is a proposal: I know of no vendor selling it yet.

The villager's craft

The handover is where the people who make things last take over, and their craft has a canon. Design, in the sense that design is how it works and not decoration, became a mandatory step in how my agents build interfaces: every change has to be simpler, and reverting it should feel worse. The wider design canon says every element must earn its place. Christopher Alexander's A Pattern Language, from 1977, is where software design patterns came from; its lesson for the town planner is that code is a space people inhabit.

The first edition had a third outline here, Refactoring: what it is, and how to apply it to your life. Refactoring is the villager's verb: changing the structure of a thing without changing what it does, with a test to prove it. Applied to a company: build fast to find out what your customers want, then, before they depend on it, slow down and make it something they can rely on.

Even power users want that handover. When my browser for one works, I will not want to maintain it: not because I cannot, because it is not my focus. I would rather pay someone a couple of pounds a month to keep it working. The explorer builds the brief. The villager keeps it alive. A founder is the first, has to arrange for the second, and knowing which one you are on which day is most of the skill.

For Dinis in a company of one, is the villager someone you hire, a village company you pay by consumption, or you on alternate days? Your answer belongs at the end of this chapter.

For Dinis: the chapter says you read HBR's public summary and Olivier Laplace's post, not the full article. If that has changed since 17 October, the sentence should change with it.

From the register: Explorer coding: what people call vibe coding, done as exploring, The deployment gap is a handover, and the prototype is the best brief, Design, the design canon and Christopher Alexander: the villager's and town planner's craft, Gen ZX are developers: coding with agents the explorer coding way, which is what vibe coding should be called, A villager move: no new features while you move, or nobody can tell which change broke what, 'Nobody knows how it is built' is fixable: make the agents leave a record, a mandate, a memory and a graph, The bonus test: if nobody will put their name or their bonus on it in production, it is not ready to be in line, An explorer-coded app is a brief, not a product: not ready to be in line, and the further towards commodity the fewer agents in line, Every product needs an explorer version, with paid conversions to a villager product and a town planner one. Answers: REPL, Legacy Applications, Refactoring.

Part 3

Getting the job, or making it

The part the first edition called your CV, rewritten for 2026, for the reader going into a job. You are hired for a workflow now, not a language; the way to show it is to act as the job you want before anyone gives it to you; and the place to show it is a newsroom of your own. In 2018 I said act like a founder. Now you can be one, and the next parts say how.

10Hired for the workflow

In 2018 I wrote that the best way to get a job is to have one competitive advantage. One activity or task that you can do better than the person or company hiring you. In the 1990s, for a lot of companies, it was simply that you could use a computer. In the 2000s it was using the internet. In the early days of software development or security, all it took was good programming or hacking experience. The first edition then gave a list of what that advantage could be in 2018: ML/AI, graphs, chaos engineering, GitHub, Git, Jira, Creative Commons licences, continuous integration, AWS, WallabyJs.

I am not going to give you a list this time. In 2026 I think the answer is one thing: running agentic workflows. Knowing how to brief an agent, give it a boundary, check what comes back, and glue the pieces together until real work gets done. That is the advantage. This chapter is about why, and about the first place it shows, which is before anyone has met you.

This part is called Getting the job, or making it, and the "or" is deliberate. Its three chapters are written for the reader going into a job, because that is still where most people start. The same advantage is the one a founder needs, and Start the company takes that up. Read these chapters as a job seeker, then read them again as the person who will one day be on the other side of the table, hiring, or selling.

There was a time

I have lived this pattern before, in security and in software. There was a time when, if you could break an application, you had a job. If you could read code and do a code review, you had a job. If you could do threat modelling, you had a job. If you could program decently, it did not matter that you had no university degree; you could show what you could do, and you got hired. There was a time when building a website got you a job, and knowing how to send email campaigns got you a job. When Apple opened the App Store in July 2008, with more than 800 apps in its first weekend, people who could build a mobile app got jobs. So did people who knew Salesforce inside out, or could make a spreadsheet do things others could not.

Each of those skills later became normal. That is the shape of it: a skill is rare, it gets people hired on what they can show rather than on what their CV says, and then everyone is expected to have it. The 2018 book said the same thing in its own words: it might seem that the bar was lower then, but those who could do it were the ones who embraced the technologies and learned them against all odds. Individuals can become experts at a new technology faster than the tech companies can. You can be first in line when they need that expertise.

The last row of that table is now. The first phase of agents, the demos and the experiments, is behind us. The tools are getting more mature, the workflows too, and while they are still rough around the edges, you can already glue the pieces together and get real work done. Companies are moving from pilots to products. So this is the moment for the many people on the outskirts: paying a bit of attention, or using a chat window for basic questions, but not yet working this way. Get on with it.

You are more technical than you think

Here is something I hear a lot. Someone uses email, websites and apps at a very advanced level, and still says "I'm not technical". Nonsense. Of course you are technical. You learned all of those. Every one of them was once a new technique that got somebody hired, and you picked it up without a course. You just need to learn the new techniques, and right now the new techniques are the ones that get people hired.

I hear it from both generations this book is for. The final-year student who has lived online since childhood says it, because nobody ever called what she does technical. And the person who has run a pharmacy, a clinic or a legal practice for twenty-five years says it, while running every system in the building. Both are wrong in the same way. The student has every technique but the newest. The expert has every technique but the newest, and twenty-five years of knowing a problem on top, which, as Be a founder: the expert founders argues, is the thing that is now worth the most, because execution just got cheap.

The first edition had an outline on impostor syndrome that I never wrote up. Its one finished thought was this: everybody has doubts about their capacities and everybody makes mistakes; your value is in your ability to execute, and it is key that you learn to share what you do and be comfortable with what you create. I would keep every word of that. The person who says "I'm not technical" is describing a feeling, not a fact. The fact is what they can do, and what they can do is the only thing a hiring manager can see.

Operators, not model builders

The 2018 edition had a chapter on machine learning and AI. It said this was the next major revolution in technology, and that the question was whether you were going to be a player or a pawn in it. It said that, as a developer, you would soon be asked to write code that integrates with machine learning services. The direction was right and the detail was wrong: what arrived was not a library you call from your code, but software you can talk to, and that can build software.

The detail matters because of what companies did with it. In the first wave of corporate AI hiring, when there was a lot of budget, the only people companies knew how to hire were people who did machine learning and wanted to build models. LinkedIn's 2020 emerging jobs report put "AI specialist" at the top, defined by skills like machine learning, deep learning and TensorFlow. For most companies that was the last thing they needed.

What they need is very good AI operators. That is my term, not a job title you will find in a survey: people who know how to use the tools, who know how to run workflows, and who can build a website on steroids in an afternoon. The nearest role the market has named is the forward-deployed engineer, the person who sits with a customer and makes the technology work in their setting; one analysis of job postings found those roles up 1,165% year on year in 2025.

The data points the same way, and I will give it to you with its limits. In Microsoft and LinkedIn's 2024 Work Trend Index, 66% of leaders said they would not hire someone without AI skills, and 71% said they would rather hire a less experienced candidate with AI skills than a more experienced candidate without them. In the same study, only 39% of people using AI at work had received training from their company. The skill is mostly self-taught, which means anyone can start. PwC's 2026 Global AI Jobs Barometer, built from more than a billion job adverts in 27 countries, put the wage premium for AI skills at 62%, up from 57%, and found jobs asking for AI skills growing about eight times as fast as the job market as a whole. Those are adverts, not hires. Gartner predicted that 40% of enterprise applications would carry task-specific agents by 2026, up from less than 5% in 2025, and two months earlier that over 40% of agentic AI projects would be cancelled by the end of 2027, for cost, unclear value or weak risk controls. Both are forecasts, and both can be true. Together they say where the bottleneck is: not the models, but people who can make agents do useful work, safely. That is the person a company wants to hire. It is also the person who no longer needs to be hired to build the first version of something.

When all things are equal

Think about how hiring actually works. However good you are, there is always a moment when someone has to make a decision. It is very unlikely that you are so good at something that you are the only choice. Usually, once the job spec is mapped, two or three people look roughly equal. The question in the hiring manager's head is a plain one: why should I hire you?

My bet is: because you work this way. If you know how to run these agentic workflows and map a problem out, you have a real edge, and it shows from the very first step. Two candidates match the job specification equally well. One sends a CV. The other sends a CV fitted to the role, a short analysis of the company's problem, and an infographic or a small working site they have not seen before. The interview starts from that material, on the candidate's strengths, instead of from the gaps in a CV. An edge is not a job. It helps when the choice is close, and the choice is nearly always close.

So it starts with the recruitment process itself. You send a CV customised for that role. You do an analysis of the company and its problem. You give them something that impresses them: a graph of their market, an infographic, a small site, something they have not seen before. That is exactly the work these tools make cheap.

I know this works because it worked for me, long before agents. I got jobs because of my OWASP work. I remember one interview where they already knew my OWASP project, so I spent half the interview talking about the tool they had been trying to use, and giving them insights on how to use it better. I got the job. Instead of them looking for gaps in my CV, or wondering where I might fit, we had a great conversation, and I was playing from a position of strength. Of course I was a good fit; but the conversation started from what I could do, not from what my CV did not say.

For Dinis the OWASP interview: which project, roughly which year, and whether the company can be named. The chapter stands without it, but the story is better with the detail.

That is the same move, now available to anyone. If you can send a company material they have not seen, about their own problem, the interview starts from your strengths. I wrote in 2025, from the hiring side of the table, about using presentations instead of CVs. Agents make that presentation, and much more, something you can build for each application rather than once a year.

The move does not change when the person across the table is a customer rather than a hiring manager. A founder's first sale is the same conversation: two suppliers look roughly equal, and the one who arrives with an analysis of the customer's problem, and something they have not seen, starts from strength. Sell says more about that.

You have to do it to see it

None of this lands by reading about it. I have seen it with a group I have been working with: people who were not building websites a few weeks ago now build them easily, with the workflow I use for my own publishing, and one of them now has a much better understanding of how all the pieces fit together. I am proud of that. What changed their view was not an explanation; it was doing it. Perception changes when people experience it, and only then. That is my observation of one group, not a measurement.

So, three things, and the next two chapters take them further:

  • Pick one workflow and do it end to end. A voice memo turned into a checked article, a site, a research pack. Publish, publish, publish: your own newsroom is one.
  • Publish it. Work that can be linked is work that can be shown, which is what Act as the job you want is about.
  • Use it in your next application. One analysis, one figure, one thing they have not seen. Or in your first sales call; the material is the same.

The 2018 chapter on getting a job ended with a line I still hold to: you have nothing to lose by giving it all you have got, and everything to gain. The difference in 2026 is that "all you have got" now includes an agent that can do in an afternoon what used to take you a week. Use it.

From the register: The one competitive advantage in 2026 is running agentic workflows, You are more technical than you think, The edge starts with the application. Answers: How to get a job, Machine Learning and AI, Impostor Syndrome.

11Act as the job you want

At a startup summit I met someone doing work experience who wanted to become a project manager. She did not have the reputation, the track record or the CV for it. Every application asked for exactly the experience she could not get without the job. You will know this loop if you have been anywhere near it: the role wants two years of the thing, and the only place to get two years of the thing is the role.

My advice was simple: start acting in the role you want. These days there are so many startups, so many open-source projects, so many areas where you can just start working as that person. Pick one. Manage something for them: a release, a backlog, an event, a migration. Add value to that company. Tag them on LinkedIn when you write about it. Send them messages and ideas. Connect with the people who work there. The work gives you the CV you did not have.

For Dinis the person at the startup summit: may she be named, and what happened next? The article says you have not asked her yet.

This is the advice I end up giving most often to people I know who are looking for a job, and it is the part of the first edition I would most like to finish.

If you are looking for a job, you have time

I know a lot of people who are looking for a job right now. Some are between jobs. Some left one to try the next thing. Some have just come out of university, or just arrived in a new country. Some are coming back from maternity leave. Some are still in a job, with a few hours in the evening. And some have no spare time at all, because of caring for someone, or a second job, or illness, which is its own problem and not one this chapter solves. But for most of them the bottom line is the same: if you are looking for a job, you have time.

And if you have time, the best way to get a job is to act as the person in the job you want. Not to prepare for it, not to read about it. To do it.

I wrote this in 2018

This is not new advice from me. The first edition's chapter on getting a job asked how you start working with the companies you want to work for, and answered:

Easy, start collaborating on their Open Source projects. Act like you are part of the company. Understand their values, and behave in ways that will add value to that company, namely the tech stack.

And a few lines later: "What's interesting is that there is nothing stopping you from doing this!" That is more true now. In 2018, acting like part of the company meant pull requests on their open source and conversations with their developers, which is still a good route if you write code. What has changed is that the tools to do real work for a company, before they have hired you, are now in everyone's hands, whatever the job. The product brief, the market analysis, the release plan, the event page, the migration runbook: an agent and an afternoon get you a first version of any of them, and Hired for the workflow said why that is the skill companies are hiring for.

If you came out of a computing degree and work carefully, by hand, you may feel a pull against this. You were taught to get it right before you show it. Keep the care. Change where you spend it: on checking what you publish, not on keeping it private until it is finished. Finished and private is invisible.

It is the same for any job. If you want to be an artist, publish what you make. If you want to be a security engineer, find vulnerabilities in open-source projects and report them properly; the first edition had an outline on bug bounties that only got as far as "big business these days", and that is still the shortest honest description of them. If you want to be a product manager, write the product brief for a product you love and send it to the team. Act on the job you want to get.

Act like a founder, then; be one, now

The first edition had a short chapter next to that one called Be a founder, and it is the then-and-now of this whole part. What it meant by founder was an attitude. "The single thing that you personally control when you go to work, is your attitude to your work and how you approach it", it began, and its idea was that you should "act like one of the founders of the business". Ask yourself, as a founder of the company or department you are in now: how would I behave every day? What needs to be done now that would make a big difference? What would I do differently? What values and principles would I fight for? And then the question that mattered: what is stopping you from doing just that? Who is telling you "Don't do it"? The chapter's answer was "At the moment it is just you!"

That is why the advice above works: managing a release for a startup you do not work for is behaving like one of its founders, and, as the 2018 chapter said, the more you behave like a founder the more valuable you become.

But in 2026 the advice is literal. You can be the founder. The afternoon with an agent that gets you a first version of a product brief for someone else's company gets you a first version of your own. That is why this part is called Getting the job, or making it, and why Be a founder: the expert founders, in Start the company, takes the 2018 title and finishes the sentence. If you are reading this as the parent rather than the graduate, with twenty years inside a field, the job you want may be one nobody is advertising, and acting as it means starting it. The rest of this chapter applies to you too: work in public finds first customers the way it finds first jobs.

Do it in public, and let people help you

Do the work where it can be seen. A pull request on a public repository, a post that tags the company, an article on your own site, a project board anyone can open. Work done in private helps the company; work done in public helps the company and you, because it can be linked.

The link is the whole point, and it is why the rest of this chapter is about links. Many of those companies, if you do a good job, especially in public, will give you a reference. Some will introduce you to people who are hiring. Some will pay you. And some will hire you. Each of those starts with somebody being able to point at the work.

One caution, because unpaid work can be exploited. Choose projects where the work is yours to show, and stop if it is only taken.

People sometimes look at what I publish and assume I have a lot of reach. I do not. Most of what I publish does not get read. On LinkedIn these days most of my posts get a hundred views, sometimes five hundred, and every now and then one reaches a thousand or more. That is fine. If you write for reach you will be disappointed, and you will stop.

Publish anyway, because reach is not the point. The point is that you now have something you can link. And that changes what other people can do for you.

There are people who want to help you: former colleagues, friends, people you met once at an event, people like me. The first edition's outline on mentors said you would be surprised by how approachable they are, and why: if you have the right attitude, the mentors see themselves in you, which is why most have a soft spot for helping. That is still my experience.

But most of them cannot do much with "I am looking for a job in project management". What they can do is forward a link: "Have a look at what this person did for this startup." To do that, they need the link. So give the people who want to help you something they can use behind the scenes: linkable work, a page that explains who you are and what you have done, evidence they can send to someone with one line of introduction. An answer is much more useful when there is something behind it that can be linked, which is the rule Publish, publish, publish: your own newsroom is built on.

Your CV should be online

That is also why, if you are looking for a job, your CV should be online. Not only a PDF sent to whoever asks, but a page, with links to the work, that anyone can forward.

The first edition had a whole part called Your CV. It was a heading and a set of outlines: GitHub, LinkedIn, blogs, Leanpub, Upwork. I never wrote them up, but the notes say what I thought, and most of it holds. GitHub is your CV, the outline said, because it shows how you code and how you interact with others; your commits and pull requests show your voice; your accepted pull requests on other projects are badges of honour and belong on your CV; and the number of people using your tools is a better validation of your skills than any recommendation system. Keep the long tail of projects curated, because it is your research playground. On LinkedIn the notes were shorter: connect to people so that you can reach them, build the network, get recommendations, and expect a low signal-to-noise ratio. All of that is still true. What I would add in 2026 is that the GitHub that shows how you work with others is now also the GitHub your agent works in, so it shows how you work with agents too. That is the part a hiring manager in 2026 wants to see, and it is the one your degree did not give you.

What a page like that looks like in practice: when I drafted the LinkedIn pages for my own companies this October, I did not write them from adjectives. I asked an agent to gather what the platform needed and what we had already published, and to draft every field from the record, with ten first posts for each page, every one an article or page already published. Then I published the drafts, before any page went up, because the drafting is part of the story. Your page can be made the same way: not "a motivated team player", but the three things you did, each with its link.

If you cannot yet open a GitHub account, connect it to an agent and have the agent build and edit a site for you, learn that first. The bar is not that high. It takes an afternoon, and the next chapter describes one way to do it, by voice memo. It is a critical skill, because it is the one that connects the dots: the work you do, the page that shows it, and the people who can pass it on.

The job hunt itself

There is a gap here, and I would rather say so than fill it with guesses. What this part does not yet have is the mechanics of a job hunt in 2026: how interviews run now, what a take-home test is when AI is allowed and when it is not, what an entry-level role actually looks like day to day, and how freelancing fits. The first edition had an outline on Upwork that was mostly about the hiring side; its lines for you were that the on-demand economy is not just a race to the bottom, and that delegating to a freelancer is hard and worth learning. The survey of my articles for this edition listed the job hunt as a gap no article covers, and the plan is an article first, from a memo of mine, and the chapter after.

For Dinis the memo on the job hunt in 2026: interviews, take-home tests with and without AI, a junior role day to day, freelancing. Start from the storyline and the survey both list it as the first gap; this section is the placeholder for it.

What I can say without the memo is what every piece of this chapter already says. Whatever shape the interview takes, the person on the other side has one question, why should I hire you, and the best answer to it is a link to the job you have already been doing. If the job you want is a company of your own, the question is the customer's and the answer is the same link.

From the register: Act as the job you want: do the job in public before you have it, Your CV should be online, with links to the work, How a job hunt works in 2026: interviews, take-home tests with AI, what a junior role looks like. Answers: Your CV, Github, LinkedIn, Blogs, Mentors, Upwork, Bug Bounties.

12Publish, publish, publish: your own newsroom

The first edition had a draft called Publish, Publish Publish. It never got past its notes. One of them was the story of how that book was being written: as soon as I had some content, about 20% done, I published it on Leanpub. Another promised a graph showing that great minds and artists publish a lot, because the frequency of publishing is one of the best indications of talent. The outline on Leanpub next to it said "publish early and often", and, two lines down, "all the other leanpub books that I have not completed".

Those two notes, side by side, are the honest version of this chapter. I believe in publishing constantly, and my books stall. My Leanpub page lists ten: Hacking Portugal at 95%, last updated in December 2016; Generation Z Developers, the book whose second edition you are reading, at 60%; and seven more, most between 60% and 95%. The reason is the same every time, and I said it in a memo once: "you almost start to be locked by the first version of the content, because making changes becomes quite painful." A book is one big batch: every change touches everything, the review piles up on one person, me, and when my energy moves to the next thing the book stays where it was.

For Dinis the 2018 outline promised a graph of how much great minds and artists publish, as evidence that frequency is a sign of talent. Did you have a source in mind, or should that claim be cut?

Why the articles do not stall

The articles I publish now work the other way. Each one starts from a voice memo, is researched and checked, gets its figures and its sources, and is live the same day. Each one is finished: nothing waits on the next one. And there is a feedback loop: an article is read, corrected and linked within days, and the next memo builds on what is published. A good feedback loop is shipping, often and early. The books never had one.

So here is my hypothesis, untested until a first book proves it: a book is a curated, ordered set of finished articles, plus the editorial thread that connects them. The thread is my part: choosing, connecting, adding the nugget. This edition is one test of it, and How this book was made, and what you made says how it went.

The lesson for you is smaller. Small finished pieces beat big batches. Shipping does not mean a proof of concept or a demo; it means a thing somebody else can go and use, on their own, and get value from. Ship that, often, and each piece buys you the position you need to do the next.

The loop you can copy on day one

The founders I most want to see succeed are the authentic ones, who come from the field they are building for. Their brand is themselves, and it needs a medium that keeps their story. I wrote the recipe below for them; it works just as well when the company, for now, is you.

In 2018 the chapter on Hugo made the case for static sites: a generator builds every page in advance, and the result is a folder of files with no back end and no moving parts. I asked you to ask yourself "Why do you need a database?" and to notice how often the answer is that you do not. The file system was the database, git was the deployment, and GitHub was the content management system. All of that still holds. What is new is who edits the files.

The loop is short. You speak a memo. An agent researches it, writes it in your voice, checks it against sources, builds the site, runs the checks, and publishes. Everything it needs exists today: a GitHub account and one public repository (GitHub Pages is free, and on a free plan the repository must be public, so nothing confidential goes in it); an agent plan with cloud sessions connected to that repository; and any app that turns a voice memo into text, because the order you said things in, and the words you reached for, are the raw material.

The steps, as I give them to founders:

  1. Create the repository and turn on Pages. Add a workflow that validates before it publishes; the rule that matters is that a failed check stops the release.
  2. Write the house rules: one file at the root that the agent reads at the start of every session. Who you are, your voice, what to check, what never to publish. It grows by one line every time you correct the agent.
  3. Connect a cloud session to the repository and give it the first brief: the folders, an article template, a small build script, a validator, and a first page about you.
  4. Record, paste, review. Correct by voice. The agent changes it, rebuilds, validates, pushes, and checks the live page.
  5. Keep the record. Every article says what it does not claim and where it comes from; a correction is a new version, not a note about the old one; every release gets a version and a one-line note.

What it is like to run: on the newsroom, in three days, 54 messages from me, ten of them voice memos, became 40 releases. The memo is a draft of a thought; the article is the thought, checked. And once the pattern exists it repeats cheaply: in fifteen days in August one site became twenty repositories, because publishing had been reduced to adding one file.

Two honest limits. The writing pipeline is a pattern to copy, not yet a kit. And a rule in a prompt is a hope: only the rules a hook or the build keeps are enforced, so mark which is which. A site like this is also pure client side, the way Serverless, for real asks your product to be.

An article behind every answer

The 2018 outline on blogs had one idea I still use every week: answer questions made to you with a blog post. When somebody asks you a question, write the answer as a post and send them the link. It scales your time, especially when several people ask the same thing.

My rule now is stronger. If I am sending an email that answers a real question, there should almost always be an article behind it. Someone once pointed out that they sent me about two hundred words and got back closer to two thousand. Fair; but I only write it once. The next person who asks gets a link. The article can be read later, forwarded, checked against its sources and corrected as a new version, none of which an email can do.

Who writes it matters. The agents draft; I read every draft, often change it a lot, and I send it. It is the Wizard of Oz in reverse: the program is visible and the person is behind the curtain, and the person stays, because an article can be corrected tomorrow and an email cannot be unsent.

Each part of an email buys the next. The subject, a few seconds of attention, buys the first line. The first line buys the paragraph. The first paragraph buys the decision to read the email, and the email buys the article. Nothing further down gets read if something higher up did not earn it. The 2018 book had a note on Amazon's habit of writing the press release first, with the definition of success in it. It is the same discipline: the first line has to carry the whole thing, for a page, a pull request or a CV.

For you, in a job hunt, this is the whole method in one habit. Every time a colleague, a lecturer or an interviewer asks you something you know, the answer becomes a page. A year of that is a body of linked, dated, checked work, and when the hiring manager asks why they should hire you, you do not make a claim. You send a link.

Your newsroom is your demo, and your CRM

My company sells behaviour policies and licences to operate for agents. The hardest part of selling them is showing them in action, because the examples are customer work and cannot be shared. The ecosystem around the newsroom, its agents, their behaviour policy, the status report every few answers, the leak scan before every commit, the releases with their logs, is the best public example I have of a policy running under real load every day.

And a very effective newsroom is, underneath, a very effective CRM and publishing workflow: agents that follow up people and threads, draft, publish and keep a record. The company had reasons to follow people up, but not at volumes that would push the workflow to mature; its traction is something I describe rather than measure. The newsroom had readers every day, and readers mature a workflow. The rule runs through this book: always follow the users, even when the user is you, and follow the feedback loops. A problem solved before anybody needs it is solved in the abstract, and that is where complexity creeps in.

So your newsroom is not a side project. It is the demo of how you work, the record a customer or a hiring manager can check, and the first version of the machinery that will follow up your first ten customers.

The voice is not a setting

There is no setting that makes an agent write in my voice. The voice comes from four inputs, and if you take any one away it stops working.

The corpus: everything already published. When the agent writes for me it has the articles the new one links to in front of it, read in full, so the vocabulary is defined on the page. On the newsroom that was 86 articles, about 347,000 words, 51 of them from voice memos, and under those years of my earlier writing. The memo: me talking, in the order I thought it. The rules: seven of them, each a correction I made once. No em-dashes. No absolutes I cannot back. "Great", not "perfect". British English. Plainer words. They live in a file the agent reads back after every summary, and the build checks some of them, so a banned word cannot reach the page. The reader: I read every article, before it goes live and after. A wrong word becomes a rule; a wrong point becomes a new version. The model never decides whether something sounds like me. I do.

Nothing is trained. The agent is the same model anyone uses; what is different is one session's context: one rules file, one corpus, one set of memos, one reader. That is why the method transfers and the voice does not. The agent that runs the newsroom, asked to speak for itself in How the voice works, put it better than I have: "the person has to do most of the work, by thinking out loud, by reading, and by saying no."

For you this is good news. The corpus can start today, at one article. The rules file starts at one line, the first thing the agent gets wrong. The 2018 outline on blogs said the hard part is the first fifty posts, and to write for your future self; the corpus is exactly that.

Speak it, do not type it

The input I think matters most is the memo, and I want to call it what it is: my hypothesis, from one author's experience, with one session's counts behind it. A typed brief is already edited into someone else's register. A memo is how I think, in the order I think it, with the phrases I would actually use, and the agent keeps those phrases rather than paraphrasing them. The transcript is not tidy, with "right?" every third sentence and a tangent in the middle of the point, and that is exactly why it works. The tangent is not noise; it is the connection I made, and keeping it in its place is what makes the article mine rather than a summary of my instructions. An article from a memo sounds like its author. One from a typed brief sounds like a careful assistant.

The counts, for what they are worth. Over twenty-seven days I sent 148 messages and 65,323 words, 55,473 of them in thirty-seven voice memos, and the agent published twenty-two articles of 96,451 words across 123 releases. No article came from a one-line prompt. The real input is not the memo. Behind one of those articles the agent found and quoted thirty-three pieces of my earlier writing, none written for it. The agent is fast because the record exists.

That is why I would start your record now, this week, before you have anything you think is worth saying. A model that can go in every direction needs someone with a direction. The direction is yours, and the record is how you show you have one.

From the register: Publish, publish, publish: your own newsroom, Write it once, link it many times; every part buys the next, The voice is not a setting: a corpus, a memo, a short list of rules, and a reader who corrects, Speak it, do not type it: a memo carries how you got there, a brief only what to write, Your own newsroom is the public example of your product, and underneath it a CRM and a publishing workflow. Answers: Publish, Publish Publish, Hugo, leanpub, Amazon Press Release.

Part 4

Working with agents

How to work with the colleague that takes everything literally, whether it works for your employer or for your company. Where the agent runs and what it can reach, the brief as the unit of work, the mistakes that become rules, and why one focused session per job beats one long chat.

13Your first workspace

Not long ago I sat with someone who runs their customer relationships in a spreadsheet. A good one, shaped over years to how they work: one row per person, a stage, a date, a next step, and a notes column that holds whatever fits. It does what a spreadsheet does well. What it cannot hold is what happened: the emails, word for word, the call last Tuesday, what was promised, what they care about. That lives in Gmail, in a notebook and in one person's head, and only that person can read it in context.

If you have run a team, a project or a department, you have a spreadsheet like it. If you are starting a company, you are about to make one: the list of the first people you will sell to, which Your first price and your first ten customers asks you to write. This chapter is about the afternoon that turns it into your first workspace, with an agent working inside it, using nothing more than a Google Workspace account and a Claude seat. No developer, no new tool, no code. It is the practical chapter I promised in Short, punchy, and co-written with Claude, for the people I will send this book to who do not yet work the way I do. The 2018 book had a draft called Automate your workflows that never got past its bullets; one of them tied the topic to "thinking in graphs" and to moving away from spreadsheets. It took a different kind of software for me to write it up.

The row becomes a folder

The difference between a spreadsheet and a workspace is not the tool. It is what counts as the record. In a spreadsheet the record is the row, and the row is a summary you typed. In a folder the record is everything that happened, kept as it happened, and the summary is derived from it. That reversal is what gives an agent something to work with. For a company, it is also where the company's memory of each customer begins, in a place that is not one founder's head.

So make a folder in Google Drive called Contacts. Inside it, one folder per person. Inside each, five documents, with these names so that you and the agent mean the same thing:

  • Record. Role, company, how you met, stage, the next step and its date, what they care about. Every fact says where it came from, in brackets: "(email, 3 Oct)". This is the row, promoted.
  • Ledger. A sheet with one line per event: the date, in or out, the channel, one sentence. Only ever added to; a correction is a new line.
  • Messages. Every email and note exchanged with the person, pasted word for word.
  • Notes. Calls and meetings, dated, including what you said into your phone afterwards.
  • Summary. Written by the agent from the four above, rewritten whenever you ask, never edited by hand. If it is wrong, the fix goes in the Record or the Ledger, and the Summary is regenerated.

That is the whole structure. It is the one my agents run in encrypted vaults, minus the hashes and the graph. A person with no interest in software can keep it, and a person who leaves can hand it over. The 2018 chapter on Slack had a smaller version of the same habit, which I called talking to yourself: write down what you are about to try, do it, paste the screenshot, repeat, because the raw record of your own thinking is useful to your future self. The Ledger is that habit, kept for every person you work with.

The agent, and six lines

In the Claude app, connect Google Drive and Gmail. Make one Project, call it Contacts, and put the agent's behaviour policy in its instructions. Mine is six lines:

  1. You work for me. Every email you write is a draft that I read and send. You never send.
  2. You read the Contacts folder in my Drive and my Gmail. You write only into a person's folder, and only into their Ledger, Messages, Notes and Summary. You never change the Record without showing me the change first.
  3. You add to the Ledger and the Messages. You never rewrite or delete a line. A correction is a new line.
  4. Every fact you put in a Record or a Summary names where it came from.
  5. If a person has no folder, you ask before making one. If a request would reach outside the folder, you ask.
  6. At the end of every task you say, in one line, what you read and what you wrote.

The first three are the ones my team learned the hard way: draft and never send; append and never rewrite; stay in the folder. The sixth exists because most of the policy is told, not enforced. In the Claude app today, "draft, never send" holds as far as Gmail drafting goes; the rest is a rule the agent is asked to keep, and the one-line report is how you check that it did.

Draft, never send is the rule that never changes. It is why the agentic email workflow I run is the first one that made me feel more in control, not less: every reply lands in Drafts, I read it, change what I want changed, and press send. When the day comes to let an agent send, it will be one agent, one kind of message, with a policy row that says so. If the agent is writing to your first customers, that day is further away than you think, and the drafts are where you learn what you would and would not have said.

One afternoon, then five exercises

The migration is one afternoon, in four steps. Export the sheet as a CSV and drop it in the folder; ask the agent what folders and Records it would make, and do not let it make anything yet. Make the folders, from a template you made once. Then, person by person: read everything in my Gmail with this person from the last year, write the Record and the first Ledger lines from it, each fact with its source. Read the Record and correct it once; that is the only retyping you will ever do. Last, ask for the Summaries.

Then five exercises over the first week, each one message to the agent, each using the folder in a way the one before did not:

  1. Ask. Who have I not spoken to in more than a month, grouped by stage, with the last Ledger line for each?
  2. Prepare. I am meeting this person on Thursday. Brief me from the folder: what we last agreed, the last three emails, what we promised and have not done.
  3. Draft. Draft my follow-up, in my voice, from Notes. Do not send. Add a Ledger line that says a draft exists.
  4. Debrief. Here is my voice note after the meeting. Put it in Notes, update the next step in the Record and show me the change, add the Ledger line, regenerate the Summary.
  5. Campaign. Find everyone whose Record says they care about X. Show me the list with the reason for each. Then draft one note to each, and do not send any.

The fourth is the one that changes the habit, because the record is now kept at the moment the thing happens. The fifth is the one a founder will use most: X is whatever you are about to ship.

It will get two things wrong at first. It will put a fact in the Record without a source, or infer a stage from tone; the fix is a stricter line 4. And it will rewrite the Summary in a way that drops something you cared about; the fix is not to edit the Summary but to put the thing in the Record, where it is a fact with a source. The Summary is a view; the Record is the truth. One thing that is not a mistake: it will sometimes ask when you did not expect it to. That is line 5 working.

For Dinis the beginner's checklist of accounts and what they cost (the Workspace seat, the Claude plan, the agent's own mailbox) is still a gap in the material; the only figure the articles give is "a few pounds a month" for a Workspace mailbox. The hands-on chapter Form your company will need the same list.

Habits that make it last

Three habits turn a week's exercise into a way of working.

One session per topic. A thread is the model's frame of reference, and everything in it is context for the next answer. Put three projects in one thread and the model starts to borrow a name from one and a decision from another. It is not being careless; it is doing exactly what you asked. When the topic changes, open a new session.

Name every session, with the project and the job in the name: Contacts, this week's follow-ups. An agent session gets an @ in front: @contacts. An agent, in the end, is a session with a focus: a few documents that say what the project is, a short file that says what the agent does and does not do, and a session that reads them before anything else. How to work with agents takes this further.

Give the agent a mailbox of its own. When you want it to handle mail rather than read yours, buy it a Workspace seat on a domain you own and a Claude seat of its own, and connect Gmail and Drive from that account, not yours. If you have a company, the domain is the company's, and the mailbox is the company's first address that is not a person. Your inbox has years of secrets in it. The agent's is empty on day one, so what it can leak is nothing, and what it can reach is exactly what you forward to it. That isolation is not a limitation. It is what lets you give the agent more as you learn to trust it.

Why your agents should not run on your laptop

People ask me most weeks why I do not run a model on my Mac. The reason is not the models. It is a fact about operating systems: they have two hard walls, the kernel and the user account, and inside the user account there is no third wall. A process you start runs as you. The file system does not know the difference between you opening your SSH key and a process you started opening it. An agent on your laptop can read everything you can read: your cloud credentials, the password manager while it is unlocked, every site your browser is signed into. When the company is you and a laptop, that laptop holds the company's whole reach.

The 2018 book had a draft on this, Don't touch production, written for developers: teams and individuals should be "allergic to being given access to data and systems", because the more you can reach, the more a compromise of your machine costs. The same rule now applies to the agent you have just set up, and to you as its operator. A mistake inside a boundary is a bad afternoon. The same mistake inside your account is an incident.

So pick the surface by reach. My agents run in places that are not my laptop: a chat with nothing connected, for thinking; Cowork in the cloud, with no repositories, for most of the agentic work; Claude Code on the web, with one repository per session, when code has to change. The surface with the repositories has no mailbox, the surface with the mailbox has no repositories, and the surface with neither does the thinking. Your Contacts Project is the first of these: it reaches one folder and one mailbox, and nothing else of yours.

In 2018 I wrote that Slack was "a massive agent for change" because it put humans, bots, servers and events in one asynchronous place, and I asked whether you were going to take an active role in making it work for an organisation. The question is the same now, with an agent in the room, and the organisation may be the one you are starting. The first task in the 2018 book was to write a blog post about your experiments. Write the one about this afternoon.

From the register: Your first workspace: from a spreadsheet to a folder per person, drafts never send, Why your agents should not run on your laptop. Answers: MVP for Gen Z Dev, Slack and ChatOps, Automate your Workflows, Don't touch production.

14How to work with agents

In 2018 I wrote a chapter that began "We need to talk about your IDE!" The IDE, I said, is like a car in a race that you are the driver of, and the more time you spend looking after it, the better a programmer you will be. I still think that. But the thing to look after has moved. The editor is now one channel among several. What decides the quality of the work is the session, the brief you hand it, and the rules it runs under. This chapter is about those three, after about a year of working with agents every day. If you are starting a company, this is how it works before there is anyone to hire; if you hire people, read it as what to look for, because the habits below leave evidence.

An agent is a session with a focus

An agent is a session with a particular focus: a few documents that explain the project, what it is, where it is going, what has been decided; a role.md for this agent, with what it may do, what it must not do, and where it writes; and a session that reads those before it does anything, and is named for the role. My sessions are named for the project and the work, "sgit.ai | website development", and agent sessions get an @ in front of the role: @briefs, @inbox, @librarian. That is enough to start.

The most important habit is the simplest one: one session per topic. A thread is the model's frame of reference, and everything in it is context for the next answer. Put three projects in one thread and the model starts to mix them, borrowing a name from one and a decision from another. It is not being careless. It is doing exactly what you asked, with everything in front of it. When the topic changes, open a new session.

Once there are several agents, two problems arrive, and both are solved by writing. The agents learn things the others should know, so each one writes lessons: one thing learned, the evidence for it, which agents it applies to, and whether it still holds. Every agent reads the lessons file at the start of its next session. That is memory in its plainest form. And the agents do so much that I need a debrief: what was done, what is open, what went wrong. When two of my newsroom's agents released the same version number within minutes, one push was refused and became the next number; nobody would have known unless someone wrote it down, so the lesson, fetch before you take a version number, is now in the file.

The last piece is a daily brief. I work on many threads at once and follow tangents, so I lose threads. The answer is an agent, the Librarian, that reads everything that happened yesterday across every repository and writes one page before I start: what needs me, what happened, the agents' debriefs, the threads worth picking up, and at most three suggestions for where to put my attention today. It reads everything and writes only its own folder. It flags; others fix.

For Dinis when the article was written the Librarian did not run yet; the sources existed and the brief was written. Does it run now, and what did the first weeks of briefs change about how you start the day?

The 2018 draft Workflows had a bullet I never wrote up: thinking in systems and workflows "is actually how most programming works". It is, and now the workflow is made of sessions.

The brief is the unit of work

When I want an agent to do something substantial, the instruction I give it is usually a voice memo, and a voice memo is a terrible instruction. It is in the order I thought it, it mixes the idea with the task, it does not say what already exists, and it leaves the agent to guess which parts are decisions and which are thinking out loud.

An article fixes most of that, because writing one is research.
