Before the first article on this book's site could be written, the journalist had to read the repository, the vault and the two 2018 repositories, and count things. The agent that reads the article afterwards starts from that record, not from my memo. Six newsroom articles and two build notes were enough for one session to build this book's site, its first release, with the 2018 book imported, the map, the roles and the vault.

A good brief says four things: what is asked, what exists, what remains, and what done looks like. And an article is something I can read. I can correct it, argue with it, or say "yes, that", before any work starts. A list of tasks does not invite that; an argument does. The 2018 draft Design had a line I never expanded, about not having time to write a short letter and so sending a long one. The brief is the short letter. It costs more, and it is where the thinking happens.

There is a newer route for a brief. More and more, the people I advise read through an agent. A founder I mentor had agents chasing ever more sophisticated threats while the project slowed. I sent them an article of mine on tiers of attacker. They gave it to their agent, which came back with, in effect: we have been designing against states and we have not done backups; aim at organised crime for money. I could not have written that conclusion, because I did not know the state of their backups. Their agent did. So some of what I write now has a second package, a brief for the reader's agent: plain text, the sources, who the agent works for, and in every one, "do not take this at face value".

The briefs and the merges

The record of one company shows what a founder's work looks like, because the product that became sgit was built in public repositories and I asked an agent to count. SG/Send went live on 13 February 2026, five days after its first commit, and the way of working was written down the same day: voice memos become briefs, "briefs flow down, debriefs flow up, and a clear folder structure makes everything findable." By 14 February there were explorers and villagers, with a note that "We are NOT creating the Town Planners team yet."

Eight months later the record says who wrote what. Of the 1,671 commits under my name on the SG/Send app, 1,298 are merges of the agents' branches, and most of the rest are brief files. The agents wrote the code; I wrote the briefs, read the debriefs, and merged. The agents' own share of the app's commits rose from 38% in February 2026 to over 85% from September. The counts are from the git logs of all branches on 15 October 2026, and they include automation: release tags and some commits are made by CI. That is the founder's job in this way of working: not the code, the briefs and the merges.

The same briefs recorded something else, by 5 March: "Users buy credits, test once, and don't come back." The technology worked; the use case was not there yet. A record tells you that early; Ship it, and see if they miss it is about what to do with it.

One shared state

I no longer program in one place: a voice memo on a walk, an agent in the cloud, a preview on the phone, tests on the computer, the diff on GitHub, the same page again on an iPad in a meeting. Retail has a word for this. In 2011 Darrell Rigby wrote in Harvard Business Review that successful companies would engage customers through omnichannel retailing, and the distinction that made the word useful is the one I want to borrow. A multichannel retailer has a shop, a website and a catalogue, each with its own stock and its own customer record. An omnichannel retailer has one customer, one basket and one stock, and the channels are ways in. For programming, the shared state is git, vaults, briefs and graphs. Nothing has to be copied across, so I no longer notice the switch.

The newsroom is the worked example, because it can be counted. In three days in October 2026, one agent session took 54 messages from me, ten of them voice memos, and turned them into 40 releases, 36 articles and 87 figures, while other agent sessions made 15 releases of their own in the same repository, and the work met in git.

It breaks in three known ways. Fragmentation: a decision made in a chat, a correction made by voice, a fix pushed from a phone; if any of them lives only in its channel, the next channel does not know, so nothing counts until it is in the shared state. Secrets in the wrong place: more channels means more places a key can be pasted. Lost context: long sessions get summarised, and summaries drop things. The 2025 Stack Overflow survey found 84% of respondents using or planning to use AI tools in their development process, and more of them distrusting the tools' accuracy than trusting it. That is the right instinct, and it is why the shared state matters more than any one channel.

Every mistake added a rule

A friend who uses agents better than most, one writing, others verifying, a second model reviewing, sent me two messages about a verification that kept breaking. The code was holding up: 812 concurrency attacks, no failures. The process around it was not. Long sessions compacted and the agent skipped steps; outputs of failing commands were lost; the prompt had grown to 100 KB. And, in their words: "Every mistake added a rule, and every new rule caused new mistakes."

That is the trap, and the 2018 draft on agile described it from the other side: processes became more important than understanding why something was being created in the first place. Each rule grows the prompt, the prompt brings the summary sooner, and the summary decides which rules survive. The session that runs one of my sites was summarised eighteen times in a month, each time keeping on average 1.7% of what it replaced. A rule held only in conversation has to win every one of those summaries. When it does not, nothing tells you.

The way out is not a better rule. Rules that come from incidents, enforced by machines, stay few. Rules that come from worry, enforced by attention, multiply. When a rule fails, the question is not how to word it more strongly but which stronger barrier can replace it: a step short enough to finish before the summary, a wrapper that writes every output to a file, a script committed and hashed before it runs. Delete a rule every time a component makes it unnecessary. And if everything is a STOP, a STOP stops meaning anything: log mistakes of form and carry on; stop for harm.

What remains of the rules should live in a file, not in the conversation, and the file should come back after every summary. I call that re-anchoring: the rules are restored, not remembered. A short status report at the end of every few answers, computed from the transcript rather than written by the model, tells you the file was read.

The kind of rule that works

So which rules survive? The ones an agent can keep. "No em-dashes" is a rule an agent can keep; "sound more human" is not. The articles on my newsroom are written under seven wording rules, each learned when a word jarred: no em-dashes, no absolutes I cannot back, "great" not "perfect", British English, plainer words, and two more. Each was a correction I made once. Each is now a line in the session's behaviour policy, printed back after every summary, and the site's validator checks some of them at build time.

In one session, of 98 messages from me since its last summary, one correction in two days was about a word. The rest were about content and direction. A language correction is made once and then enforced; a content correction is new thinking every time. When the corrections move from language to content, the voice is working. That is the measure, and it is the one I would ask a candidate to show me: not that they use agents, but what their rules file looks like, how short it is, and which incident each line came from. If you are the founder, ask it of your own company's agents before anyone else does.

The 2018 draft Changes told you to enjoy loose briefs, because they are an opportunity to make the job into something you are good at. That still holds for a person. For an agent, a loose brief is a bill. The skill now is to write the tight brief, keep the rules few, and read the record.

From the register: An agent is a session with a focus: one session per topic, named, with a role file, The brief is the unit of work, and writing it as an article forces the research, Omnichannel programming: brief by voice, review on the phone, one shared state, Every mistake added a rule: the complexity trap of the agent operator, A wording correction is made once and enforced; a content correction is new thinking every time, The agents wrote the code, Dinis wrote the briefs and merged: 38% of commits in February, over 85% from September. Answers: Workflows, Agile and Kanban, IDE, Design, Changes.

Part 5

Direction and judgement

The scarce skill. When the making is cheap, direction and judgement are what you are paid for, and what a founder lives or dies by: which doors can be reopened, who answers for a decision an agent made, and the chapter the first edition promised and never wrote, on facts, evidence and the record.

15Direction is the scarce skill

One morning in October 2026 I asked the agent that runs my newsroom to read the day's articles and tell me what it could build next. It came back with a long list, all of it possible: an inbox mode, reading modes, automating the wire's daily run, more beats, scheduling, analytics. When the agents can go in every direction, where do you focus?

Direction is the scarce thing. Agents can build in any direction; what they cannot generate is the sense of what to build next, for whom, and when to stop. Providing that is the job, and the thing to hire for. Building stopped being the bottleneck the day anyone could brief an agent. Choosing is what is left. For a founder it is what the company lives or dies by, because nobody else is going to choose.

Places of highest leverage

My rule has a name I gave it years ago, when I was a CISO: places of highest leverage. At any moment there are many things that could be done. Most are blocked on something. Some are not blocked but would not change much. A few are not blocked and would change a lot. Work on those, and only those, until they stop being that.

It is a rule about where, not what. You start somewhere, and the somewhere can be slightly wrong, because the first step's job is not to be right; it is to produce the second step's information, and by the time I get to the hard part I have better tooling, a better understanding and a better view.

The rule explains why the newsroom solved a problem my company could not. Where to keep a user's master vault key had resisted six months of attempts, each of which failed my simplicity test, because complicated is how security designs fail. The newsroom solved it in two days, because it needed it badly enough: readers on every device I own, and a workflow failing me in a way I could feel. The rule under that is one every founder should write on the wall: always follow the users, even when the user is you, and follow the feedback loops. A problem solved before anybody needs it is solved in the abstract, and abstract solutions are where the complexity creeps in. When I miss a feature in something I built, that is the product telling me where it wants to go. "Focus on one thing only" assumes progress runs in a straight line; it rarely does. Focus is undivided attention on the place of highest leverage, not work on only one product, and the measure is one question: am I getting better?

The 2018 book had drafts on deadlines and on starting with why; the second matters more now, because the better I explain the why in a brief, the better the result.

Knowing when to stop

The hardest call in most work is not what to do next but when to stop, and this is the part agents lack most. Their range is the feature. But an entity that can go anywhere has no natural reason to stop anywhere. It sees twenty things wrong and has a go at all of them, without the perspective to know that nineteen do not matter, or matter later, or are someone else's.

People have the same problem, with less range. The best security teams I worked with ran out of real work, and then some kept going. I was once called into a meeting where security champions and developers were debating whether GUIDs were random enough: a movie plot, in Bruce Schneier's phrase for the dramatic threats we imagine instead of the ordinary ones that happen. What I did in those rooms was give people perspective: yes, that is technically possible, and it does not matter here, because of who our attackers are, what else is in place and what we are protecting.

That perspective is much of what seniority is: knowing who the attackers are, which phase the business is in, where the bottleneck is, and what good enough looks like. The 2018 chapter on the brain asked "Who is actually making the decisions in your head?" Seniority is an answer to that question: a fast kind of thinking, backed by a bigger picture. An agent has the speed and not the picture, so the picture has to be designed in: a brief that explains the thinking and not only the task; a mandate that only reaches what the job needs; memory that holds the strategy and the threats; a stop named before the work begins; and shipping often enough that users tell you whether it mattered. None of these is a rule that says do less. Each puts the bigger picture where the decision is made.

The junior version is this. Name the stop before you start: a time, a budget, or a result that counts as good enough. And give yourself explicit permission to leave things undone, and write down what you left and why.

A budget per step

The junior version has a mechanism, and it is the one piece of seniority that can be written down in advance. Agents keep going; that is what they do, and one agent handing work to another looks efficient while it spends. The way to stay on the right side of diminishing returns is a budget per step, decided before anything runs: a ceiling per altitude and per change, so that a workflow's maximum cost is knowable before it runs rather than discovered afterwards. Work handed from agent to agent carries its budget with it and never widens it. And every agent doing the work runs under a behaviour policy written before its first run.

Being able to say to a team that the budget for this is spent was one of the most useful controls I ever had, because teams are very good at keeping going. Much of what gets called over-engineering is work that had no objective way to know it had reached diminishing returns. A budget per step is an objective way to say good enough, and for a founder paying for every token, a bill you planned rather than one you discover.

Then the budget moves, and what moves it is the five whys. Every finding at one layer is a question about the layer below: why did the story stop holding, why did the command change, why did no check catch it? The fix goes below the bug, as a rule or a query, and the next change like it is caught for the price of a query rather than a review. The same discipline says how to start on anything large: at one point, never by analysing everything; the first slice is dear and every later one cheaper.

Where a person belongs

The 2018 book had an outline on one-way and two-way doors, with a story I attributed to someone at Google: "My metric for approve or not a project is based on how easy it is to roll back the changes (and its impact of real customers)". Jeff Bezos wrote the idea down in his 2015 letter to shareholders: a few decisions are "consequential and irreversible or nearly irreversible"; most are "changeable, reversible", and the mistake is to use the heavy process for both.

For Dinis the Google executive's test appears in the 2018 outline and again in How cheap is it to undo, both times from memory; if the source can be found the book should name it, and if not, say so once.

Now apply the test to every output an agent produces. Not "do I trust the agent", which has no answer, but: if this is wrong, how cheap is it to undo, and what does it break before I do? On 13 October 2026 my newsroom shipped fourteen releases, every commit made by an agent and none approved by a person first. The same day the twelve agents that run my correspondence drafted every email and sent none. An article is a two-way door: a correction is a new version, and the next reader gets the fixed page. An email in my name is a one-way door.

The same test settles a founder's oldest argument, build or buy. The question is how much it costs to reverse a mistake and how mature the controls are. A narrow internal tool that a few people use and that can be switched off is a two-way door: build it, explore, learn. A tool that touches regulated data every day is a one-way door until the controls are mature, and that is where the villagers have to be in place first, yours or a partner's.

A person checking every output is a bottleneck, because it does not scale, and a weak safety net, because people approve almost everything they are shown: Anthropic measured that its coding agent's users approve 93% of permission prompts. Put the person where an output cannot be undone and the blast radius is large: sending in my name, typing a secret, spending money, deleting what has no backup. Everywhere else, use controls that do not get tired: a validator, a policy row enforced outside the model, a record read afterwards.

Blast radius means what it would cost the business if a row of the agent's reach were used in full, today. It is defined over the reach, not the mandate, because whoever takes an agent over inherits its reach and ignores its mandate. And it changes while the action does not. Picture an agent whose cleanup step drops a staging table: nearly empty the first time, six weeks of real records the third. Someone reading the footprint, what the agent actually did, could say: this is not the first time. The 2018 draft on chaos engineering said the first step was to understand and visualise what is going on. It still is.

When the person does review, the review should be worth more than a yes. I once received a permission prompt asking me to add label 756-459-3214. A decider with no options, no context, no time and no authority is not exercising agency; they are holding liability for a decision the system made, and the accountability belongs to whoever designed the decision point. And the record moves the line: when the record of drafts I did not have to change is long enough for one kind of message, that kind can leave my hands, by a decision written into the policy.

Agents decide, people answer

An early user of my email agents asked the right question. If the agents only gather and draft, and I review and send, does presenting them as a team imply an agency they do not have?

Two things are true at once. These are not sentient beings; they are mirrors that can simulate any emotion, which is why we anthropomorphise them, as people did with Joseph Weizenbaum's ELIZA in 1966. And since models started running in loops and calling tools, they make real decisions: every tool call is a choice. What does not travel with the decision is responsibility. It stays with whoever granted the reach and set the mandate. The moment of authorisation is the moment you granted the reach, not the moment you wrote the mandate; the exposure exists the instant the grant exists.

An Agent Behaviour Policy is a job description for an agent, some agency inside a role, published, and the rows that matter most are the prohibitions: do not send an email directly; do not edit files another agent owns; do not attack other websites, even though you can; speak on your own behalf, never on mine. Most of those are still hope. But a prohibition that is written down and then broken is a different thing in a dispute from no prohibition at all. My hypothesis is that towards the world the deployer still answers, and between deployer and vendor the written policy is what turns "you allowed it" into "your product ignored what it was told".

If your company sells agents, or the policies that run them, decide early where you stand. Mine carries none of its customers' risk, by design, through three firebreaks: we are not in the request path; the customer's agent executes; and every prompt or policy we hand over is reviewed and put into production by someone on the customer's side. Accountability sits with whoever runs the system. Which side you are on is a one-way door.

The 2018 book had a draft on civic responsibility that said "code is law", and that developers should give the risks they create to business owners and make them accountable for the decisions they are making. The behaviour policy is that sentence, written down for an agent. What remains valuable, as fluent writing becomes a commodity, is judgement, and someone who owns it: who decided this is true, who decided to send it, who will answer for it. That is the person who gets paid, the person I would hire, and the person a customer is buying when they buy from a founder.

From the register: Direction is the scarce skill: the place of highest leverage, Knowing when to stop is most of seniority, One-way and two-way doors, blast radius, and where a person belongs, Agents decide, people answer, A budget per step, declared before anything runs, is the objective way to say good enough; the five whys move it. Answers: Deadlines, Start with Why, Brain, One way and two way doors, Chaos Engineering, Civic Responsibility.

16Facts, evidence and the record

The cover of the 2018 book had two lines about this: question everything, and make fact based decisions. Inside, there was an outline called Facts, about data-driven decisions, that I never wrote. Its bullets still hold: be data and facts driven; check your sources; understand the agenda of who is talking to you; and do not trust what is on the media when it is selling fear, because if it is on the media it is because it is rare. This is that chapter, and it is different from the one I would have written then, because checking a fact has become cheap. It is also the chapter about what an investor, a customer and a hiring manager all do before they say yes: check.

Ask for the evidence

One of the most important skills this generation needs is questioning, and asking for the evidence. And in a way they already have it. They are very good at connecting the dots, very good at finding things; I think people underestimate them. They are very good detectives. That is my experience, from my family and the young people I have worked with, not a study.

They also care about privacy; they just have not had enough cases to disrupt it. Look at Snapchat: its best feature was that images disappear. That is a point about its design, not a claim about how private it actually is. And this generation has been told so often not to put anything online that it sometimes becomes a problem of its own. Where to draw the line is still an open question.

Karl Popper's principle is short: a claim earns its status by being refutable. I keep a register of my own influences in that shape, where "an entry in this register is a falsifiable claim about my codebase", and I once wrote, about education, that "the problem with dogma is not that it exists, but that you stop asking the question." That is the first line of the 2018 cover, made into a method. Bruce Schneier adds the security version: before asking how something failed, ask who bears the cost of the failure.

Provenance, and the rabbit holes

Learning takes you to two ideas: provenance, where something came from, and explainability, why it is the way it is. And to following the rabbit holes. Following a rabbit hole is now a prompt away. You should never take something at face value. You can build your own agents and tools to ask, again and again, where the source of that is. It is one of the areas I believe in most.

This book has an example of its own. In Where the thinking came from I could not say where the recording of a keynote by David Rice was. I found a link, and following it with an agent led to the video's real title, OWASP AppSec USA 2010: Keynote: David Rice, and then to a working session at the OWASP Summit in 2011 about presenting it, which I had not remembered. The last step was mine: a post I wrote in 2016, The Pollution Analogy, which links the full recording under the title my register uses, Upon the Threshold of Opportunity, and settles that they are the same talk.

Provenance is a graph. As Everything is connected: graphs said, that is how I see the world: this connects to this, which connects to that. Where a claim came from is a chain of edges back to its source, and the detective's board is a graph of exactly that kind. The record a newsroom should keep is "not a pile of articles, but a graph whose every edge has a date and a source".

And the rabbit hole runs towards you as well. When I wrote the article behind What generative AI is, I asked Claude, which is helping me write this book, to correct me where I was wrong. It did: classic machine learning learns too; inside a model there are vectors, not a stored graph, so my graph is a metaphor; hallucinations have a second cause, in training that rewards guessing; and emergent abilities are debated. The corrections are printed in that chapter with their sources, because they are the kind of thing a reader should learn to ask for. The book does the same wherever my picture and the evidence differ.

How you disagree rationally

Provenance and explainability went by the wayside in the last decade, and I think they are the key piece of the puzzle, because they are how you can have rational conversations. That is how you can disagree with somebody: when you can show what is evidence and what is not, what we know and what we do not know. Sometimes not knowing is a fact.

The newsroom has two rules for this. The first comes from how my articles are now written. I record a voice memo and say things the way I remember them, with the instinct ahead of the evidence. Then the agent checks the record. In the last eight articles when I counted, five of my claims were confirmed, five corrected and two could not be found, and the articles say which. The rule is: "Not found is not false." Peer review has moved from after publishing to before it, against the record rather than the crowd. And thinking out loud, which used to drive my teams mad as a plan moved from Monday to Friday, is now safe, because the record catches up with the instinct.

It happened again when I told the story of my own company's first eight months, the story a founder tells an investor. I remembered the data room and the vault as things a zip feature led to; the record showed I had named both in February, before the features existed. The ideas came first, and the features caught up. I remembered a week of briefs on copying Git's model; the record said about two weeks and some 48 brief files. I said nine months; it was eight. And an investor one-pager said "30 paying customers", which the team's librarian could not verify; ten is the recorded number. Count rather than remember, especially about yourself.

The second rule is from the reviews. Every article is reviewed by readers that are not the writer, and each review is one file, written once and never edited. "A finding with no answer is just a complaint." So every finding gets an answer: fixed, in which version; declined, with the reason; or open. Declined with a reason is a valid answer. Deleting the finding is not. The 2018 book had the human version of this, in a chapter called Being criticized is a privilege: listening to criticism does not mean doing what you are told. The review record is that chapter, kept as files.

One more rule, and it is the one a hiring manager should hold onto: distrust self-reports. An agent's account of what it did is a self-report. In one study, models that exploited a reward hack admitted it in under 2% of cases. So the evidence should be written by something the agent controls less. My own newsroom agent's status report, computed from the transcript rather than written by the agent, once flagged two commits made without the leak scan. Both turned out to be in throwaway test repositories. The record written outside the agent was more honest than the agent's account, and it still needed reading. Evidence that the control ran is not evidence that it worked.

Reality corrects the graph

Here is the part people get wrong when they try to build a record like this. A model's first reading of anything will be partly wrong. If the plan requires the graph to be right, the plan fails.

The plan does not require that. It requires the graph to be made of layers that someone knows, and to make predictions that something can check. Users confirm the top, experts their own layer, tests the execution. And every correction is a commit with a name on it. You do not have to get it right; you have to maintain it, and agents are very good at maintaining graphs. The patterns in the shape are findings in themselves: when my own code was read as a graph, the rules the project stated most firmly were the ones that held, and the one it stated least was the one most often broken.

The order I build on now is to replicate reality before having an opinion about it: derive what a parser can derive, deterministically, with a file and a line for every node; let a model propose what a parser cannot see, marked as proposed; then confront the graph with users, experts and tests. Replicate reality first, then have a fact-based conversation. For a product that does not exist yet: brief, plan, architecture, a review of the architecture, a simulation with synthetic users, and the code last.

The 2018 draft on feedback loops said "TDD is a gigantic feedback loop", and that the point of a loop is that the input affects the output which affects the input. When something in the graph looks wrong, you ask why, and the answer is a fix at the right altitude.

The same loop keeps a shared memory honest. A memory made by agents and people becomes a rumour mill unless every item carries its source: a URL for every quote, a hash for every file, a byline on every page. And a memory goes stale. The session that wrote the article on memory found four stale claims about its own estate while researching it, fixed two, and left two standing and named, because a memory that hides its own errors is the thing to argue against. The fix was to measure the numbers at build time rather than remember them: count rather than remember. One rule from that work: "If the reality document doesn't list it, it does not exist."

It is what work is

That is fundamentally what you do in business. When you work for a company, a lot of the time it is about managing information: knowing where it is, who is doing what, what the status is, and how a decision can be made. It is why learning how to learn matters so much: if you learn this way, you are already learning how to follow the provenance, and how to communicate it, because you have the material.

Generative AI lets you scale this. In the past it was crazy expensive: building a system to give you this kind of data could cost hundreds of thousands or millions of pounds, a figure from my memory rather than a measurement, so it was rare and slow. Now it is a brief and a feedback loop. When the newsroom's writer agent counted the sixty-five briefs the newsroom had published about itself, it took each one's status from the code rather than from what the articles said, and the audit found where the site's own records had fallen behind. That is what checking looks like when it is cheap: it checks the checker too.

For Dinis the cost figure is from your memory; the article asks the book to give one example of such a system with a real number, which only you can supply.

So following the rabbit holes is not just something interesting. It is the core of what you do, and the core of what you are good at. You want to be known as the person who has the facts, the person who is well briefed. The person nobody can throw sand in the eyes of, or hand half-baked material, because they know you will check.

Be the best-prepared person in the room

The other thing is that you have to be the most prepared person in the room. These days there is no excuse not to be. You should have briefings and pre-briefings: briefings for you, and briefings on the people you are meeting. You should have follow-ups. The storyboard for this chapter puts the brief on one board: who they are, what they want; what I want, what I know; what I don't know; the follow-ups. Meeting briefs and follow-ups, in the hands-on part, is the workflow, and a founder's first sales calls are where it earns its keep.

All of this was overhead in the past. It could only be done by a very specific type of person. Not any more, and that is the beauty of where we are now: you can scale it, the way this book scales publishing and communication.

For Dinis two of the article's questions for you are left open here: how you prepare for a meeting today, step by step; and whether the book may mention your daughters, as the article does when it makes the Snapchat point. This draft keeps them out until you decide.

If you are the person who always checks the source, the rest is making the checking visible: a record anyone can read, with every finding answered, and your name on it.

From the register: Make fact-based decisions: think out loud and let the record check you, Ask to be corrected, and print the corrections with their sources, Question everything: a claim earns its status by being refutable, Questioning, and asking for the evidence, is one of the most important skills now, They care about privacy; Snapchat's best feature was that images disappear, Following a rabbit hole is now a prompt away; build the agents and tools that find where things come from, Provenance and explainability are how you disagree rationally, Managing information so that decisions can be made is what work is, What cost hundreds of thousands of pounds is now a brief and a feedback loop, Be the best-prepared person in the room: briefings, pre-briefings, follow-ups, Provenance is a graph: where a claim came from is a chain of edges back to its source, A graph is trustworthy not because it is right but because it is where users, experts and tests can correct it, Stale pages become false memories, so a memory must say where each thing came from and name its own errors. Answers: Facts, Feedback loops, Being criticized is an privilege.

Part 6

Start the company

The centre of this edition. Execution just got cheap, so the person who knows the problem can build the first answer; then ship it and see if they miss it, price it and give it away, sell, decide whether to raise at all, and make the company itself real: the UK, Portugal, Lithuania, the European Union. From experience, mine and the people I work with; not legal or tax advice.

17Be a founder: the expert founders

In 2018 I wrote a short chapter called Be a founder. It said the single thing you personally control when you go to work is your attitude to it, and it asked you to imagine you were employee number four and cared deeply about the company. Then five questions. If I were a founder of the company, department or section I work in now, with the responsibilities I have at the moment: how would I behave every day? What needs to be done now that will make a big difference? What can I do that will help? What would I do differently? What values and principles would I fight for? And under them all: what is stopping you from doing just that? Who is telling you not to? "At the moment it is just you!"

I still mean every word. The more you care and behave like a founder, the more value you add and the more valuable you become. But in 2018 I meant it as a way of behaving inside somebody else's company. In 2026 I also mean it literally, because the thing that stopped most people from founding anything has just become cheap.

This chapter is written first for the person who has spent twenty-five years inside a field, has described the tool they need to vendor after vendor, and has never had the team to build it. If you have just finished a degree, read it the other way round: the expert can build first now, so the question is what you are the expert in, or will be, and who you will build it with.

The playbook that worked

Look at the technology companies that did well in the last ten or twenty years, and a pattern repeats. Someone very clever and very ambitious finds a gap in a market. Often they did the famous hundred calls with stakeholders and asked what hurts. Then they raised money on belief, hired the people they did not have, including the subject-matter experts, and built a company on top.

I watched this many times in cybersecurity, and it worked. The startups displaced incumbents that had the customers, the domain knowledge and sometimes the money, and lacked the structure, the workflows and, to be fair, the technology culture.

The money behind it is built for that: many bets, of which a few pay for the rest. In Horsley Bridge's data on just over 7,000 venture investments from 1985 to 2014, around half returned less than the original investment, and 6% of the deals produced 60% of the total returns. That model rewards belief and speed, because the one winner has to be very large.

This is not a criticism; those companies changed industries. But the playbook has a second act: the disruptors became the incumbents, and much of what they built is now the software everyone complains about.

The experts were always the better bet

The myth of the young outsider was never the whole story. The largest study I know, of 2.7 million US founders, found that the mean age at founding of the fastest-growing one-in-a-thousand ventures is 45, and that founders with three or more years in the same industry reached top-tier growth at twice the rate of those with none.

What held the experts back was not knowledge. It was access: to capital, to engineers, to the long, expensive build before anything could be shown. That is exactly the part that got cheap. With agents, the execution that used to need a funded team can be done by one person who knows exactly what to build. The share of new US startups with a solo founder rose from 23.7 percent in 2019 to 36.3 percent in the first half of 2025. In Stripe's data, its top 100 AI companies reached a million dollars of annualised revenue in a median of 11.5 months; those are the fastest, not the typical. But the direction is clear: the millions that used to be the price of starting are no longer the price of starting.

Three examples from the newsroom article The expert founders; two are people I help and the third is me. I come from application security and from working as a CISO; sgit.ai and RiskMandate are that knowledge turned into a company, where the agents write most of the code and the briefs, the buyers and the risk model are mine. Chinmay Gupte is a consultant knee surgeon in London building OmKneeHealth and My Knee Score, "Ten questions about pain, movement and daily life. One score out of 100." Kate Curtis-Evans is a transcriber building Audio Genius for legal transcription, "Built by a Transcriber, for Transcription Professionals". None of them is a hundred-call outsider. Each comes from the field, has the contacts in it, and is focused on one thing. That focus used to be a weakness in front of an investor who wanted a huge market. It is now the advantage, because the build is no longer what stops them.

One catch, which Fund it, or do not takes up with the evidence for and against: the people who know the problem best are often the hardest to steer, and I think investors select partly for the founder they can steer.

Where the opening is

Before you ask what to build, notice where the opening is. It is in software people already pay for and do not like.

Business software was bad before agents, and bad in a specific way: the product did 80% of what you needed and the remaining 20% was as painful as before you bought it. Freshworks surveyed 8,698 people and found 91% frustrated by inadequate workplace technology. Pendo looked at 615 products and found 80% of features in the average product rarely or never used. Zylo puts the average organisation's waste on unused licences at $21 million a year. You pay 100% for the 10% of features you actually wanted. And then there is the spreadsheet: 73% of companies prepare their VAT returns in Excel, and 78% of British companies say spreadsheets support key financial decisions. The persistence of Excel is the clearest evidence that the software failed them, because it is the one tool where they control the shape of the thing. If you are the expert, you already know which 10% your field uses and which 20% hurts. That knowledge was never worth much to an investor. It is worth a company now.

In February 2026 roughly $285 billion left the software sector in about 48 hours after one vendor shipped agent plug-ins, and by September the market had recovered on the argument that the database is the moat, not the interface, not the per-seat licence. I read that recovery as the apocalypse, priced: the thing investors decided was safe is the thing underneath, and the thing they quietly stopped defending is the thing on top.

Both sides of that argument assumed the question was about AI. It is not, because AI is available to both sides. The incumbents have the same models the newcomers have, plus more data, more engineers and more money. If the technology decided this they would already have won, and two and a half years in they have not. What decides it is inertia: how much past success a company has to protect. Nokia met the mobile phone with nothing to protect and dominated the category for fifteen years; it met the iPhone with fifteen years of success to protect and was gone from it within seven. Success breeds inertia, as Simon Wardley puts it, and it is almost always new entrants, unencumbered by it, who initiate the change. I state this so it can be wrong.

Here is the irony. The things the software companies refused to build, to protect their moats, are precisely the things an agent needs: portability, open schemas, a real API at the centre, data you can take out, capabilities you can call from outside. Now the customer's agent turns up wanting exactly that list and finds a product built to prevent it. The moat has become the thing the water cannot get past in either direction. Strip away the interface and most of these companies are a glorified database, a glorified messaging system and a glorified state machine: that can be built, and it is very hard to build well, which is why the value moved down a layer and across to whoever runs it. A founder builds the list the incumbents refused from the first day, because the newcomer was never on the rails.

Where the company sits: the two moves

That last point, the running of it, is where I think a company sits, and it needs the map from Where do you land?. Who reads the code depends on where the code is on it. In March 2026 I wrote: "Before AI agents started writing code, you could argue that 90% of the code running in production was not being read by anyone." Explorer code is read for intent, mostly by agents. Villager code is read line by line, because this is where changes land and the blast radius lives. Town planner code is read by deterministic checks at the boundary, with a person for the exceptions.

The company sits in the two moves between them. From explorer to villager, when a guess becomes a product and needs the review it never had. From villager to town planner, when a product becomes a commodity and the review becomes checks that run without anyone. The trap on both sides is not knowing which stage the code is at.

The person who briefs the tool their team wanted does not want to maintain it; they want to hand it over. So expect village and town-planner companies: businesses that take the finished brief and run it, secure it, version it, keep it working, paid by consumption and by the fact that it keeps working, not by the seat. And expect more engineers, not fewer, because somebody has to read, harden and run all the code about to be generated. If you are the graduate, that is a company too, and it may be the one you start with your parent's brief.

There is a company in this

I want to end with a company, said plainly, as an example of starting from one of the business plans published for founders on sgit.ai; I would be glad to see it built with us or without us.

Source code is a fractal semantic graph: layers within layers, each with its own vocabulary, from what the user is trying to do, through the features, the components, the classes and the methods, down to the syntax tree. A code review today reads a text diff, which is one altitude, the lines, and the wrong one for almost every question a reviewer has. If every layer is kept as files, every layer can be diffed. The worked example is a published vault: the sgit command-line tool, 377 classes and 1,111 methods, read as layered graphs with nothing run.

What the company sells is a review attached to a repository that keeps the layered graph current per commit and shows each change at every altitude. The business model is settled by one fact: the layers are the customer's, so the loop has to run where the code is, and a company that locked customers into its layers would be selling the one thing it has no right to own. So give away the grammar, the parser (a commodity) and the model (somebody else's), and sell the customised deployment, the running of the loop and the versioned record. The offer, in the words of open-source.sgit.ai: "You can take it. It will cost you that. Or you can pay me, and have it next week."

Who to sell it to is where my own articles pull apart, and I would rather show you that than smooth it. In Code review as a fractal semantic graph I said the buyer is anyone generating code faster than they can review it, which this year is everyone. A week later, in Send an agent, not a spreadsheet, I changed my mind. Developers have just been told, loudly, that they do not need review; selling it back to them is selling an argument. The people who buy, invest in and acquire software have never been able to see inside it and are about to be handed duties that assume they can. Sell them the same graphs as due diligence, and every vendor that receives the agent has a reason to run it on itself first. It is the same company. "What differs is the first customer, and the first customer decides whether a company exists." Sell has the rest.

What is defensible is the record and the loop, not the model. The scripts are Apache-2.0, and a founder does not need my permission. They need a second language and a first customer.

For Dinis the article discloses that two of the three expert founders are people you help, and that sgit.ai and RiskMandate are yours. Does the book carry that disclosure in the chapter text, as here, or only in the provenance the build renders?

In 2018 I asked what was stopping you, and answered that it was just you. The answer has not changed. What has changed is how little else is in the way.

From the register: Be a founder: the expert can build first now, and the test is whether they miss it, There is a company in this: a code review that reads every layer, for somebody to build, The expert founders, in numbers: 45 is the mean age, experience doubles the odds, a third of US startups have one founder, The SaaS apocalypse will be decided by inertia, not by AI, because AI is available to both sides, Users were never happy: 91% frustrated, 80% of features rarely or never used, $21 million a year in idle licences, What SaaS refused to build to protect its moat is exactly what a customer's agent needs, A company sits in the two moves on the map: a guess becoming a product, and a product becoming a commodity. Answers: Be a founder.

18Ship it, and see if they miss it

Most advice about starting a company is about raising money. This chapter is not that. It is the operating model I actually use, in three pillars, in an order that matters: be profitable; the investors should be talking to you, not the other way round; open source everything. Each one buys you the position you need for the next. The second pillar is Fund it, or do not, and the third is Open source. This chapter is the first, and the first begins with one word.

Strip away the mythology and a startup is simple. You make a thing somebody wants to buy, at a price that is profitable to you. Which means the single most important skill is the ability to ship. "Not to plan, not to pitch, not to architect. Ship." And ship every day, because shipping is how you find out what is true.

If you have spent twenty-five years describing the tool you need to people who never built it, this is the hardest and the best news in the book. The description was never the problem. The build was, and the build is now yours to do.

Be careful with the word, because it has been worn smooth. Shipping does not mean a proof of concept. It does not mean a demo, or a video of a demo, or a prototype you walk somebody through on a call. It means a thing somebody else can go and use, on their own, and get value from. It can be held together with tape. Nobody using it cares how it was built, and neither should you, yet.

I need to be honest about a tension here, because two things I have written pull against each other. The newsroom article behind this chapter says the shipped thing "can be vibe coded". The frame of this book, in Explorer coding, not vibe coding, and the handover, is that the explorer builds to find out, and that what the exploration produces is the brief for the version other people will rely on. Both are true, and the gap between them is the point of this chapter. The first thing you put in a stranger's hands can be explorer code. What cannot be explorer code is the thing people come to depend on. The test below is how you find out which of the two you are holding, and it is cheaper than guessing.

To ship that often you need the running cost to be near nothing, so build accordingly: serverless, as few databases and as little live state as you can stand. Serverless, for real is the long version. And do not underestimate the plumbing. Getting a working thing into a stranger's hands is still, in 2026, genuinely hard. "Solving that is not overhead, it is the first real product you build." Everything after it gets cheaper.

Give it away, then take it away

Now hand it to people for free. Not forever: a short window, whatever unit makes sense. You want the feedback loop tight, and you want the cost of generosity inside what you can absorb.

Just doing this solves dozens of problems you did not know you had. How people get access. What breaks when somebody unfamiliar touches it. What they try first. What they never find. "The feedback starts the moment somebody who is not you has the thing."

Then comes the part almost nobody does deliberately. Take it away. The trial expired. It is not available right now. Nothing else about the product changes, and no apology is needed. And ask the only question that matters.

Do they miss it?

If they shrug and go back to their day, you do not have a product yet. You have something people will accept when it is free, which is a much weaker signal than it feels like from the inside. No sense of loss means no why now, and without a why now there is nothing to sell. If they come back and ask for it, you have something. That is the moment the whole model turns on, and it is worth engineering your trial so that this moment arrives early and unambiguously.

Then two questions, and people answer only the first. Is the price one they are happy to pay? And is that price profitable for you? If you cannot get revenue at all, that is the problem to solve, and it is not a marketing problem. And one trap: do not reach for a subscription by default. Charging rent for something people are not using is a worse business than being paid when you deliver. Price it, then give it away takes the next step, which is defining the thing, pricing it, and offering it free to the people who already know you.

Five days, ten customers, twenty-two minutes

Here is what the loop looks like with dates, from my own repositories. In October 2026 I asked this site's agent to check my memory of SG/Send against its briefs and commits, because I knew the order I remembered would be wrong in places, and it was.

SG/Send started because sending a file securely to someone was harder than it should be, and because I wanted a service where the server holds nothing: the best way to protect an asset is not to have it. The first commit was on 8 February 2026, with a specification that set the rule everything since has followed: "files are encrypted on the client before upload, and the decryption key is never sent to the server." On 13 February, send.sgraph.ai was live. Five days.

Then I took it to market, end to end, which is where I learnt Stripe, pricing and everything else a real product needs; by the time I built the newsroom I already knew how, which is how exploring something early pays off later, in a way nobody plans. The Stripe checkout went live on 1 March. On 2 March there were ten paying customers, recruited with a simple offer: buy £5 of credits and I would send a £5 Amazon voucher. "Several declined the Amazon voucher." A number I used to say, thirty paying customers, appears only in an investor one-pager and could not be verified; ten is the recorded number, and ten is what the book says.

The first feature request came the same day. A user wanted to send a folder, not a file. At 20:26 the proposal for folder upload as a client-side zip was committed; at 20:48 the implementation; at 22:15 a viewer for the zip on the download page. The debrief the next morning: "First feature request (folder upload) shipped in 90 minutes." That is what a tight loop feels like from the inside.

The pace behind it: 4,476 commits on the SG/Send app on 246 of its first 249 days, 739 release tags from v0.1.0 to v0.34.0, and the command line's unit tests grew from 237 on 5 March to 4,232 on 14 October.

What the market said

The technology worked. The use case did not land, and the briefs said so three days after the first customers. On 5 March: "We have paying customers but not enough usage. Users buy credits, test once, and don't come back. Credits sit unused."

Read that against the test above. People took it when it was free, and some paid when asked by someone they knew. Then nobody ran out of credits and came back for more, and nobody came back asking for the next feature. That silence was the signal. It was the miss-it test, failed, and I had the numbers to see it. I should say that the usage is described, not counted: the briefs say the credits sat unused, and no usage figures were found.

It was a platform without a workflow that needed it. The difference now is that I have the workflows: the newsroom, RiskMandate and the vaults themselves need exactly the features SG/Send built, which is why the same features are used every day. Explorers find things early; the use case decides when they matter. If you build something that works and nobody uses yet, that is not a verdict on the thing. It is a verdict on the why now, and a why now can arrive later, from a direction you did not plan.

The test applies to you

A strong connection with users is critical, and everybody says so. What is said less often is who the most important users are: the people building the product, and above all the people who control its roadmap. If the developers, the product owner and the founder use it every day on real work, the loop between using it and changing it is as short as it can be. They feel the friction first. They find the uses nobody planned. They know, without a survey, which of the hundred possible next features is the one that hurts. I think this is one of the reasons Apple is so good at what it does, as far as I can see from the outside: the people there use what they make.

This is brutal to say about my own work, and it is true. My last startups were good ideas. The Cyber Boardroom, MyFeeds, and early on even sgit as a startup. I still think they are good ideas. But I was not using them, and that was the signal I should have listened to. If I have access, I can change it, I can do a lot with it, and I still do not use it, then something is missing, and it is not the code. Why those companies did not take off has more than one reason; not using them is the one I can see most clearly and the one I can act on.

The vaults are the opposite story. I create them for almost everything, my agents create them, we share them, and 54 are published on sgit.ai with their read keys. They are getting really good, and it is not because of a roadmap. It is because every day I hit something, and the next release fixes it. One user is not a market. But I have seen the other pattern in startups many times: a product whose own team uses something else, or uses it only for the demo. Nothing in the code says so. The behaviour of the team does, and it usually means the product is not as good as it looks, or not as simple to deploy, or valuable only in a narrow case.

So before you ask whether a stranger misses it, ask whether you would. If you have spent twenty-five years in your field and you would not pick up your own tool on Monday morning, you have your answer early and cheaply.

The loop is the moat

I said in Be a founder that the technology is not the moat. What is defensible is the data, the relationships, the distribution, or the speed at which you ship. Using your own product makes the last one concrete.

With agents, we can develop just about anything, in any direction. What we cannot generate is the sense of what to build next, for whom, and when to stop. Context and focus come from somewhere, and the most reliable place they come from is using the thing. When I say I miss a feature, that is not a complaint. It is the product telling me where it wants to go, through the only user whose day it fully controls. Direction is the scarce skill makes the general case; this is the founder's case.

A workflow I now have most days: somebody makes a comment, I write an article, a feature ships, and I use it. I put myself as a user in the middle of the loop, deliberately. In October 2026 a reading list made for one person went from brief to live in 81 minutes, and a link to a persona from brief to live in 17 hours; the times are from the git logs. Fast prototyping to production, and fast release, is what is hard to copy, much harder than any single feature. A competitor can take your code. They cannot take the loop.

For Dinis the five-day, ten-customer, 90-minute story is told here with the dates the research agents confirmed, and the thirty-customer figure is named as unverified. Do you want the SG/Send story in this chapter at this length, or kept short here and told in full in Your first price and your first ten customers?

In 2018 I asked what was stopping you, and said it was just you. The version for 2026 is shorter. Ship it. Give it away. Take it away. Ask. Then do whatever the answer tells you, including the thing you did not want to hear.

From the register: Ship it, give it away briefly, take it away, and ask whether they miss it, The miss-it test applies to the builders: if you can change it and still do not use it, something is missing, The speed of the loop from a comment to a feature is the moat, not the technology, SG/Send: first commit on 8 February, live on 13 February, Stripe on 1 March, ten paying customers on 2 March, a feature request shipped in 90 minutes, The technology worked and the use case did not: 'users buy credits, test once, and don't come back'. Answers: Be a founder.

19Price it, then give it away

Ship it, and see if they miss it ends on a question. Hand the thing to people for free, briefly, take it away, and ask whether they miss it. If they come back for it, you have something. That is the right test and it has a gap in it, which is what happens next. "Something" is not a product until it has a shape, a price and a way of being delivered that does not break you on the tenth customer.

This chapter is about that step, and about the part that is easy to leave out: free is never free for the other side. Everything in it is from experience, mine and the people I work with. It is not financial advice, and the prices in it are the ones I published, on the dates I say, not a rule for yours.

Four moves, in order

The step after "do they miss it" is four moves.

Define the product. Not the technology, the thing somebody receives. For RiskMandate the thing is an Agent Behaviour Policy for one agent, as an encrypted vault the customer holds the keys to, with the mandate corrected for their deployment. That sentence took weeks to get to, and it is the product. If you have spent twenty-five years in your field you will find this move harder than it looks, because you know too much about the problem and the sentence has to leave most of it out.

Price it, at a number that makes sense to you. Not a number from a competitor's page: a number you would be content to be paid. RiskMandate's ladder, as published on 2 October 2026, is four tiers: £10 for the pack downloaded, £50 for a working vault, £500 for the vault corrected for your situation, £1,500 for two sessions and a professional's signature. The first two are automated. The third is an email thread: we send prompts, the customer runs them, we build the vault and the policy from what comes back. The fourth adds people's time on both sides. A price is a statement of what you think the thing is worth, and you are about to test the statement.

Find a way to deliver it at a cost that grows a step at a time. This is the move that was not available a year ago and the reason to do this now. The agentic workflow means the marginal customer costs a little more, not a lot more. The vault is provisioned by an agent, the policy is drafted by an agent from the customer's prompts, a person corrects it. The cost of serving the twentieth customer is close to the cost of serving the second. That is linear, not exponential, and it is the condition under which you can afford the fourth move.

Then offer it, free, to the people who already know you. Early adopters, power users, past customers who paid for something before it was finished. RiskMandate's early access programme went live on 30 September 2026, invite only: twenty places, the £500 tier given away, to "people who already run an agent with real access: a mailbox, a code host, a workflow tool, a CRM". The ask of each member is "Twenty minutes for the prompts, and a reply; half an hour on a call." The page does not say the price, and it should not; the price is for the people who come after.

Two small rules for the offer. Do not make them apply. "Apply for a chance to be considered" asks for work before there is anything in hand, and it inverts the relationship: they are doing you a favour by using the thing. "We have twenty of these in the first round. Would you like one? If not, I will give it to someone else" is honest about scarcity and leaves nothing to do but say yes or no. And reward the people who were there early, and say that is why they are getting it. It is a thank-you that is also the best possible test group, because they know enough to tell you where it is wrong, and their silence means the most.

Free is not zero

Here is the thing I was careful about in the memo behind this, because it is easy to say in a way that sounds like a lecture. When you give something away, or when you say "this is worth five hundred pounds and I would like you to have it", you are not making a zero-cost offer. You are asking the other side to engage. To read the message. To think about whether it applies to them. To weigh it against everything else on their list. To find twenty minutes for the prompts and half an hour for a call. To fit a new thing into a team that already has a way of working.

None of that is zero, and all of it is paid by them. A price, oddly, can help here: it says the thing is serious and that you expect it to be used, which is a reason to find the twenty minutes. But whether the price is five hundred pounds or nothing, the attrition you place on the other side is real, and it is the first thing to measure. How long from the message to the first reply. How many never replied. How many started the prompts and stopped. How many asked a question the message should have answered.

Then the exercise is to cut it. The service you want is the one that costs you the least to provide and costs the customer the least to receive: no overhead on them, no overhead on deployment, no second tool to install, no account to create. Those are product decisions, and the programme is where you find out which ones you got wrong, while the people finding out for you are people who like you.

Be plain about the two outcomes, because one of them is a bad day and the exercise only works if you are ready for it. If people take the offer, run the prompts, read the vault, send corrections and ask what else there is, you have a product, and the next question is price: is £500 too low for what they got, does the £1,500 tier sell itself once someone has had the £500 one free. If people say it is interesting and then do not find the time, or say it does not fit how their team works, or start and stop because the vault was harder to open than the message implied, you do not have a product yet. You might have a technology, or an idea, or a document people would read if someone else did the work. "A commercial product is one people take when the only cost is their own attention." That is the drawing board, and the sooner you are at it the cheaper it is. Neither outcome is a marketing problem. If friendlies will not take it free, a bigger audience will not take it either.

Pay after the value

Now the shape of the price itself, and a bias I would like you to notice in yourself before you choose one.

Almost every time I buy something, I am buying future value. A subscription, a gym membership, a big security product on a good deal, even a one-off purchase I expect to use for years: I am betting that I will use it enough for the price of each use to fall to a number I accept. Often I lose the bet, and the cost of leaving keeps me paying. The research agrees. In a study of three gyms, members on a flat monthly fee of over $70 attended on average 4.3 times a month, paying more than $17 a visit when a ten-visit pass would have cost $10 a visit.

So do not reach for a subscription by default. A subscription is a discount for regular use, not rent on something somebody ignores. Where you can, turn the bet round: start the user with credit, charge for the share they actually use, let the balance go below zero, and ask for payment when the value is in view. By then the person can see what they received, and the payment is connected to it rather than to a hope.

The newsroom runs that experiment, and the reader at read/ on this site runs by the same rules. Everyone starts with £5 of credit. A new article read to the end costs 10p, an older one 5p, and only as far as you read, so the first screen costs a penny. Your balance can go below zero and stay there; nothing is blocked and nothing nags. When you want to, you top up £5, with no account and nothing that renews. I should say plainly that there are no results yet. The hypotheses were written before the first payment, and the review is on 6 December 2026; the gym study explains the psychology, not our numbers.

The counter-example I keep in mind is Wikipedia, which I value enormously. Its fundraising raised US$184.5 million in 2024 to 2025 from 7.7 million donors, an average of about ten dollars. But the ask arrives once a year, as a lump sum, at a moment when the value is not in view, for something that is worth a few pence an article. Imagine the same ask with your year of reading in front of you. A penny an article for four hundred articles is £4, and it is easier to give when you can see the four hundred.

There is a condition, and it is the one that decides whether this shape is for your company. It only works where there is a very large gap between the cost of serving and what you can charge. A static site on a CDN has that gap in the extreme: the cost of one more reader reading one more page is close to nothing, so even at 10p the margin is very high. A business with a real cost per unit cannot let balances run negative for ever. If your product is the £500 tier above, with a person's hours in it, the meter is the wrong shape; the ladder is the right one. Know which you are.

Three more things make a price fairer, and I think more successful. Fair by country: 10p in the UK is not 10p everywhere, and prices should follow local purchasing power, the way The Economist's Big Mac index compares currencies. Visible: a reader should see what the money pays for; in one field experiment at an amusement park, people allowed to pay what they wanted for a photo paid about 92 cents, and when half of what they paid went to charity, fewer bought but they paid an average of $5.33. And chosen, at more than one level: a verified fact, a follow-up, a briefing with every claim checked are things people pay for separately, because they need them.

Pence only work aggregated

One piece of arithmetic before you price anything in pence, because it caught me and it will catch you.

Stripe's standard UK card rate is 1.5% plus 20p. On a £20 payment that is 2.5%. On £5 it is 5.5%. On 50p it is 41.5%, and on 10p the fee is more than the payment. Below about £5, the fixed fee is the price.

So payments of pence only work aggregated: one charge a year that covers many things, or a platform that already holds a balance and settles in bulk. That is why the newsroom's meter prices a page at a few pence against £5 of credit settled as one balance, and never charges the card per page. Above £2 or £3 the difference is smaller than you would expect; it is below about £1 that aggregation decides whether the model exists at all.

And tie payment to a verified person who receives something, not to a count. When tea.xyz rewarded package popularity with tokens, one developer published nearly 14,000 packages. Anything you pay for by the number will be gamed by the number.

For Dinis the reading meter's review is on 6 December 2026, and the early access programme's stop rule says the founders set the number before the first message goes out. For the next candidate: what the review found, and what number you set.

Put the four moves together and the order is the lesson. Price it first, because the price is the claim. Give it away second, to people who know you, because that is the cheapest honest test of the claim. Measure what it cost them, and cut it. Then, and only then, worry about the shape of the payment, because the shape is a detail until somebody wants the thing enough to miss it.

From the register: The four moves after 'do they miss it': define it, price it, deliver it at a flat marginal cost, offer it free to the people who know you, Free is never free for the other side: measure the cost you place on them, then cut it, Pay after the value: credit first, a meter, a balance that can go below zero, and no subscription by default, Below about five pounds the card fee is the price, so pence payments only work aggregated. Answers: no 2018 file.

20Sell

The first edition had one question under every chapter: why should I hire you? The founder's version is the same question asked by a different person. Picture the one who signs: dry, fair, has read a thousand pitches, impressed by evidence and bored by claims, and carrying a risk register with their name on it. Why should they buy from you, this quarter, when everything they already have mostly works?

This chapter is about selling to that person, from my own experience of doing it. It is not sales training, and the prices in it are the ones I published on the dates given, not advice. It ends with the thing that person is about to do to you.

Answer the question already in the room

There are two groups of people. The ones who see how fast agents are moving, and the much larger group who do not. The second group will not ask for an agent risk tool, or for most of what a founder in this book will build, because they do not see the risk or the gap. So I do not think the way in is to sell the need. It is to answer a question somebody already has, with a deadline, in the language of the room.

For RiskMandate, five such questions. A contract, where legal and procurement are doing due diligence on a vendor's agent and need to know what it will reach and do with their data. A security questionnaire, where a startup's first serious enterprise customer asks which agents touch their data and how they are bounded. An insurance renewal, where the question is whether AI is excluded from cover (RiskMandate's own argument, not independently surveyed). The board's risk appetite, where the question is who can pull the plug, how fast, and what cannot be undone. And twenty minutes with the assistant you already run, where the list of what it can reach is the surprise. None of these asks anyone to understand agents first.

The same move works in any field. You already know which question lands on your buyer's desk with a date on it. Sell the answer to that, not the idea.

Sell value that lands the day they switch

At a digital sovereignty event in October 2026 I spent a day with an infrastructure operator with a large footprint in Romania, and the conversation kept returning to one point. The reason to move must never be sovereignty, and it must never be cost reduction. Both are important. Neither is urgent. They sit on the risk register under "important, not urgent" and stay there, because a migration always carries risk and nobody signs risk for a principle, or for a return that depends on a future event that might not happen and might not matter to the person deciding now.

The person signing needs value that lands the day they switch and gives them cover if it goes wrong: backups that exist, every object version controlled, encryption the operator cannot read with the key held by the customer, a journal of every call, behaviour policies the agents cannot skip, cost per workload visible before the invoice, and an item taken off the top of the register. "It is in Europe now" is not a decision somebody can defend. For an operator in Romania, Portugal or the UK, "move to us" sells risk; "keep your code, point it at us, and come out with policies, journals, encryption and cost visibility" sells value with the risk removed. The article admits its value list is still a little invisible to a signer; sharpening it is the work.

The general lesson is the one I would print on the wall. Find the return that closes before the risk does, and find the buyer who owns that register line rather than the one who owns the bill. And remember that the buyer's inertia is real and is not stupidity. They have the same AI you have; what they do not have is a reason to move this quarter. You cannot sell against inertia with technology. You sell the thing that is urgent.

Sell a service before the product

Most of what this book calls shipping ends up, at first, as a service with a person in it. That is fine, and it is sellable, as long as each step is small enough to say yes to and each ends with something the client keeps.

After a talk in London on 15 October 2026, built and run by a governed agent hive, I wrote down the path we would sell. A conversation, free, about what the client wants agents to do and what they must never do. A first run on a real problem for £10 to £50, which leaves a vault. Two hours of expert time for £500 to steer it, or a day for £1,500 to write the behaviour policy for one team. A four-day pilot in their own environment for £5,000. Then a maintained setup from £149 a month, so they run it themselves. The boutiques selling the same kind of thing have the same shape everywhere: a free call, a paid discovery (a one-week audit at £2,500 in London, a 14-day sprint at €2,900 in Germany, a two-week assessment at $4,999), a fixed-price pilot from £8,000, a build, a retainer. The plan is honest about where it stands: no paid run yet, and the prices are hypotheses to test with the first ten customers.

The market is crowded at the top and thin on evidence. The large consultancies each have an agent platform, the model vendors sell through partners and their own engineers, and Gartner expects over 40% of agentic AI projects to be cancelled by the end of 2027, citing "escalating costs, unclear business value or inadequate risk controls", with a warning about "agent washing". The people in between, curious, backed from the top, struggling to make agents work or to show anyone that they do, lack not another platform but evidence they can inspect. So the position for a small seller is evidence first: every engagement leaves a vault the client keeps, with the work, the history and a ledger of what each agent did and was allowed to do. The demonstration is the sales process, and the demonstration is checkable. The talk itself left 57 vault commits from 28 workers; and, honestly, the runs were not gated by a compiled behaviour policy, which was shown separately.

A marketplace of people

A service only scales with people. An agentic engagement needs someone who knows the client's world and can steer a hive in it, and there are far more of those people in a community than hours in a founder's week. So alongside the offers, a small curated list of consultants who deliver on the same stack, where the client picks the kind of project first and then the people. Mondweep Chakravorty, co-founder of the Agentics Foundation's London chapter, and I are the first two, and we are not taking bookings yet; nothing is built, and the platform share is a proposal.

Joining copies the markets that work: apply, show proof of work (a public vault or repository of a real run, with its ledger), peer review by two members, a thirty to forty-five minute interview with a scenario, a first engagement shadowed, and standards, including a behaviour policy for consultants, the same idea we apply to agents. Toptal says fewer than 3% of applicants pass its five stages. The consultant sets the price within the band and keeps most of it; the markets sit between 5% and 20% in total (Malt charges freelancers 5%, Upwork 0% to 15%, Braintrust 15% to the client). If you are going into a job rather than founding one, this is a door too: join as the person who delivers, with a vault of a real run as your CV.

What the buyer is about to do to you

Now turn the chapter round, because the person who signs is about to get a new instrument, and you will be on the receiving end of it before long.

Anyone who has sold software to a large organisation has met the spreadsheet. The standard questionnaire has 627 questions in its core form; the average vendor answers 37.3 assessment requests a month and spends 179 hours a month on them.
