Each answer is true, in the sense that there is a document somewhere with the right title, and each is written by the people being asked. Due diligence was a form because the alternative did not exist.

It can now be an agent. A buyer can send a prompt, or a small agent under a behaviour policy, to run inside the vendor's environment and come back with a graph of what is actually there: what reaches production that no person reviewed, whether the documentation matches the code, whether there is a threat model and who wrote it, what the last fifty bugs touched, which agents touch the pipeline and under what policy. The vendor reads every file before it leaves and can withhold any of them. What it cannot do is show a map that says there is no unreviewed code when there is some, because the map is read from the repository, and a false map is a false statement on a record, which is the thing companies will not do. "The questionnaire gave the vendor the pen. The agent takes the pen away and leaves the vendor the redaction marker." And the buyer holds the power to ask: "A buyer asking costs nothing. A supplier not answering looks worse than a supplier answering badly." Investors and acquirers get the same visibility in the same month. The agent does not exist yet; this is the case for it. The regulation arrives first: the EU's revised Product Liability Directive applies to products placed on the market from 9 December 2026, and presumes a product defective where the manufacturer fails to disclose relevant evidence.

The test is risk-based, not pure, and that is why this is good news for you. A startup that says we generated most of this, we have limited resources, this is not mission-critical, here are the mitigations, has passed, for a product sold as what it is. The same sentence from a vendor whose software holds my customers' data is the finding. What fails is not knowing.

So if I were a startup I would double down on understandability, for two reasons. The startup can play the same game as the biggest buyer: the tools that build the graphs are open source, so build the graph of your own repository, keep the threat model with the code, write a behaviour policy for every agent, trace your bugs to your changes, and answer the buyer's questions in the buyer's format before being asked. A threat model is the fastest read of a team's understanding there is; one that says we have a weakness here, exploiting it needs a nation-state, and we will not defend against it, is a good threat model. And the startup has less code. "Understanding ten thousand lines is a week; understanding ten million that nobody still at the company wrote is a programme." The incumbent's advantage was always that nobody could check. The startup's advantage is that it can be checked and pass.

One last thing a buyer will do, and you should do first: check your answers against your own site. My co-founder came back from a conference with ten hard questions, the ones serious buyers, investors and competitors ask about a product like RiskMandate. I answered them in a two-hour structured interview with an agent acting as a journalist with an eye for detail, challenging every answer until the set held together, and we published an honest maturity table of what is live and what is design.

Then we checked the answers against what we had already published, and found three contradictions on our own site, still there on 9 October 2026: a homepage saying "enforced in real time" when we do not enforce, an insurance page in the present tense for things that were design, and a Level 1 price of £10 on two pages and £5 on a third. A buyer with an agent would have found all three in a minute. The line that closes the set is the one to sell on: "Where we are early, we say so."

For Dinis the marketplace article names Mondweep Chakravorty as the first consultant beside you, and says neither of you is taking bookings. Does the book name him, as here, and does this chapter wait for the first paid engagement before it says what the prices turned out to be?

The person who signs is not your enemy. They are the first customer, and the first customer decides whether a company exists. Give them a question they already have, a return that lands the day they say yes, something they keep at every step, and a record they can check without trusting you. Then let them send the agent.

From the register: Send an agent, not a spreadsheet: due diligence derived from the code, not composed by the vendor, A startup should double down on understandability: less code, the same tools, and it can be checked and pass, Selling a service: each step small enough to say yes to, and each ends with something they keep, The market is crowded at the top and thin on evidence: sell the record the client keeps, A service only scales with people: a curated marketplace of consultants, joined by proof of work, The reason to move is never sovereignty or cost: sell value that lands the day they switch, Answer the hard questions in full, publish what is live and what is design, and check your own site against the answers, Do not sell the need; answer a question somebody already has to answer, with a deadline. Answers: no 2018 file.

21Fund it, or do not

The analogy I keep coming back to is music. Do not get signed before you have the album. Write the songs, record them in a bedroom studio, play locally, get people buying. Then take the record deal, and take it as somebody who already has an audience and does not strictly need one.

That is the whole of the second pillar from Ship it, and see if they miss it: the investors should be calling you, not the other way round. This chapter says why, what I think investors are choosing when they choose a founder, what a founder who has already won could offer instead of the usual round, and what breaks. It touches money, shares and tax, so I will say it once and mean it throughout: this is from experience and from the research I cite, not legal, tax or investment advice; the UK figures are for one tax year and other countries differ; and I am a founder whose companies run on open source, so this argument suits me.

The worst time to raise

Here is the claim, stated plainly. The worst possible time to raise money is before you are profitable, before you have the product, before you understand the fit. Which is, of course, exactly when most companies raise.

Everything follows from the negotiating position. You are bargaining from weakness and both sides know it: you have no product and you have a clock. Profitability does not just mean money. It means time. When you are not bleeding, you can wait, and being able to wait is most of what a good negotiating position is.

So the terms are bad, and the worst of them are not about money. They are about control. You hand a say in the business to people whose job is a return and who have not lived the vision. Their focus is now structurally misaligned with yours, and their opinion has been given weight. I think that is one of the most common ways companies fail, and it is self-inflicted at the moment of signing.

With agents making execution cheap, getting to profit before you need anyone's money is more possible than it has ever been. So the pillar is not "never raise". It is: "Raise from strength, on terms you set, from people who came to you because the thing already works."

We study survivors

Before the evidence on investors, a warning about evidence on founders, including the stories in this book.

There is a whole genre of business writing that goes: look at these successful companies, here is what they did, do the same. What it almost never does is count the people who made exactly the same moves and did not make it, or were never funded. Jerker Denrell put this on a formal footing in 2003: "risky practices, even if they are unrelated to performance in the full population of organizations, may seem to be positively related to performance in a sample of survivors." So the question that matters is not what the winners did. It is why that one was chosen, funded and backed, when others who did the same were not.

Timing and place are part of the answer. In the MusicLab experiment, 14,341 people downloaded unknown songs, and showing them what others had downloaded made success more unequal and less predictable: "The best songs rarely did poorly, and the worst rarely did well, but any other result was possible." And the ten largest computer-science clusters hold 69% of US inventors in the field. Being in the room is an advantage the success stories rarely list, including mine.

Who investors choose, and why

Take two founders with the same opportunity, the same drive, the same idea and the same talent. Why does the investor pick one and not the other?

My hypothesis is that one of the deciding factors is control: which founder the investor believes will take direction, accept decisions made in the investor's interest, and step aside if asked. The relationship is financial, not emotional; the founder is there to deliver a return, and is, in the end, replaceable. In Wardley terms the investor moves the founder towards commodity, as record labels did to performers once the songs were the asset.

Nobody has tested that directly, as far as I could find. But a well-studied trait sits very close to it: coachability. Investors say openly that they look for it, and the researchers who reviewed the evidence in 2023 wrote: "While investors and support organizations such as accelerators prefer coachable entrepreneurs, being coachable doesn't necessarily make your firm more successful." If investors reliably select on a trait that does not reliably predict success, something other than success is being selected for.

Control is in the paperwork. Kaplan and Strömberg read 213 venture investments in 119 companies and found that "if the firm performs poorly, the VCs obtain full control. As performance improves, the entrepreneur retains/obtains more control rights. If the firm performs very well, the VCs retain their cash flow rights, but relinquish most of their control and liquidation rights." They also found non-compete and vesting provisions that make it more expensive for the founder to leave. And the replaceability gets used: in Wasserman's study of 212 US startups, by year three "50% of founders were no longer the CEO", and "fewer than 25% led their companies' initial public offerings". Those are startups of the late 1990s and early 2000s.

Now the part where my own evidence cuts against me. Investors have a serious reply: control creates value. Wasserman's later study of 6,130 US startups found that each additional level of founder control, over the board or the CEO position, reduced the pre-money valuation by 17.1 to 22.0 percent, and Ewens and Marx argue that replacing founders improves exits. I accept the numbers. What I do not accept is that they settle the question, because both measure value as the investor measures it, at the valuation and the exit, not at the mission. Over a longer run Bain found founder-led companies in the S&P 500 performed 3.1 times better in shareholder returns from 1990 to 2014; that is Bain's own analysis, not peer-reviewed. And there is a selection effect inside the investors' evidence: if they fund the coachable founders in the first place, the strong-minded ones who walked away or bootstrapped are mostly not in the sample. Denrell again.

If the hypothesis holds, the founder with a strong vision, a clear direction and strong ethics is at a disadvantage in early funding, not because they build worse companies but because an investor reads them, correctly, as someone who will not easily be overruled. That is the expert founder of Be a founder: the people who know the problem best are the hardest to steer. The practical conclusion is not to avoid investors. It is to get to the big stick before you need their money, so that the choice of investor is yours.

Europe's money, oddly, helps here. It is said that American investors back the vision while European investors want to see it working, and one vendor's aggregated figures have a version of it: European companies raise at higher revenue (a median band of $1 million to $5 million against $100,000 to $1 million in the US), at lower pre-money valuations ($6.54 million against $14.64 million), with funding targets 46% smaller. That is weakly evidenced, but it fits what founders say. I think that scepticism is what an expert founder needs, because money too soon is one of the worst things that can happen to a startup: a study of about 3,200 high-growth internet startups found 74% failed through premature scaling, and that "Many investors invest 2-3x more capital than necessary in startups in the discovery phase." The gap is real at the other end: the EU's share of global venture funds raised is 5% against 52% in the US, and close to 30% of the unicorns founded in Europe between 2008 and 2021 moved abroad. Europe is weak at scaling. My argument is that the first part, the part that now matters most, is where it is strong, and its values fit: an open source strategy in the Commission's June 2026 sovereignty package, and impact at 15% of European venture funding against 7% in North America.

Sell, don't issue

Suppose you did it the hard way. You have paying customers, profit and growth, built cheaply with agents, and more than one investor wants in. The negotiation has flipped: you do not need the money to survive, and the investor needs access to the deal.

My answer has two parts. First, sell, don't issue. In a normal round the company issues new shares and the money goes into the company. Instead, the investor buys ordinary shares you already own, in a private sale. You are paid now, for years of underpaid work; the company is not diluted; and nothing in the deal requires preferred shares, a board seat, a veto or a liquidation preference. The investor buys the vision and the direction, not the steering wheel.

Second, lend, don't dilute. If the company needs cash, lend it part of the proceeds, subordinated by contract to every other creditor, at a modest fixed rate or none, repaid if the company succeeds. The company gets money without issuing a share; you keep your ownership; nobody outside gains control.

The investor's obvious objection is: what if the founder goes rogue? The answer is the third pillar. With everything open source, the investor's insurance is not control but the fork: if you take the company somewhere they will not follow, they can take the code and back a better service. The fork is slow both ways: by my estimate an established company holding a full copy needs three months at best and closer to a year to catch up. It disciplines the founder because it is possible, and protects the founder because it is slow. That is an argument, not a tested mechanism; Open source has the longer version.

A worked example, illustrative only: a UK company worth £10 million, a founder who owns all of it, an investor writing £2 million. The founder keeps 80% either way. In this model the investor buys 20% of the founder's shares; the founder pays about £420,000 in tax (18% on the first £1 million under Business Asset Disposal Relief from 6 April 2026, if its conditions are met, and 24% above), has about £1.58 million, lends £0.75 million back and keeps about £0.83 million; the buyer pays 0.5% stamp duty. I think the structure raises the odds of success, not only the founder's comfort, because a founder lending their own money watches every penny, and premature scaling is how 74% of those high-growth startups died. No study tests this model; the claim is from discipline and incentives, not data.

What breaks

A founder who uses this should know its costs before the term sheet does.

UK angels get EIS relief of 30% and SEIS of 50% only on newly issued shares, so bought shares are that much dearer to them; expect a lower price, or a hybrid with some new shares. Ordinary shares are often priced 10% to 30% below the last round, though competition shrinks the discount. A large early sale can read as a lack of conviction: Balderton writes that it would not expect a founder to sell more than 10% at growth stage and has walked away from deals over the size of a secondary. A founder who sells and lends back has moved part of their risk ahead of the new investors unless the loan is subordinated in writing. Control can return through a shareholders' agreement, and the model only holds if you refuse those terms.

And refuse a broad non-compete. The fork protects both sides; a broad non-compete keeps it for the investors and takes it from you. Offer confidentiality, no poaching of staff or customers for a period, and no use of the brand instead. The law will not do this for you: the UK's 2023 plan to cap non-competes at three months was never legislated. Four more points for a lawyer are in the article, from transfer restrictions to the order of issue and sale in a hybrid.

The model fits a profitable company growing on its own revenue, with competition for the deal, open code and customers who stay for the service. It fits badly where the company must spend heavily ahead of revenue, with angels who need EIS, where the value is proprietary code (no fork), and with funds whose model depends on control.

For Dinis the controllable-founder article proposes three ways to test the hypothesis: follow the coachability ratings for ten years, compare the mission rather than the multiple, and count the founders who refused term sheets. Does the book carry the test, so a reader with data can run it, or only the hypothesis and its counter-evidence?

What matters is not the exact mix of shares and loans. It is that you set the terms, and that no part of the money buys the steering wheel. Do not get signed before you have the album.

From the register: Fund it, or do not: the worst time to raise is before you are profitable, and the best is when they call you, We study survivors: a risky practice can look like the cause of success when only winners are counted, Investors select for the founder they can steer; control is written into the contract, and founders get replaced, Sell, don't issue: the investor buys the founder's shares, the founder lends the company what it needs, and nobody buys the steering wheel, What breaks: EIS is lost on bought shares, a big early sale reads as doubt, the founder-lender is a creditor, and a broad non-compete closes the fork, Europe's slower, sceptical money suits a founder who can build first. Answers: no 2018 file.

22The company itself: the UK, Portugal, Lithuania, the European Union

This chapter is a placeholder, and it says so. By the end of this book you are meant to have started a company, so this is where the book should say how to actually do it, for real: the steps to create a limited company in the UK; the case for Lithuania; Portugal, and what it costs to run a small company there; the European Union and what exists in it for a founder; the cloud providers and the startup programmes as they stand this year. All of it from experience, mine and the people I work with, and none of it legal or tax advice. The material is not written yet. The newsroom has no article on any of it, and the journalist is waiting on memos of mine. Here is what the sources already give.

Two lines on Portugal and Lithuania

Everything the newsroom has on the two countries, as of 12 October 2026, is in one paragraph of Not a straight line, and the book should not say more than this from that source. The company I created in Portugal for MyFeeds is being closed, because running a company with little revenue in Portugal is expensive in a way it is not in England. I am looking at Lithuania for the next one, for its digital advantages. The article's own limit stands here too: the company move is a consideration, not a decision; Lithuania is being looked at, and nothing has been registered.

The UK and the European Union are, for now, questions: creating a limited company in the UK, from experience, and what exists in the EU for a founder. Neither is written.

What spending millions on a distributed workforce taught me

One piece does exist, on how a small company hires. As a CISO and a CTO I used Upwork a great deal; across the open source projects and the teams I ran we spent millions on it, and it was one of the most successful things we did. We built distributed teams, hired people as good as, and often better than, those we could hire the ordinary way, and delivered faster than was possible before. Those numbers are mine and are not independently verifiable from the article.

It worked because we invested first in the infrastructure around it: a project management team, a structure team and an onboarding team, and then Upwork to manage Upwork, so the internal teams carried no overhead. People said hiring that way was buying the cheapest. It never was. It was the right talent at a price fair where they lived, good value for both sides, and a real career for the person.

The second lesson is where the jobs go. A good job today means moving to a capital city and spending most of what you earn on being there, when what most people want is to go back to where the family and the lifestyle are. A small country that builds the plumbing, forming a company, hiring across a border, being paid across Europe, makes the jobs available everywhere inside it. Romania's technology sector is close to 8% of GDP, with more than 250,000 professionals and over ten billion euros a year of service exports. The talent is not the constraint; the plumbing is, and Civic responsibility takes the small countries from here. For the founder, the lesson is practical: the team can be anywhere, if you build the structure before the first hire.

For Dinis the UK memo (creating a limited company, from your experience), the Lithuania memo and the Portugal memo are the three the journalist needs before this chapter can say the steps. Your own open question stands: Lithuania and the EU, written now from what you know, or after you have done it?

For Dinis: the EU memo (the programmes and what exists for a founder), and whether this chapter or The credits and programmes, 2026 carries the cloud providers and startup programmes, so the two do not repeat each other.

Until the memos arrive, the honest version of this chapter is short. The book will say how when I have said how.

Part 7

Build it without a server

Build it so that there is nothing to run at three in the morning. Serverless, for real; privacy by design, with data encrypted on the user's device on a server that cannot read it; control of the agents your company runs on; open source and who pays for it; and the only definition of shipped that counts: the user has it.

23Serverless, for real

The first edition had a draft on the cloud that warned it "is as complex as an OS". True, and the wrong thing to tell you. You do not need to learn the cloud. You need to know what the blocks are, which are free, and how to have an agent click them together once. I am technical, and I could learn any of these platforms. I do not want to. And a founder is also the person who gets woken up when it breaks. So this chapter has a rule before it has a map. Ship your company serverless. A founder with no servers has nothing to run at three in the morning and nothing to pay for when nobody is using it.

The by-the-hour test

The 2018 outline on serverless said the word does not mean no servers, and that it was important to understand the limitations. To me it always meant four things: it scales to zero, it costs little or nothing when it is not there, it starts fast, and it turns a capability into a commodity. The CNCF's whitepaper says "there is no charge when code is not running", and the Berkeley paper warned in 2019 that few so-called elastic services could scale to zero.

The word has been stretched since. In October 2026 I read the pricing pages of the products sold under the name and asked each one question: what does it cost when nobody is using it? Excluding storage, on 18 October 2026:

Product Idle How it bills
Lambda event functions Zero Per request and per millisecond
Cloud Run Zero Per 100 ms; idle instances not charged
Cosmos DB serverless Zero Per request unit, no minimum
Neon Zero Compute suspends when idle
Redshift Serverless Zero Per second while running
Aurora Serverless v2 Zero since November 2024 Pauses after a set idle time
OpenSearch, next generation Zero since May 2026 Scales to zero after 10 idle minutes
OpenSearch, classic About $350 a month A floor of 2 capacity units
MSK Serverless About $550 a month $0.75 per cluster-hour
Neptune Serverless About $80 a month A floor of 1 capacity unit
ElastiCache Serverless From $6 a month A minimum of storage, metered hourly

The monthly figures are my arithmetic at 730 hours and US East rates. The line between the two halves is the test: if it bills you by the hour when nobody is using it, it is not serverless, and whoever runs it has something running on their side. The table is dated because floors fall, and Ship a serverless product in a weekend renews it each year. The test does not date, and a founder needs it because of the line in The question is whether they miss it: "Not to plan, not to pitch, not to architect. Ship." To ship that often the running cost has to be near nothing.

The glue behind the commodity

I would rather replace the word than keep defending it. Three words each carry a part: ephemeral, because it comes and goes with use and costs nothing when gone; town planner, Simon Wardley's word for something mature and industrialised, a commodity; and services, because it is exposed for others to use. Ephemeral town planner services. Lambda is one, sitting on town planner services like S3, which are always there. The name is a proposal, not a prediction.

Naming things this way makes you ask whether something is really a commodity or only looks like one. Plenty of things are treated as commodities while, behind the scenes, they are a large amount of custom technology glued together, which is what a Wardley map shows. Lambda is the documented case. In its builders' own words, "we chose to use Linux containers to isolate functions" and "We were unsatisfied with this approach"; from 2018 they moved customers to Firecracker. For four years, the definition of serverless was containers inside rented machines, glued together, while it matured with its customers. That is how services become commodities, and your first version will be the same, a villager's work dressed as a town planner's. But a per-hour bill for a "serverless" product usually means the provider cannot yet spin the thing up and down fast and safely enough to bill by use: an engineering gap on their side, passed to you as a cost. Build the villager's version. Just do not bill by the hour and call it serverless.

Cloud as Lego

In October 2026 I mapped the developer platform of the company I keep my domains with, Cloudflare, as the smallest blocks a non-specialist could put together: static files served free, small pieces of code at the edge, object storage that can be pinned to the EU, hosted models with a promise not to train on what you send, inbound email, a sign-in gate. Cloudflare as Lego blocks has the limits as the pages gave them on 18 October 2026, and they change. The blocks are more useful as packages, each the smallest set for one job: a site on your domain; your vault, hosted; an assistant over your documents; an inbox that does things; a form or a small API.

A read-only vault needs no code at all: a published folder of encrypted files that any static host can serve. A read-write vault needs one function of 83 lines, one storage bucket and one tiny coordinator per vault, run against the real client with no plaintext anywhere on the server. I should be straight about those 83 lines, because the rule of this chapter is no servers of mine with logic in them, and 83 lines of mine, running on someone else's platform, is logic. They store encrypted objects, check a hash of the write key, keep two pushes from overwriting each other, and cannot read a byte. That is the honest version of the rule: not that there is no code on the server side, but that whatever is there holds nothing, knows nothing, and can be replaced by any other store that does the same job.

The catch is setup, and it is in your favour. The platform's API cannot be called from a web page, so the one-time setup is a deploy button or an agent with a terminal that signs in, runs the commands and hands back the URL. It happens once; Your first workspace is where you learn to ask for it. The 2018 chapter on Docker had the same instinct: stop clicking buttons and write the environment down as code.

Three smaller blocks. The one you will use most is a model, and the question is who pays; on 18 October 2026 four free offers let anyone start without a card and let a web page call them directly, so the key goes from your browser to the provider and nowhere else (Free models, your own key). Free means they may learn from it: fine for what is already public, never for your customers' data. Git: the 2018 outline said "use Git everyday", and what I would add is why Git won. Not because of GitHub, but because it is a standard that any tool, any host and any person can read; sgit, in the next chapter, is the same kind of thing with the trust in a different place. If what a tool stores is not a format other tools can read, you are renting your own history. And the bill: models are paid for by the token, and the person who sees least is the user. A hook of 132 lines shows under each answer what a session read, how much came from the cache and what it cost; the session that wrote The tokens hook had read 99% from the cache, the difference at list prices between about $170 and about $1,950. You need the box under the answer, and the habit of looking at it.

For Dinis what a founder on their first product should expect to pay in a month, for one site, one vault and a model on a free tier with their own key; the register says costs for a newcomer is still a gap, and only you have the receipts.

The file system is the database

Here is a rule I have held for years: you do not need a database. The file system is the database. A database server is a large, stateful, always-on thing you have to provision, secure, back up and pay for whether or not anyone is using it; it fails the by-the-hour test before you have a customer. Load the state, work on it in memory, save it back. I call the pattern LETS, Load, Extract, Transform, Save: a cache, a database and an audit trail in one, with Git as its history.

If you run a business on a shared spreadsheet you already know this; what you lacked was a way to query the file, and now the newsroom runs two real database engines inside the browser, over the JSON files its pages are built from. The database is the files; the engines are just readers. The 2018 outline on micro-services worried about a constellation of services nobody understands. Files have the opposite property: every piece of state is a thing you can open.

No server, by design

The second step crept up on me over months, and the newsroom made it explicit: retire the back end too. The newsroom keeps personas, charges by the share of a page read, takes money and runs SQL over its own data, and none of that logic runs on a server. What is left is commodity: static hosting, a vault server that stores only ciphertext, a payment provider's hosted page, DNS and a build pipeline. I am not saying there are no servers. It is no servers of mine with logic in them.

I did not choose this architecture and then add encryption. Encryption came first, and the client-side design followed, because the vault server cannot read a byte, so anything that reads the user's data has to run where the key is: in the browser. A server that cannot read has nothing to compute, so there is no server code to deploy or patch.

Here is what no server buys a company, from a product we built in August 2026 that turns a voice note into a transcript and a debrief, in the browser, on the user's own key, with nothing stored. We never see the audio. That one fact removes, at a stroke: data custody and its governance; a place where a breach can happen; a billing system; a database of users; a thing that has to scale; a thing that has to be up at three in the morning, and a person to wake. Add a server and every item comes back. "That is not one feature; it is a different company, with a different cost base and a different risk register." No server is the product, not a preference. Its first three weeks, 239 commits and 27 releases, are what moving fast looks like, because the complexity of the moving parts is what decides how fast you can move.

There is one honest limit, and a founder meets it first. Anything the user must not hold cannot live in the user's browser. A static site has no place to keep a secret, so a chat box on one cannot hold an API key; the honest answers are the reader's own key, or a bridge through a vault. The voice product met the same wall as a billing question: a capped key per paying user, metered by the provider, was the plan, and the provider's terms, not the architecture, stopped it. The route left, a metering proxy, is the smallest possible backend and still a backend: the audio passes through it, and custody, scaling and on-call all return. Servers are not bad; a bank's ledger needs shared, live state. The pattern is for products where each user's state is theirs, which is most first products, and the next chapter is about the key that makes it work.

The code must not know it moved

One more thing a founder in Europe will be asked, usually by the first serious customer: where is the data, and could you move it? At a digital sovereignty event in October 2026, one conversation kept returning to the same point. What stops workloads moving is not policy or price. It is code. Every call to S3, EC2 and IAM is written into applications that work today and that nobody wants to touch. A hyperscaler's European cloud does not answer it: your code still talks to one vendor's API under one vendor's terms. Sovereignty that depends on one company's API is sovereignty by permission.

So the requirement is that the application must not know it has moved. Not one line changes. The same calls land on a twin: an open source interface to a real system, valued for one thing, that the client cannot tell the difference. The twin decides where the bytes go: real S3, an sgit vault, a European operator's data centre, a laptop. Switching a mock costs a code path; switching a twin costs an endpoint, and if it costs an endpoint, migration is a configuration decision rather than a project, and projects get cancelled. The S3 twin exists, as open source; EC2 and IAM do not yet. And none of it works if the twin layer is closed: swap one vendor's API for another vendor's wrapper and the lock-in has moved, not gone.

For a company with no servers of its own this is cheap to honour from day one: your product's state is files in a vault whose key the user holds, so make where the files live a setting, and the customer's question has a one-word answer. Shipped means the user has it takes the same twin into testing, where it may matter even more.

From the register: Building blocks: cloud as Lego, free models with your own key, git as a standard, The file system is the database: load, extract, transform, save, and keep every intermediate as a file, No server, by design: retire the back end too, and keep only commodity servers, Ship now, and ship your startup serverless: no servers of yours, no database, client side, with privacy by design and control of your agents, What serverless costs when nobody is using it, product by product, 18 October 2026, The name shows the glue: a first version is often a villager's work dressed as a town planner's, Move the cloud, not the code: a twin the application cannot tell from the real service. Answers: AWS, Serverless, Micro-Services, Git, Docker.

24Privacy by design: SG/Send, sgit, and the value of constraints

There is a category of file with nowhere good to live. A risk register with real system names in it. An analysis that is still being argued about. The working memory of an agent that has read your codebase for a month. Client material under NDA. If you have run anything in a regulated field, you know the shape: it wants history, branches, diffs and a link you can send to one person, and it is the reason people say "we can't put that in GitHub". So it ends up in a shared drive, or in a thread nobody can diff. A founder meets this on the first day, because the first customer's data is exactly this kind of file. The first edition had a whole part on security, and it was mostly bullets. This chapter is one of the ones I would have written.

Git for things you cannot put on GitHub

sgit is git for those files. Everything is encrypted before it leaves your machine, versioned like git, and stored on a server that holds ciphertext and nothing else: not your filenames, not your directory structure, not your commit messages. There is no key escrow, no admin override and no support engineer who can recover it for you, and that last part is a feature with a real cost, which I would rather say up front than have you find out.

Zero knowledge is a claim people make loosely, so here is what it looks like from the machine's side. One object in a published vault is 786 bytes of AES-256-GCM ciphertext, named by the SHA-256 of the encrypted bytes. Because the name is a hash of the ciphertext, the host can deduplicate it, cache it for a year and serve it from a CDN while remaining unable to tell you what it is. Underneath is a real version control system rather than a sync folder: multi-parent commits, a tree per directory, a merge-base computation and three-way merge, all working on content the server cannot interpret. Before I wrote the article this comes from, in August 2026, I cloned all nineteen published vaults from their published keys: 1,389 files, no failures. Status, plainly: sgit is beta and says so on every page; it is a pip install, pure Python, Apache-2.0. In 2018, in Backup your life, I wrote that the fewer secrets you have the easier it is to back up the rest, and that I pushed as much as I could to GitHub and trusted its backups over mine. The 2026 version of the same habit is to back up into a vault whose key you hold.

One string, and the key you can publish

What makes it work is a single string: a passphrase and a vault id. That is the vault key, and it is three things at once: the address of the vault, the authorisation to write to it, and the key that decrypts it. Lose it and the data is gone. Leak it and someone can rewrite your history.

From it you can derive, one way, a read key: same vault, read-only, safe to publish. That asymmetry is the whole sharing model. The read key is the entire credential, with no account, no login and no access request, and every vault linked from sgit.ai is published as one. No vault key has ever been published, a rule enforced by a tripwire in the release pipeline that scans every file before either remote is touched. The site this book is made on runs on the same rule: the public read key is the only key that belongs in its repository, and the build refuses the private prefix. In the browser the key sits after the # in the address, which browsers do not send to the server. Beside the vault key sit credentials made for one job each: a write key the server checks as a hash, an append token that writes one message to one lane and nothing else, and a key pair for sealing something that only its holder can open.

The part that surprises people is that a vault can contain an application, not only documents, and the application runs against the vault without ever being handed a credential. Its manifest declares permissions of nothing; the host reads the vault and passes results in; the app never sees a key. Capability without credential: you can run code you did not write against data you care about, with the blast radius set by a config file you can read in one screen. The same shape holds for a model. On a static site a chat box has nowhere to keep an API key, so the plan on sgit.ai is a bridge: the key lives below the permission floor, the host makes the call, and the app never sees it. The qualification travels with the claim, in the bridge's own words: it "protects your key; it is not yet a boundary that prevents all egress". It protects the credential you trusted it with. It does not yet stop a malicious app calling a provider with a credential of its own. Controlling your agent: RiskMandate is where that gap gets written down.

Protect the keys, not the data

The security benefit of all this is the one everyone expects, and it is real. When the server can read what it stores, all of it has to be protected: every copy, backup, log and replica, and every person and process with access. When the server holds ciphertext named by the hash of that ciphertext, the problem moves to protecting the keys, which is a much smaller problem.

The sovereignty benefit is less obvious. The line stops being where the data is stored and becomes who holds the keys. So the data can sit with a provider, or in a country, that has sovereignty gaps, and you remain sovereign over it and able to move it. The European Data Protection Board takes exactly this case, storage that does not need the data in the clear, and concludes that with strong encryption and keys "retained solely under the control of the data exporter", "the EDPB considers that the encryption performed provides an effective supplementary measure." For a founder selling into Europe that paragraph is worth more than a data centre.

The cost is that everything that matters is now in the key, so key management is where the effort goes. I use two homes. A password manager, for the keys I hold as a person; that is, and will stay, my preferred answer, even though the integration I want does not exist yet and today it is a careful paste into an entry of the right kind. And a registry, for everything the agents create: a private vault, run by its own isolated session with no other access, the vault of vaults, so a key is never lost and never has to live in a browser tab or a chat. Remembering keys under aliases on one machine is a convenience, not a management system. Once agents create vaults several times a day, as mine do, nothing less than a system works.

The weakest step is giving a key to another agent, because the agent platforms have no secrets per session. On the platform I use most, the documentation is direct: an environment variable is one that "any command Claude runs can read", and "Anyone who uses the environment can read the values". A network secret keeps a key outside the machine, but it cannot help with a vault key, because decryption happens inside the session. So today a key for another agent travels in the prompt, which puts it in a transcript; in an environment variable, which every session can read; or not at all, with the agent making its own vault and sending the key back, which is the pattern I use most. The next chapter has the handover.

Write-only lanes

The piece that makes "not at all" work, and that lets a site with no server take input, is the append lane. A lane is a write-only channel attached to a vault. A sender holds a token for one lane and the owner's public key, seals its message to that key, and sends it with a single POST. The owner decrypts with a private key that never leaves them. The server keeps only hashes and ciphertext, so compromising it yields hashes and ciphertext. One lane per sender, so one can be revoked without touching the others.

That shape turns out to be useful for far more than messages. I use lanes for key handover; for logs and observations from sessions that hold no vault key; for what a reader fills in on a web page, which is how the newsroom's sign-ups reach us from a site with no server; and for messages between agents on different platforms with different owners, where neither of us holds the other's key. It is a little like email. And the token is still a secret: it cannot read, but with the vault id it authorises writes, so I hand one out per sender and never publish it. For honesty's sake: the lane is the one part of the vault server that needs a small piece of server logic. Everything else would work over read-only files in a bucket.

Two corrections

Two things in this chapter I got wrong first, and the book should not repeat them.

The first is the order I told it in. When we describe sgit we start with the encryption, and I did too. But I do not think anyone will buy sgit because it is encrypted. Encryption is an important property, not a reason to choose a tool. If sgit succeeds, it will be because it has better features and better workflows, and what took me a while to see is where those came from: the constraint. Innovation needs constraints and the loop of trying, failing and shipping, and the encryption was the constraint that shaped sgit most. The usual reason for a server is to serve a client just the files it needs; sgit could not do that on the server, so it does it in version control, and a partial clone carries every folder you did not ask for by its id. With immutable encrypted objects, a read is a plain GET, so any static host can serve a vault. Each is a better engineering decision than the server-side one would have been. The research is more careful than the slogan: a review found "an inverted U-shaped effect of constraints on creativity and innovation", which is to say some constraint helps and too much hurts. The same applies to what you build for your customers. Take things away until it hurts, and stop there, because past that point it gets worse. The best product is not the one you use because you have to; it is the one that hurts when you stop. I have a stake here, sgit is mine, and the market will decide.

For Dinis the register flags a tension: the frame article presents SG/Send and sgit by their privacy property, and The value of constraints says nobody buys them for it. Which does the book lead with when it names them, the workflow or the property?

The second correction is about the law. My memo said that with the data encrypted you do not have to worry about GDPR. That is too strong. For whoever holds the keys, encrypted personal data is still personal data; the UK regulator says not to "think the encrypted data is not subject to the UK GDPR", and storing it is still processing. The controller's duties stay. What encryption does is reduce the exposure, and the law recognises it: Article 32 lists encryption as a security measure, and under Article 34(3)(a) a breach need not be communicated to the people affected if the data was made "unintelligible to any person who is not authorised to access it, such as encryption", provided the key was not lost with it. The Court of Justice held in September 2025 that pseudonymised data is not personal data "in all cases and for every person", which may help a store that never has the keys, though that store still processes data on someone's behalf, under a contract. None of this is legal advice. The 2018 outline on GDPR had one line, "Know your rights", and never got further; the line I would add now is that client-side encryption gives a founder much less to worry about, not nothing.

From the register: Git for things you cannot put on GitHub: what an encrypted vault is, One string is address, authorisation and key; the read key derived from it is safe to publish, An app in a vault runs against the vault without ever being handed a key, Encryption on the client moves the problem from the data to the keys, and the sovereignty line to whoever holds them, Where the keys live: a password manager for the keys a person holds, a registry vault for the keys agents make, Append lanes: one POST from anywhere, readable only by the vault's owner, Nobody buys it for the encryption: the features came from the constraint, Client-side encryption reduces the exposure under GDPR; it does not take you out of it. Answers: Security.

25Controlling your agent: RiskMandate

The first edition had a whole part on security, and most of it is bullets I never wrote up. Learn to Hack was four lines, the last the one that mattered: "in order to write secure code you need to understand how to exploit it". Threat Modeling said the technique "helps with the two curses of developers": changes to the brief, and the lack of accountability for them. Then the thing that takes the brief became an agent, and a company now runs on several. This is the chapter for the founder who will be asked, by a customer before the contract or a board after something goes wrong, what the agent was allowed to do.

Mandate, reach and the gap

When you connect an agent to something, it gets a credential, and that credential lets it do far more than the job you had in mind. So before you give an agent access, write down four things. Its reach: what the credential actually lets it touch. Its mandate: what you authorised it to do. The gap between them, which What can your agent reach? calls "authority nobody ever accepted". And for every capability in the gap, the barrier: what actually stands in the way. That document is an Agent Behaviour Policy, and the goal is the smallest mandate, the smallest reach, the smallest gap.

There are four kinds of barrier, and only one bounds anything: nothing; a rule written in the prompt; a setting the agent's own account can change; and a boundary enforced above the agent, out of its reach. The test is one sentence: "A control bounds a grant only if it is enforced by something the grant does not include." Rules in a prompt are hope; rules in a hook, a gateway or the platform are enforcement. Add one more fact to each rule, whether a named person has accepted the risk of it not being enforced, with an expiry, and every rule lands in one of three states: backed by a control, governed by an accepted risk, or resting on hope. In a worked example of one firm's inbox agent, five of seventeen rules had a control, six an accepted risk and six were hope: illustrative proportions, but with an agent it can be counted.

Reach is not a document; it is the output of a calculation, so ask what calculates it and whether it is wired to your pipeline. Add a connector and the reach grows, the gap recomputes and a re-review follows. The mandate is locked business logic, so new capability can only land in the gap as excess, never as new authority. Unbounded includes volume: £10,000 per action, a thousand actions, a £10 million exposure.

A behaviour policy answers "may it?". It does not answer "is it right?". An agent allowed to refund up to $200 refunds $150 to a customer owed $120, and every control passes. So behaviour says what the agent may do, decision says who chooses at a branch point and on what evidence, and workflow says which steps run in what order.

RiskMandate's try-it page is thirteen prompts you paste into the assistant you already have connected to your mail. The first asks it to list its own tools: "the list is longer than the tile that sold it". The second asks what you actually wanted it for, in three lists, wanted, refused, never mentioned: "the correction is almost always downward". The third writes the gap down. Twenty minutes, and a self-report: the cheapest evidence there is, and the weakest.

The policy beside the prompt

Why is a rule in a prompt only hope? Because models do not separate code from data. In a computer, instructions and data are kept apart, and the worst bugs in history, from buffer overflows to SQL injection, are what happens when that separation breaks. In a language model there is no separation to break: every instruction and every piece of content arrives in the same channel, which is why the UK's National Cyber Security Centre said in December 2025 that prompt injection may never be mitigated the way SQL injection was. Simon Willison's phrase is "LLMs follow instructions in content". The vendor says the same of its own product: its permission rules "are enforced by Claude Code, not by the model".

So here is a proposal. An agent today gets a system prompt from its vendor and a prompt from us. It should get a third input, a behaviour policy written to a published standard, which the vendor accepts as part of the deployment, enforces as far as the platform can see, reports every breach against, and eventually answers for. The user answers for what is outside the policy, the vendor for what is inside it, instead of the user being liable for everything because they clicked allow. Most agents already run a hidden policy of their own: Claude in Chrome will not type a password, and that is a policy you cannot point to in a dispute. No vendor accepts a policy as an input today in this sense.

The vendors should want it, for the reason aviation and payments got safe. At 1995's rate of 2.72 fatal airliner accidents per million flights, 2025's 38.7 million flights would have meant about 105; there were 12, because every crash anywhere was news. Payments got its security because the liability moved onto the companies: a US cardholder liable for at most $50 since 1970, £35 in the UK, and UK banks reimbursing scam victims up to £85,000 since October 2024. The improvement comes where a rule, a liability that lands on the company and a risk to the business model point the same way, and agents can be that case.

The policy is the business

The first rules anybody writes for an agent are mechanical and the same everywhere: do not send, only draft; do not delete; use your own account. A few questions in, you are describing the business. Which drafts may it send? Can it mention a price? Can it write to a client in dispute? Those rules come in layers with different owners: the function, the process, the relationship, and what the company is for. Vendors stop at the mechanics, because a vendor that models each customer's business becomes a consultancy. A skill says how; a policy layer says what is allowed, who owns the rule and what stands in the way of breaking it. Written together, they are the business codified for an agent, and only the business can write them.

Why has nobody written this down before? Because the software was the law. A clerk could not send an unapproved invoice because the finance system had no button for it until the approval was recorded. An agent works through APIs, not screens. It can call the endpoint the button would have called, so the rule the interface enforced by omission is no longer enforced unless somebody writes it down and something enforces it.

Read by a buyer, the same document says something else. It says which identity the agent uses, what it may read, write, not do and spend, and which risks were accepted by whom. A buyer reads the blast radius off it: a deploy agent whose identity has no merge right and no production credential can at most break staging. "The moment of authorisation is not when you allow the agent to do something, it is the moment you give them the permissions." A vendor with agents in its pipeline and no policy to show has agents whose blast radius nobody has written down, which is a finding before any code is read.

Who can pull the plug

Most AI projects do not fail technically; they stall when the business asks: can you stop it, what if it issues ten thousand of something, who is liable. Every board asks the same thing about an agent in one sentence: who can pull the plug, how fast, what breaks, and what cannot be undone. A deployed agent's risk cannot be denied, only accepted, by a named owner, for an interval. An agent that cannot be stopped is far riskier than one that can, so containment (terminate, revoke, block, quarantine, ticket) is designed in advance and executed by the company's own stack. RiskMandate is not inline, not an insurer and not a one-button solution; it makes agents insurable and does not insure them. I have a stake in it, and I say so.

Secrets, and small vaults

A rule from running agents every day: an agent told something cannot untell it. A transcript is a durable artefact that is retained, can be exported and outlives the session; two keys in my own estate were rotated because they were displayed and the display was captured. So the secret goes from the drop into the tool, never into the chat. The handover that replaced pasting a vault key: the agent seals the key to the registry's public key, sends it with one POST on a write-only lane, and replies with only the vault id and the HTTP response, so no transcript holds the key.

Classify the credential before it touches anything. When we published seven vaults in a fortnight, three arrived as a vault key described as a read key; a catch that depends on somebody looking is not a control, so it became a tripwire in the build. The shape to fear has a name, the lethal trifecta: private data, untrusted content and a way out, in one place.

Working this way you start making very small vaults for very specific things: one for the two or three agents on a job, in which they talk and work through versioned, encrypted files. A leaked key then exposes one small vault and one conversation, not an inbox, a drive or an account, and every word is on the record for the person who holds the keys.

Security as a business tool

Every line of code that ships is a capability, and the vulnerability is usually in one nobody asked for. Log4Shell was a convenience, a lookup inside log messages, and the fix was not a patch: version 2.16.0 said "Completely remove support for Message Lookups". My 2018 outline Strings should be banned had the same instinct: a string is a grant of anything. We measured the idea on our own tool: of 1,500 files in sgit and its dependencies, 428 ran for one real use case, and a tree holding only those passed it. The best code to protect is the code that is not there.

The 2018 line still holds: to write secure code you need to understand how to exploit it. In my 2016 keynote in Lisbon I defined it: to hack means to solve problems. My own habit was the bookmarklet, an attack you click rather than describe, and the business owner I handed one to cared more about the interface than the flaw. Who are you protecting against? is a ladder of six tiers, from your own mistakes to states, and the encouraging truth is at the bottom, in the NCSC's words: "Organisations that defend effectively against commodity threats present a very hard target for all attackers."

That experience is what lets me say: yes, ship it with a list of gaps. The newsroom's reading meter runs entirely in the browser, and No server, by design writes the gaps down: a reader can edit their balance, start again in a private window, credit themselves with an invented payment id. Every one changes a number only the cheater sees. Closing them would need accounts, a database and a server; the model names what would change the call. Ask who you are protecting against. Accept, in writing, what does not match the threat. Name the condition that would make the gaps unacceptable. The 2018 threat-modelling outline ended with the right sentence: a threat model "leads to the creation of risks which are then given to the business owners". In your company, that owner is you.

For Dinis the 2018 outline on third-party modules asked for the story behind the developers who thought of mining coins from users with the sleep function, and a simulated npm hack story. Only you know whether either belongs here.

The model cannot be the barrier

The vendor's own measurements of prompt injection in browser use went from a 23.6% attack success rate without mitigations to 11.2% in August 2025 and to about 1% in November 2025, a rate that in their words "still represents meaningful risk". A desktop shows an agent pages, documents and emails written by other people, so the question is not whether a page will try but what the agent can do when one succeeds. An egress allowlist limits where it can send; a box without secrets limits what it can send; a revocable identity limits for how long; a recording says what happened. The model's defences reduce how often that matters, and do not replace it. So: one mandate per task, written before the desktop exists, and the desktop configured from it. On 7 October 2026 every one of the nine properties of a locked-down desktop could be bought somewhere, and no single product had all nine.

From the register: Mandate, reach and the gap: hope versus enforcement, Code is a liability; the future is the smallest code that does the job, Learning to hack is still how you learn how things work: draw the line where the attacker is, The secret goes from the drop into the tool, never into the chat, Security experience is what lets you ship with known gaps: list them, match them to the reader, say what would change the call, The agent behaviour policy is the due diligence document: a buyer reads the blast radius off it, and its absence says the most, A vault per conversation and per job: a leaked key exposes one small thing, not an inbox, The desktop is the barrier, not the model: prompt injection is not solved, so the box has to hold, Reach is a computed value, and new capability lands as excess, never as new authority, Who can pull the plug: an agent that cannot be stopped is riskier than one that can, A third input: a behaviour policy beside the system prompt and the prompt, which the vendor acknowledges, enforces where it can and answers for, Models do not separate code from data, so a rule in the prompt can be argued with by an email, Aviation and payments got safe when the rule, the liability and the business model pointed the same way, Zoom into an agent's behaviour policy and you find the business logic, which only the business can write, Software was the law: the rule the screen enforced by omission is no longer enforced once an agent calls the API, Every rule lands in one of three states: backed by a control, governed by an accepted risk, or resting on hope, Write down what your agent can reach in twenty minutes: list its tools, say what you wanted it for, write the gap. Answers: Threat Modeling, 3rd-party-modules, Security creates better developers, Learn to Hack, Pointers, Strings should be banned, The future needs you.

26Open source

In 2018 I asked a question I already knew the answer to: "Why don't you open source your code?" The answers, I wrote, would be some mix of "I don't think my code is good enough", "I'm embarrassed about my code" and "Nobody will want to use my code". My advice was blunt: "The key is to just do it!" Create a repository, add a licence file, and that is it. Then I told the truth about what happens next: "Nothing!" Most open source is maintained by a very small number of people.

Eight years on, everything in that chapter still holds, and the reason to do it has become clearer to me, and sharper now that the reader may be starting a company rather than joining one. It was never about contributions. It is that nobody can lock you in, and that includes you.

What open source is, and what it is not

Open source is a copyright licence: the holder using the right to control copying to grant the opposite, you may copy, use, change and share, on these terms. The GNU General Public License, in 1989, made sharing a condition; the term "open source" came in 1998 as a pragmatic reframing of the same freedoms for businesses. The repositories behind the newsroom are Apache 2.0 for code and Creative Commons for writing, and this book is CC BY 4.0, as the first edition was.

One thing I believed for a while, and was wrong about: that the most open thing was not to licence at all. With no licence, all rights are reserved; if you want people to use your work, a licence is how you say so.

At an event in October 2026 I heard misconceptions that are now common among developers who use open source every day without having grown up in its culture, and What open source is, and what it is not corrects six: that nobody controls it (a few maintainers do, visibly); that anybody contributes (almost every project is a handful of people); that it is more, or less, secure than proprietary software (the metric is whether somebody was paid to review it); that it costs nothing; that open weights are open source (weights are a compiled binary); and the one I just confessed to. Through all of it, one sentence from 2018 is the one I would keep. When I see an application given away for free, as in zero cost rather than as in freedom, "I always think 'What is their agenda?'"

The fork next door

Here is why that matters to a company. In a healthy startup everything is open source, so the technology is not the moat; customers stay for the service, the quality and the trust. Now ask what happens when one corner goes wrong. In an open source company every corner has an exit, and the exit takes the technology with it. If the founders stop executing, investors can fund a team to run the same code next door. If a new owner changes direction or the licence, the founders can leave with the code. If anyone lets the customers down, the customers can move, as long as their data moves with them. The fork next door lays the triangle out.

Forks are rare and serious: in a study of 220 significant forks, only 7 merged back. So the real defence is execution, and in the agent era, speed. The moat is a rate, not a wall: "A competitor who forks our code today gets our position as of today. They do not get our velocity."

This is also the answer to the investor who asks what happens if the founder goes rogue, which Fund it, or do not comes back to. In a conventional round, control is the insurance: the right to replace the founder. Here the fork is the insurance: the right to replace the company. The fork disciplines the founder because it is possible and protects the founder because it is slow. Two conditions make it real: a genuinely open licence, not open core, and a copyright structure not gathered into one corporate holder. The same protection works for your customer: with the code open and the record kept, "who will fix it?" has many answers, your team, the partner, another partner, or the agents, under a mandate, reading the record.

The security argument has moved too. Eric Raymond's line was "Given enough eyeballs, all bugs are shallow", and the honest answer was always that the eyeballs were not there. Models can now run deep security reviews, over and over, at a cost that used to buy one consultant for a day; The eyeballs get cheap tells curl's year, ending with a frontier scan that found one vulnerability, "yes, as in singular one". The thing open source lacked was paid attention, and that is what just became abundant. It is why I think open source is about to become the most secure software we have.

Open source is not free

Somebody is paying for open source, even if only with their time. I gave my agents an old iMac as a desktop of their own, clean-installed with the newest system it would take, and the package manager would not install, the container tool would not install. Every project that dropped that machine did it for the same reason: supporting it costs money, in CI hardware and maintainer time, and the people who still need the support are not the ones paying.

The long tail is measurable and not small. In Homebrew's install analytics, 6.99% of macOS install events in the last 30 days, and 16.66% over a year, came from versions older than the newest three; on PyPI, 18.55% of pip downloads run on end-of-life Python. Money flows to old versions when a vendor owns them and not when a community does: Oracle earned $19.8 billion from support last year; Red Hat's subscriptions ran at about 93% gross margin; open source as a whole receives about 0.09% of its estimated value in investment, and in one survey 60% of maintainers describe themselves as unpaid hobbyists. The xz maintainer wrote "this is an unpaid hobby project" two years before the backdoor. The question was never whether somebody pays. It is whether the people who get the value have an easy way to pay for it.

All of it, or it is an evaluation version

For a founder, open source is a constraint, and its value is in what it takes away. There is no private code. That makes the code easy to deploy, run and change, including by the customer, which solves a lot of sovereignty problems on its own. And it removes two temptations. The first is to lock the customer in; without lock-in, the only reason anyone uses the product is that they want to, and the best product is not the one you use because you have to but the one that hurts when you stop. The second is what I think is the curse of most startups: they win customers, and the customers get them to build every feature and integration only they need, until the roadmap belongs to everyone but the startup. With open source the answer is: here is the code; integrate it yourself, or pay us to do that specific thing.

That is why you cannot open source half of it. A community edition that turns proprietary as soon as you use it for real is an evaluation version with a better name. Elastic, HashiCorp and Redis each moved to a licence that is not open source, each produced a well-funded fork backed by the cloud providers it targeted, OpenSearch, OpenTofu and Valkey, and two of the three have since added an open licence back. The Open Source Initiative's verdict on one of those licences was that it was not an open source licence at all.

Sell the build, not the code

So what do you sell? The two constraints, open code and a server that holds nothing, remove a lot of bad business decisions and leave the good one: you cannot sell access to the code or rent the data back, so you sell customisation, and keeping it working. Everything RiskMandate and sgit build is open source: policies, code, vaults, backend. What the customer buys is a customised, maintained build for them, sometimes with fewer features and so less attack surface, tested on their target, kept working as models, connectors and threats change, with someone accountable for it, and cheaper through us than doing it themselves. The moat is delivery, maintenance and trust, not lock-in.

I tested whether that pays in a model of one project at Homebrew's scale with one long-tail target to keep alive. Donations covered the cost in 24% of draws. Supported builds sold to individuals at about £1.80 a machine a year covered it in half the draws with typical conversion, and no price reached nine in ten. Customised builds for companies, priced below what it costs them to keep the tool working themselves, covered it in 94%. It is a model, not a forecast. But the shape is the one I would build a company on. Not a donation, and not a licence that gives you a reason to make the free version worse. "The value is the support, not the code, so the code can stay open."

Why it is easy to throw your own technology away

There is a quieter benefit, and it is the one I feel most in my own work. When everything I build is open source, I do not have a moat I have to keep using. There is no part of the stack I need to protect from being replaced, and I am usually the first to try to replace it when something better appears. The habit is to ask of every component whether it is still earning its place, and open source removes the one bad reason to say yes: that I built it. Serverless, for real leans on that habit throughout, and The question is whether they miss it adds the founder's version: build in the open and the work is still yours when you move on. You leave with your tools.

Your first pull request

Now the part for the reader going into a job rather than making one. In 2018 I wrote that a great way to get a job in a company is to contribute to an open source project hosted by that company, and the OWASP chapter put it more plainly: most projects are maintained by a couple of people, and all of them need help. Act as the job you want adds the 2026 version: if you are looking for a job, you have time, so do the work in public, where it can be linked.

Here is what a good first contribution looks like now. On 16 October 2026, late in the evening, Mondweep Chakravorty opened a pull request against an agent framework with 9,625 commits on its main branch. It added one page, 83 lines of documentation, describing his adapter: a behaviour policy compiled into the framework's own rules, gating memory checkpoints into an encrypted vault. The maintainer merged it the next afternoon; what got it in was the page being specific about what it claims. It pinned the exact commits it depends on. It listed its evidence and its gaps in the same breath: "Semantic quality is NOT validated." A page that does not oversell can be merged in under a day.

That is the bar, and it has risen since 2018: curl's contribution rules now say that if you asked an AI tool to find problems, you must reveal that, so a first pull request today carries its provenance, what you did, what you checked, what you did not. The 2018 outline GitHub Flow was five bullets I never wrote up, "Learn how to do Forks, Commits and Pull Requests" among them; the mechanics take an afternoon with an agent beside you.

I should say where this came from, because ideas have a lineage. My own shift into an open source mindset happened, as the 2018 chapter records, "20 years ago after I read" The Cathedral and the Bazaar: "Release early. Release often. And listen to your customers." Linus Torvalds built two of the things all of this stands on, Linux and Git. OWASP is where I learnt that knowledge compounds fastest in the open, and some of my best friends in the industry came from it. The 2018 chapter on Creative Commons quoted Tim O'Reilly: "For a typical author, obscurity is a far greater threat than piracy".

For Dinis your own first pull request to someone else's project, and what it led to; the 2018 chapter says the usual outcome is nothing, and a reader will want the counter-example from your life.

The excuse I hear from careful graduates is the one I heard in 2018: the code is not good enough yet. Put the licence on it, send the small, honest pull request, and let the people who want to help you find something they can link.

From the register: Open source means nobody can lock you in: the fork next door, Your first pull request: open source is still the newcomer's best door, Open source, community and OWASP: working and getting hired in the open, with the names behind it, Open source is why it is easy to throw your own technology away, Open source as a constraint: all of it, or it is an evaluation version with a better name, Open source everything, and sell the customised, tested, maintained build, Open source is not free: somebody pays, and the people who get the value have no easy way to. Answers: Open Source, Creative Commons, GitHub Flow.

27Shipped means the user has it

In August 2026 two consecutive releases of sgit.ai pushed cleanly, verified themselves against both remotes, printed "both remotes in sync, done", and never reached anybody. The site served a two-release-old page for forty minutes. A person on a phone looked at the version badge and asked: "is this the latest version?"

Everything about that sentence is worth sitting with. The checks were not weak. They were checking the wrong boundary. And for a founder the word at stake is the one the whole company turns on. Shipping does not mean a proof of concept, a demo, a video of a demo, or a prototype you walk somebody through on a call. It means "a thing somebody else can go and use, on their own, and get value from". It can be held together with tape; nobody using it cares how it was built. Getting a working thing into a stranger's hands is still, in 2026, genuinely hard, and solving that is not overhead. It is the first real product you build.

Green does not mean live

The release script did five things, and four of them were good. Build the site from source. Run the validator. Push the encrypted vault and confirm it is in sync. Push the git mirror and confirm the local head matches the remote. That last check is strong; it compares hashes, not hopes. The problem is what it means. It means the bytes arrived at the code host. It does not mean anybody can read them.

Publishing the site has three boundaries: source to remote, remote to deployment, deployment to reader. We were verifying the first two. Between the second and third sits a hosted build job with its own failure modes, and nothing in our release looked at it. The deploy job died before running a single step of its own, because the download of a third-party action it depends on was rate-limited: "429 (Too Many Requests)", three attempts, give up. It is the ordinary case: the most likely thing to break in a deploy is not your code. It is a piece of shared infrastructure you do not run, on a day you were not thinking about it.

The 2018 outline on CI testing had the line, borrowed from a talk on chaos engineering: "config changes can be more dangerous than code changes", because we do not test them the way we test code. The outline on test-driven development had a story next to it that I never wrote up, about a site that went down for hours because of a one-character change to a web server's configuration file.

For Dinis the story of the site that went down for hours over one character, a pipe, in an nginx config; the 2018 TDD outline promises it and this is where it belongs.

The fix is one question, asked at the end. A release now finishes by asking the live site what version it is serving, polling with a cache-buster for up to eight minutes, because the origin's own answer is the only one that counts. If the version never appears it aborts loudly and names the build page. The cost is real: up to eight minutes on every release. We took it, because the alternative had already been demonstrated twice in one afternoon: telling somebody a fix was live when it was not, twice, in writing.

The site already had two rules of exactly this shape, and the third made the pattern obvious. A page nothing links to is unpublished; the build fails on an orphan. A page the machine index omits is unpublished; the build fails if a page would be missing from the index for agents. A page the deploy never served is unpublished; now the release fails on it. All three are the same claim, and it is the title of this chapter: shipped is a fact about the reader, not about your repository. Every one was learned by getting it wrong first, which is the only reason the list is short and specific rather than long and aspirational.

Files make releases cheap, and they also make it easy to ship something incomplete. When the newsroom moved to its own domain, 51 of the 527 figures in its articles did not come with it, and nothing failed in a way a server would have noticed, because the pages were files and they served. That is the cost of the speed Serverless, for real argues for, and it is why every release there ends by checking what is actually live.

Tests that never run are not tests

The 2018 outline on testing had two lines I still say. If you make a change and no test breaks, you are making random changes, and you have no way of knowing whether they are still in effect tomorrow. And: if you do not have full coverage, what are the bits of code not covered by tests, and what happens when they change?
