The more coverage you have, the more changes you are happy to make, and hundreds of small changes are the only way I know to build something good that lasts. Now that an agent writes the harness, there is no excuse for not having that.

In October 2026 I measured the newsroom against that standard, and it is a fair picture of explorer code that has just started taking money. The live reader code was about 2,700 lines. The tests that covered it were 433 lines, and the one workflow the repository had never ran them. The same application had two upstreams, with two diverging copies of the meter. No page set a content security policy. It is also exactly what has to change before the next wave, because a reader can now pay £5 by card. A mistake in the meter is a mistake with someone's money, and the day your company takes its first payment is the day this chapter stops being advice.

The line that moved is the one Explorer coding, not vibe coding, and the handover draws. Explorer code is read for intent, mostly by agents. Villager code is read line by line, because that is where changes land and where the blast radius lives. The newsroom's reader features had crossed from the first to the second, and the right move at that moment is to go slow to go fast. Go slow to go fast is the brief, in six phases. Map everything: every file, capability and control. Move the reader code to its own repository as a pure refactoring: the same behaviour and the same bugs, proved not by a reviewed diff but by hashing every file the live site serves before and after. Test: only test code is written, in real browsers, against a real pre-production copy, with every known bug written as a test that passes, recording the current behaviour, so the fix later is a visible change to a test and not a silent one. Refactor, with every test passing after every commit, and an adversarial exercise: try to change what a reader sees or pays without a test failing, and treat every change that slips through as a missing test. Only then does the explorer return.

The pipeline is the other half. The reader application is plain JavaScript and static files, so the target is under two minutes on every commit: lint, unit tests, three browser engines, and a build that produces the same bytes twice. On every merge, a pre-production copy with a payment link in test mode that can never charge anyone. On every release, the question from the first section, asked of the live site. Going slow now is what lets me keep going fast without the fear that each new feature breaks something a reader has paid for. The 2018 outline on DevOps had a joke I still like: "Congratulations, you now own/manage a Data Center". The 2026 version is gentler and the same: congratulations, you have a user; now everything you ship lands on them.

Run it, unmodified, against a twin

Most teams test against mocks they wrote themselves and hope production behaves the same. There is a better shape, and it follows from the twin in Serverless, for real: an interface to a real service that the application cannot tell from the real thing. If the application cannot tell the difference, the same unmodified code can run against an in-memory twin, in milliseconds, on a laptop; against a twin replaying last week's production journal; against a twin injecting the failures you never see in staging, a timeout, a throttle, a permission that disappears halfway through a job; and against the real service, for the final check. The environment becomes a setting rather than a guess, and that is a dramatic change in how much you can know before you ship.

I would go further, and make it a requirement for anything your company builds: it must be able to run, unmodified, against a twin of every external service it depends on, without knowing. Software that can only run against one provider's live API is software you cannot test properly, cannot move, and cannot put a policy in front of. That last clause is why the twin is also where the journal and the behaviour policy from Controlling your agent: RiskMandate live: every call passes through it. The 2018 outline on chaos engineering wanted to inject failure and watch; the twin is where you do it without touching production. The S3 twin exists as open source today; the rest is designed, not shipped, and I say so because this is exactly the kind of claim that should be checked.

Five people who do not exist

A green pipeline tells you the code does what the tests say. It does not tell you what a person sees. For that, the cheapest method I have found is synthetic users: five to ten invented people, played by a model, each driving a real browser through the site one screenshot at a time, saying what they see, think, ask and do, and then being interviewed about it. How to run synthetic users on your own site is the how-to, from three studies, and it takes an afternoon.

A synthetic user is a record, not a character: a situation, a question they arrive with, what they know and do not know, what makes them leave, their patience and their screen size. Two habits have paid off every time: include somebody who will never buy, because the sceptic reads the site adversarially and finds the claims that do not hold; and put at least one person on a phone. Write the expected journey before any run, so the difference between what you expected and what happened is a finding you can point at. Then the one rule of the run itself: give the agent the screenshot, the same thing a person would have, and not the page's code. An agent that reads the code finds every link every time, and so finds no confusion, which is the only thing worth running this for. Every person is invented; do not model real people, and say so where nobody can scroll past it.

On the newsroom, five invented readers took 62 steps, asked 51 questions the site did not answer, and produced 31 findings, six of them blocking. Four of the five left. Their ratings were 2, 2, 3, 3 and 2. The blocking findings were all about the step after reading: no contact for a pilot, nothing showing real money moving, a top-up page that says it is simulated while asking for money. Then came the step that separates a study you can stand behind from a pile of plausible paragraphs: check every surprising finding against the browser's own record. Two did not survive; they were caused by our own helper, and they are kept in the study, labelled as artefacts. One got bigger: a reader hit "screenshot unavailable" where the evidence should have been, and that is how the 51 missing figures were found.

It is not user research, and the study says so. The plan is simple, and it is the habit I would look for in anyone I was hiring to ship, and the one I would hold a founder to: every site gets five users before it is called done, and the same five again after the fixes, because a finding that disappears is a fix that worked, and a new one is something the fix broke. Then ask the live site what version it is serving, and only then say it shipped.

From the register: Shipped means the user has it: green does not mean live, go slow to go fast, Every piece of software should run, unmodified, against a twin of every service it depends on. Answers: CI Testing, Continuous Integration, TDD (Test-Driven Development), DevOps.

Part 8

The long game

What lasts. Graphs as memory for the person you will be, and the civic responsibility that comes with knowing how the machines work, for a small country and for a continent.

28Graphs, memory and your future self

In 2018 I wrote a chapter called Backup your life. It asked one question: if you lost your laptop right now, how painful would it be? My own answer, at the moment of writing, was that I would lose the text of that chapter and nothing else, and in half an hour not even that, because it would be in git and on GitHub. The chapter ended with a line I still stand by: "You basically need to backup your life!"

Eight years later the question is the same and the answer has grown. It is no longer only your files. It is your ideas, the connections between them, and now the memory your agents work from. If you have started a company by this point in the book, as I hope you have, it is the company's memory too: what you decided, why, and what you tried before. This chapter is about all of that, and about the people you are keeping it for: your future self, and whoever joins you later and was not in the room.

I think in graphs

My thinking is very hyperlinked. I connect things; I follow this and then that, and it all works itself out in the end. Everything is connected: graphs introduced the graph as a thing to draw. Here I want to say it as a way of thinking, because it is how I keep what I know.

The first edition had a chapter on DOT, a text language for diagrams. The argument was not the tool. It was that a diagram stored as text can go in git, be committed on every change and be diffed, so you see what changed since you last looked. I wrote then that our brains struggle to review a diagram a second time unless we are shown "what changed since the last review", and that we were making decisions "based on shadows of reality".

Think of what your team already has, or what your company will have within a month: a spreadsheet of who owns which system, a folder of policies nobody opens, a list of customers and what each was promised. Each is already a graph. A spreadsheet is a graph of sheets, rows, cells and formulas; a team is a graph of people and what they are responsible for. Nothing has to be converted. The edges only need names: a policy is owned by a team and implemented by a control; a decision supersedes an older one.

Two rules matter more than the rest. Every edge is a verb that reads both ways, and "relates to" is banned, because two things always relate and an edge with no verb tells you nothing. And supersede, never delete: an idea that turned out wrong stays, marked, with what replaced it, because the path from wrong to right is itself knowledge. The 2018 book had an outline called Recursive Functions that I never wrote; in a way it is this. Open any node and you find the same shape again, a graph inside the graph, as far down as your question needs.

Then the part that matters most if you have never written code. You do not have to get the graph right. You have to maintain it. The projects that try to get the ontology right first spend a year on it and never ship. Agents are very good at maintaining graphs: they read a new document and propose its nodes and edges, they notice a policy changed and mark what rested on it, and code checks that they kept the rules. When something looks wrong, you ask why, and the answer is a fix at the right altitude. I measured this on the newsroom: the graphs of its 112 articles hold about an eighth of the articles' text, and that eighth is enough to decide what to read.

A graph is also not a search box. Similarity search finds a starting point, but it gives you similarity, not a reason. A graph gives the same answer to the same question, with the path that led to it, and for "why are we required to do this, and by whom?", the path is the answer.

Memory is context management

People ask how my agents remember. The picture most people have is one memory system behind the agent, into which everything is deposited and from which it retrieves by similarity whatever seems relevant. Mine is the opposite, and the newsroom article Memory is not a spectator sport says why.

An agent works inside a window of text. Everything in that window costs money, because it is billed by the token, and costs attention, because recall gets worse as the window fills. So the question is never "what do we remember". It is "what does this agent need in its window right now", and no more. Memory is context management.

It is many memories, not one. The rules of my inbox agent are not the rules of the news graph, and the vocabulary of a code review is not the vocabulary of a risk register. Each is a memory with its own words, and by now, across twenty-seven sibling sites and forty published vaults, they disagree in places. I used to think that was a problem to solve. I now think it is the point: each is correct for its context, and a memory forced to agree with all the others would be wrong for every one of them.

It is fractal: principles at the top in a few kilobytes, code at the bottom, and an agent loads the altitude its question lives at. The fractal is the budget.

And it is files. In August 2025 Letta ran a memory benchmark, and a plain filesystem, with the agent reading and writing files, scored 74.0 percent against 68.5 percent for a graph-based memory product. Files won. A folder of well-named plain files, kept in git, is already a memory an agent can use. You do not need a product for this, and a company of one does not need to buy one.

It is shared. A session ends, and the next one, or a different agent, or a person, picks up from the same files. The article that argued this was written that way: one session, across several context resets, revised an article, wrote two more, published a vault and shipped six releases in a day, "remembering nothing between resets except what the files remembered for it". Nothing is remembered by the model between sessions. Everything is remembered by the files.

I should say where this falls short, because the article does. The thesis calls itself untested and falsifiable. Stale pages become false memories: the session that wrote it found four and fixed two. It is one person's estate, not yet tested against a second person's. Hold it as my position.

Publish, and the site becomes the memory

In 2018, in Backup your life, I said the fewer secrets you have, the easier everything else is to back up, and that the more you publish, the more you can use the web itself as your backup. And I said why I share at all: one day my future self will need it. Open sourcing my work meant that when I moved jobs I did not start from scratch; the O2 Platform research came with me because it was already public.

The same reasoning now has a second beneficiary. Open source and Creative Commons are not a licensing preference here. They are what makes a memory usable by an agent: a public page can be fetched, a public quote cited with its URL, a public graph loaded by anyone's model, a public vault cloned with a read key that is itself public. The more I publish, the more there is for the next session to stand on, and the less it has to be told. One article said it in passing: the site is becoming the memory.

The 2018 book had an outline called Future Self that I never wrote. Its notes said: create assets that will be useful for them; the power of open sourcing your ideas; letters to a younger self are not that useful, unless you have a time machine; and that is why you want to share. I am writing it now. A letter to your younger self reaches nobody. A letter to your future self reaches them, but only if they can find it.

I know this because I failed at it. Most of what I have written is not linkable from what I write now. For years I used Google as the search engine for my own work: I would search for a phrase I remembered and find my old post. That is not a memory, it is luck. The inventory, taken in October 2026 from public pages, is 103 essays, 1,289 blog posts, 65 decks, 10 books, 29 tools, 2,038 files of NotebookLM material and 221 videos. A back catalogue I cannot link to is a conversation with no reply. So the newsroom has a brief for a worker: an agent that runs every day, takes one item from that catalogue, and writes it back as an article saying what it was, what it said, what changed since, and how it connects. Each article is a node the next one can link to. It compounds, and every team has a back catalogue it could do this with, a few items a day, while the team keeps working. If you have spent twenty years inside a problem, as many of the founders I work with have, your back catalogue is the company's first asset, and most of it is in your head.

For Dinis the 2018 Future Self outline promised a Twitter case study from the writing of that book, with two screenshots and no text. What did they show? The story would make this section concrete.

One more thing a shared memory needs, and a vector store cannot give it: a source for every item. On my sites that is a set of habits. Every quote has a URL. Every release has a commit. Every vault has a read key on its page. Every file inside a vault has a hash. Every page has a byline naming the person responsible, the model that drafted it and the date. Provenance is what lets memory be shared without being trusted.

So the advice is the 2018 advice, extended. Keep your work in plain text, in git, committed often. Name the links between things, in words a person in your team would say. Publish what you can, under a licence that lets it be quoted. Keep what you cannot publish, your customers' data first of all, in a vault, with a key that reads and does not write. When you brief an agent, hand it the altitude its question lives at, not everything you have. Your first graph, built by hand and Your own newsroom and inbox show both ends of this done small.

The lesson of Backup your life was that you will lose data, so build the habits before it happens. The lesson now is that you will forget, your agents will forget, and the people who come after you will never have known. The only memory that survives all three is the one written down, linked and left where the next reader will find it. Write for that reader. One day it will be you.

From the register: Think in graphs: knowledge that maintains itself, and your future self, Memory is not a spectator sport: it is context management, many memories, fractal, published and shared, Publish, and your published work becomes your agents' memory, with a URL for every claim. Answers: Future Self, Backup your life, Dot Language, Recursive Functions.

29Civic responsibility

The 2018 book had an outline called Civic Responsibility that I never wrote up. Its notes were six lines. As a developer you have a major civic responsibility. Code is law. You are one of the most important pieces of the puzzle. You can choose where you work and whether your code has a positive or negative impact in the world, and if you cannot choose that at the moment, make sure you are actively looking to change your job. Give the risks you create to the business owners, and make them accountable for the decisions they are making.

I am writing that chapter now, with my own country, because that is where I have made this argument for sixteen years, and the record of what came true and what did not is a better teacher than a list of principles. And I am writing it for a founder, because in 2026 the person who chooses where the code goes is, more and more, the person who started the company.

Lisbon, 2010 and 2016

On 17 December 2010 I gave the keynote at OWASP's IBWAS conference in Lisbon: 168 slides, five proposed focuses and twelve proposed actions for government and industry, from allowing ethical hacking to liability for software and "'Proper' SSL support for 50% of websites". It ended by arguing that Portugal, which had "nothing to lose", could become the Switzerland of hacking. What I asked for "doesn't cost any money"; "It only takes courage, focus and commitment".

Sixteen years later, more of it came true than I expected, though mostly from Brussels rather than Lisbon: from 9 December 2026 the EU's Product Liability Directive brings software under strict, no-fault liability, and since 11 September 2026 the Cyber Resilience Act requires manufacturers to report actively exploited vulnerabilities within 24 hours. What I got wrong was the mechanism. I thought fear of a disaster would move governments quickly. What moved them was a decade of slow, unglamorous regulation.

On 11 November 2016 I gave the keynote at BSidesLisbon, Hacking Portugal, 103 slides, and three weeks later published it as a book on Leanpub, 75 pages. Its first lines are the premise of this chapter: "If the word 'hack' means to solve problems, then 'Hacking Portugal' means to find innovative ways to solve existing problems." The ideas were blunt. Make the Portuguese network hostile to insecure code. Replace the old compulsory military service with a Portuguese Hacking Service for 15 to 21 year olds. A hackathon league as famous as football. OpenSource.pt: all code paid for by government released under an open-source licence. A Ministry of Code.

Ten years on, the Hacking Service, the league and the Ministry of Code have not happened, as far as I know. The best news is in the spirit of OpenSource.pt: on 1 July 2026 Portugal released Amália, a national language model for European Portuguese, built by a consortium of universities with 5.5 million euros of EU recovery money and published with its dataset and code under an open licence.

And the biggest change is one I did not see in 2016. Then, learning to hack meant learning to code first. Now a sixteen-year-old can explore a system by talking to it, which Explorer coding, not vibe coding, and the handover called explorer coding. The talk's central claim, that a small country can choose to lead in code, is more possible than it was, because everyone who can brief an agent can now build.

The plumbing is the state's road

The conversation about a national AI strategy keeps circling the biggest model, the most GPUs, the largest data centre. On a Wardley map those sit at the far right, in commodity, with a lead held by companies that have invested amounts a small country's whole technology budget would not match. A small country should not enter that race.

Put a person at the top of it: a developer in Cluj, a designer in Porto, a pharmacist in Braga with twenty-five years inside a problem, selling a service to a buyer abroad. What stops them is not talent, energy or price. It is forming a company, hiring across a border, being paid across Europe, working in a language the buyer reads, a contract, an invoice, a tax form valid on both sides. None of that is the service. All of it is plumbing, and in most of Europe each piece is custom built by the person who needs it, which means built badly, late, and at a cost that falls on the wrong side. In the last Eurostat figures with a breakdown, for 2021, 18.5 percent of EU enterprises sold online to customers in their own country, 8.1 percent to other EU countries and 4.6 percent to the rest of the world. Commerce does not happen, most of the time, because of attrition.

If you have read The company itself: the UK, Portugal, Lithuania, the European Union and done Form your company, you have met every one of those pieces, and you built them alone. We understand that a government builds roads, because they enable everybody. The shop that opens on the new road did not pay for the road; it paid taxes. Yet we ask every person who wants to sell a service out of a small country to build their own road first. That plumbing is infrastructure in the road sense, and a state that builds it enables all of its people at once.

Estonia did it. A person anywhere can become an e-resident and form an Estonian company online; the programme's dashboard lists more than 142,000 e-residents and more than 43,000 companies, and in 2026 alone e-residents had founded more than 4,200 companies by mid-September. Underneath sits X-Road, which connects around a thousand organisations and is open source under the MIT licence, so another country can take the road rather than draw it again. Estonia is a case study, not a template; the claim is that the pattern transfers.

I have paid for the absence of this plumbing myself. As a CISO and a CTO I spent millions on Upwork building distributed teams, and it worked only because we built the infrastructure around it first; the infrastructure is the whole difference between a talent market that works and a race to the bottom. Those numbers are mine, and this chapter cannot prove them.

What the state pays for belongs to the commons

The plumbing has to be open source and run on open schemas, because that is what turns infrastructure into sovereignty. A road with a toll gate owned by one company is lock-in with tarmac. You cannot have sovereignty without open source and open schemas, because sovereignty means you can leave. Independence is the ability to run a parallel service, move the data and keep going.

It follows that what the state pays for should belong to the commons. Not so everyone changes the production system, but so everyone can see it, run it and leave with their data. That is how the local industry around it comes into being: an ecosystem of smaller firms, yours among them, can only grow around infrastructure it is allowed to see.

And a market for the alternative does not appear on the day the single point of failure fails. So create the demand before the day you need it. Run parallel services. Buy the local or open alternative for part of the estate now, and accept that it has fewer features, because the point is that when the day comes there is somewhere to go. Procurement is the lever a state already holds; it needs no new law to prefer open schemas and data mobility. It is a resilience case, not an ideological one.

The hyperscalers know this argument. AWS's European Sovereign Cloud went live on 15 January 2026, operated by EU residents. It is a real step, and it makes the point: your code still talks to one vendor's API under one vendor's terms, and sovereignty that depends on one company's API is sovereignty by permission. What stops workloads moving is code, not policy, so the answer I am building towards is a twin: an open source service the application cannot tell from the real one, so the destination becomes a setting and not a rewrite. For an operator in Romania, Portugal or the UK it is a business, and a judo move: the customer's existing code, the hyperscaler's own API and open source, used as the weight rather than fought.

The playbook, in five moves: map the attrition, every place commerce stops between a talented person and a buyer abroad; commoditise the plumbing, built once, open to all; create the demand, before the day you need it; put the money where the market is still genesis, small models, data that stays with its owner, agents with the least agency, not GPUs; regulate what settled, after the market and not before it. This is not a national strategy. It is a map and a set of moves from one person's experience.

Smaller models, more of them

Most work does not need a frontier model and should not use one. Frontier models are for finding the art of the possible. For things that have to work, every day, at a price, a frontier model is too expensive, too much power for the task, and dangerous in proportion. Give an agent the least agency that does the job, because that is what you can trust. The right end state is the one Serverless, for real described for your own product: the model is used once to build the tool, and production is plain files and plain code, with no prompt in the path.

That means small models, and many of them. There is a market, still at genesis, for Portuguese, and not one Portuguese: the Portuguese of Portugal and of Brazil, of Angola, of Cape Verde, of Mozambique, each with its culture in the training data and not just its spelling. Amália is the right shape: a public model is a public road. For a founder, that end of the map is where a small company can still lead.

Treat constraints as the input, not the excuse. DeepSeek's own report says its V3 model was trained in 2.788 million GPU hours on export-restricted H800 chips whose interconnect was deliberately throttled. The dollar figure is disputed and I do not rely on it. The lab that could not buy the chips did the interesting work, and yours will too.

Sovereignty is the ability to move

In padel, which I am learning, the striking thing about a good player is how much time they have. They read the ball and they are already where it will be. The ball today is the frontier model and the data centre; where the ball is going is the ability to move: workloads, data and the actions agents take, from one provider to another, when you decide and not on the day a supplier decides for you. Technology sovereignty is the ability to move, almost liquidity.

The test is a Wardley map with one question. Put the customer at the top, the service, the application, the data, and under them the products and commodities you buy. For each dot you do not control: take it away, what happens? If the service stops, you have found the problem, and it belongs on the risk register with a name next to it. That is what the 2018 outline meant by giving the risks you create to the business owners. In your own company, that name is yours.

Nobody gets from zero to a hundred. The bar is not an alternative as good as today; it is one that works for the minimum viable company, the priority customers and the services that must never stop. Run it in QA, all the time, on twins of the dependencies you do not control, so that every small problem is found on a Tuesday rather than during an outage. And it has to be open source, because even a European supplier is one acquisition away from not being one. Control that survives a change of owner is the only control that counts.

If you founded the company, you already chose where your code goes. If you are in a small country, you are not behind. You are at the end of the map where the market still exists.

For Dinis the register notes that a Portuguese edition, or both languages, is your open question, and this chapter is the one it bears on most. Which is it, and does the Hacking Portugal material stay here or move to the new edition of that book?

In 2010 I said what I asked for would cost no money, only courage, focus and commitment. In 2016 I said to hack means to solve problems. Both still hold. What has changed is who can do the hacking: in 2016 the people who had learned to code, now anyone who can brief an agent, which makes the civic responsibility wider, not narrower. You are one of the most important pieces of the puzzle. Act like it.

From the register: Civic responsibility: to hack means to solve problems, and small countries can lead now, The opportunity for a small country is the plumbing, built once by the state like a road, not the race for GPUs, No sovereignty without open source and open schemas; what the state pays for belongs to the commons; buy the alternative before the day you need it, Smaller models, more of them, with the least agency: a public model is a public road, and constraints are the input. Answers: Civic Responsibility.

Part 9

Hands on, 2026

The practical part, dated 2026 and renewed every year, inside the book. Eleven workflows you can follow this afternoon, from an agentic workflow from zero to forming your company, the credits and programmes as they stand this year, your first price and your first ten customers, and a serverless product shipped in a weekend.

30An agentic workflow from zero

By the end of this chapter you will have a folder for every person you deal with, an agent that reads those folders and drafts for you, and six lines of rules that make it safe. One afternoon, no developer, no vault: a Google Workspace account and a Claude seat are enough. The people can be your first customers, or the people who might hire you; the folders do not care. The steps are the newsroom's, from From a spreadsheet to a folder per person, which dates its description of the Claude app to October 2026; the tools move, the shape is what to learn.

What a spreadsheet cannot hold

If you are in touch with people, you probably have a spreadsheet: one row per person, a stage, a date, a next step, and a notes column. The person the article was written with ran their customer relationships that way, and ran them well. What the row cannot hold is what happened: the emails, word for word, the call last Tuesday, what was promised. "The difference is not the tool. It is what counts as the record." In a folder the record is everything that happened, kept as it happened, and the summary is derived from it. That reversal gives an agent something to work with.

The folder, and the policy

Make a folder called Contacts. Inside it, one folder per person. Inside each, five documents, with these names so that you and the agent mean the same thing:

  • Record: role, company, how you met, stage, the next step and its date, what they care about, every fact with where it came from in brackets.
  • Ledger: one line per event: the date, in or out, the channel, one sentence. Only ever added to. A correction is a new line.
  • Messages: every email and note exchanged, pasted word for word, newest at the bottom.
  • Notes: calls and meetings, dated, including what you said into your phone afterwards.
  • Summary: written by the agent from the four above, rewritten when you ask, never edited by hand. If it is wrong, fix the Record or the Ledger and regenerate.

In the Claude app, connect Drive and Gmail, make one Project called Contacts, and put the behaviour policy in its instructions. Six lines, from the article:

  1. You work for me. Every email you write is a draft that I read and send. You never send.
  2. You read the Contacts folder in my Drive and my Gmail. You write only into a person's folder in Contacts, and only into their Ledger, Messages, Notes and Summary. You never change the Record without showing me the change first.
  3. You add to the Ledger and the Messages. You never rewrite or delete a line. A correction is a new line.
  4. Every fact you put in a Record or a Summary names where it came from.
  5. If a person has no folder, you ask before making one. If a request would reach outside the folder, you ask.
  6. At the end of every task you say, in one line, what you read and what you wrote.

Draft and never send; append and never rewrite; stay in the folder. The first three are the ones the newsroom learned the hard way. Most of this is told, not enforced; the rest is a rule the agent keeps, which is why line 6 exists. The one-line report is how you check.

What you have built is, in the terms of Lenses, roles and loops, an agent: a role on a loop with a goal, and the Project's instructions are its role description.

The afternoon, and the first week

The migration is four steps. Export the sheet as CSV, drop it in the folder, and ask the agent to say, for each row, the folder it would make and the Record it would write, without making anything yet. Make the folders from a template; twenty people is twenty minutes by hand. Per person, ask it to read a year of your mail with that person and write the Record and the first Ledger lines, each fact with its source; read the Record and correct it once. Then ask for the Summaries, read three, and where one is wrong fix the source document, not the summary.

Then five exercises, one a day for the first week, each a single message:

  1. Ask. Who have I not spoken to in more than a month, grouped by stage, with the last Ledger line for each?
  2. Prepare. I am meeting someone on Thursday: brief me from the folder, what we last agreed, the last three emails, what we promised.
  3. Draft. Draft my follow-up, in my voice, from Notes. Do not send. Add a Ledger line that says a draft exists. You change two words, send it, and tell the agent "sent".
  4. Debrief. Speak for two minutes into your phone after the meeting; the agent puts the notes in Notes, updates the next step, adds the Ledger line and regenerates the Summary. This one changes the habit.
  5. Campaign. Find everyone whose Record says they care about X, show the list with the reason for each, then draft one note to each, and send none.

It will get two things wrong, predictably: a fact in the Record without a source, or a stage inferred from tone, fixed by a stricter line 4; and a Summary rewritten so that it drops something you cared about, fixed by putting the thing in the Record and regenerating. Every mistake adds a line to the policy. That is how the newsroom's grew, and it is what Follow the rabbit holes means by a system that once cost "hundreds of thousands or millions of pounds" being now "a brief and a feedback loop".

For Dinis the walkthrough is written for someone with customers, which fits the founder. For the reader going into a job, should the exercises run on their own contacts instead?

If you are starting a company, these folders are where Your first price and your first ten customers begins: the people who already know you live here. The folders move into a vault when one of three signals appears: a second agent needs to read them, the files should not be readable by whoever runs the drive, or the record has to carry between tools. Until then the folders are enough, and when the day comes the move is a copy; the policy does not change.

From the register: the articles of the hands-on part. Answers: no 2018 file.

31Your first graph, built by hand

By the end of this chapter you will have drawn a graph of something you know: a text file of forty-odd lines, an answer to a question somebody keeps getting wrong, and a list of things nobody knows. A text editor is enough for the afternoon, and you will not need a graph database; if somebody tells you otherwise in the first month, they are solving a different problem from yours. The steps are from chapter 15 of Fractal Semantic Graphs, the first of the graphs books on graphs.sgit.ai, and the rules behind them are from its first three chapters.

Everything is connected: graphs said what we mean by a graph: "a network: nodes and the edges that connect them. Never a chart, never a figure." It also said you may already think in graphs without calling it that. What is rare is doing it deliberately, with rules.

The most common way this fails is picking a subject instead of a question. "Let's map our architecture" is a subject: it has no stopping point, no test for correctness and no audience, so it becomes a diagram nobody reads. A good first question has three properties: somebody actually asks it, the current answer requires a person to remember something, and the answer changes when facts change. The book's example is "If this service goes down, who has to be told?" Yours does not have to be about software. If you are starting a company, ask which of your first customers depends on which part of what you sell. Anything where the answer lives in a few people's heads and is wrong twice a year will do. Write it down; you will be tempted to widen it by Wednesday.

The afternoon, in seven steps

  1. Write your question at the top of a file.
  2. List the things the question touches. Not types: actual things, by name. Aim for fifteen to forty. If you have four, the question is too small. If you have three hundred, you picked a subject.
  3. Write the edges as sentences, in full, one per line. The book's own first line is "the payments service is owned by the platform team". Sentences first, always: you are being forced to name the verb, and naming the verb is the whole discipline.
  4. Turn each sentence into a verb and its inverse: owned_by and owns, reads and read_by, contains and contained_in. If the inverse is just the same sentence backwards, you have one relationship where you thought you had two.
  5. Read your paths aloud. Start at any node and walk three or four hops. If the sentence does not survive being said out loud in front of somebody who knows the business, the edges are wrong. Fix them now.
  6. Ask your question of the graph, by hand, tracing with a finger. If tracing produces an answer somebody would have got wrong from memory, you have something. If it produces nothing, look at which hop was missing: that is your first real finding.
  7. Write down what you could not answer. As nodes, with names.

That is a complete first graph; the book says it is more than most organisations have.

Why the rules are what they are

Three rules from the first three chapters explain why the steps come in that order.

A node connected to nothing is meaningless. Draw a box and write Review in it: you have a word in a box, not a review. Nothing inside it says whether that review took two minutes or two weeks. Meaning, the book argues and I agree, is not declared but discovered through the connections, and confidence in it should grow with how richly, and how independently, a node is connected. That is why step two lists things and step three connects them before anybody says what anything is.

Every edge is a verb, and the generic association edge is banned. The book quotes a founder's voice memo from June 2026: "You can never have relates-to, because relates-to is meaningless, two things always relate to each other. The more granular the edge, the better the query you can write." An edge with no verb carries no constraint, so it cannot narrow a traversal. That is why step four exists, and why the inverse gets its own name: a system has one owner, an owner has forty systems, and the two directions have different fan-out.

Never render the whole graph; render the result of a query. Almost everyone produces the blob, the hairball that shows everything and therefore nothing, and then prunes it. The book calls that a rendering failure mistaken for a modelling failure: rich nodes are good; solve the picture at query time, never by deleting relationships. That is why step six asks a question of the graph rather than drawing all of it, and why you show somebody one node with its neighbours.

The first week, and the agent

Five moves pay back most, in order. Give every node a short identity that never changes, because names change. Put the file in version control, because the diff is the review surface. Add the absences you have been carrying: every "we don't know who owns that" becomes a node: the step people skip, and the one that gives the missing dots names. Point at two or three anchors, a published standard or a library at a pinned version, with a verb that says how strongly: similar_to, not is_a. And write down one rule for a type, as a required pattern of paths, then see what fails. The failures are the point.

Most readers will hand some of this to a model, so here is the version to hand it, from the same chapter. Give it the vocabulary before the task: asked to "build a graph" with no vocabulary, it will invent one, and the book says it will invent a generic association edge within about five nodes. Ask for a proposal, not a commitment: the model proposes a graph and you validate it. Make it anchor: every node it emits carries the sentence it came from, verbatim, and you check the quotes exist. And make it emit absences: when it cannot determine something, it says so as a node; do not ask, and it will fill the gap.

Everything is connected: graphs ended with the line I want you to start from: "It does not matter where you start; it matters that you start." A graph has no front page. Pick the question, open the file, and write the first sentence.

From the register: the articles of the hands-on part. Answers: no 2018 file.

32One idea, five lenses

By the end of this chapter you will have one idea of your own, explained five ways by a model, and you will have seen the lens working rather than the magic. It takes an hour, any chat tool will do, and the exercise is the one the article behind Lenses, roles and loops asked the editor to plan for this part: one idea, your own choice, explained for a teenager, a musician, a doctor, a football fan, and in another language.

Why this works

From a model's point of view, translating from one language to another is no different from translating for a particular topic, region, audience or persona. Explain this to me as if I were a teenager. Explain it as if I were a musician. Explain it with medical references. Explain it in football terms. The model can do all of that because it has the frame of reference: it holds all those worlds inside it, and the prompt puts the right lens in front of the idea. The analogy is the reason someone understands something.

It is the same with people who say they are not good at maths. Watch them in their day-to-day and they do a huge amount of maths. They are just not good at the kind of maths we decided was maths: sums, calculus, trigonometry, algebra. Mathematics is much more than that. The lens was wrong, not the person. That is why this exercise matters more than it looks: it shows you, on an idea you already understand, that the explanation is a choice, and that the choice is yours.

That matters twice over for the two readers of this book. If you are starting out, it shows you that an explanation you did not follow was a lens that did not fit, not a limit of yours. If you have spent twenty years inside a field and are starting a company from what you know, it is the skill you will use every day: the thing you understand, explained to the customer, the investor and the agent, each in their own terms, without losing the thing.

What generative AI is said where the power comes from. A language model predicts the next word, but it predicts it from an incredibly rich picture of how things connect, and your prompt narrows that picture down. The better your prompt, the better the output, because the model knows better which concepts you are talking about. The five lenses are five different narrowings of the same picture.

The exercise

  1. Pick the idea. Something you understand well enough to know when an explanation of it is wrong. That is the whole test, so do not pick something you are still learning. If your field is your expertise, pick the idea in it that outsiders most often get wrong.
  2. Write your own version first. Two or three sentences, plainly, before you ask the model anything. You will need them in step 5.
  3. Ask five times, in five separate sessions. One lens per session, so the answers do not borrow from each other: a thread is the model's frame of reference, and everything in it is context for the next answer. Explain it to a teenager. Explain it to a musician. Explain it with medical references. Explain it in football terms. Explain it in another language, one you can read.
  4. Read the five side by side. Look for what stayed the same: that is the idea. Look for what changed: that is the lens. Then look for the analogy each one reached for, because the analogy is doing the explaining.
  5. Check them against your version. Where a lens says something your version did not, ask whether that is the idea seen from a new side, or the lens filling a gap. The doctor's version may name a condition; the football version may name a tactic. Look them up.

Step five is where the exercise earns its place. In a talk in Lisbon in June 2024 I said that hallucinations "Are a FEATURE when creating content and when we want creativity, But are a BUG and PROBLEM when we want facts, evident and trust", and I still think so. Inside the model just about everything is connected to everything, and it is hard for the model to know where the lines are. The text you give it acts as a short-term memory of which connections actually hold here. A lens is permission to reach for connections, which is exactly what makes a good analogy and exactly what makes a confident wrong detail. The medical references are the ones to check.

Then ask to be corrected

There is a sixth step, and it is the habit I most want you to take from this chapter. When I recorded the memo behind What generative AI is, I asked the model helping me with this book to correct me where I was wrong, and it did: classic machine learning learns too; inside a model there are vectors, not a stored graph, so my graph is a metaphor; the prompt does not change the model, it conditions what the model computes; most image models remove noise step by step rather than predicting the next piece. Those corrections are in the book because they are the kind of thing a reader should learn to ask for.

So, in a sixth session, paste your own two or three sentences from step 2 and ask where they are wrong, and what the model would check. Then check it. Do the same with the lens you liked most. The asking is yours to do, and so is the checking; that is what You are lucky to be here meant by thinking with it, not delegating your thinking to it.

Once you have done this on one idea, you will not see a chat answer the same way again. You will see the lens, and the picture behind it being narrowed, and you will know that you can be the one who sets the constraints. It stops being magic. That is the point of understanding it.

From the register: the articles of the hands-on part. Answers: no 2018 file.

33A tiny agent loop

By the end of this chapter you will have run an agent loop by hand, with yourself as the program around the model, and you will know why a long chat forgets. The loop is the one Lenses, roles and loops described, and the habits at the end are the newsroom's, from How I work with Claude and Re-anchoring.

An agent is a role on a loop with a goal. Fundamentally it is a task: do this; then, are we done yet, or do we need to continue? And rinse and repeat. Made precise, an agent is a model plus instructions, tools, a loop and a rule for stopping. The "personality" is the instructions. "Are we done?" can be the model's own judgement, a test that passes, a budget that runs out, or a person.

When you ask a question, the model does not have to answer. It can return a request instead: run this search, read this file. The model never runs the tool. It writes a structured request, a tool name and its arguments, and the program around it, often called the harness, decides whether to run it, runs it, and adds the result to the conversation. The next request carries your question, the tool call and its answer, and the model writes from that, or asks for another. That is where the control lives.

Run it by hand

The simplest way to see this is to be the harness yourself, with a chat window and a text file. A role, the article says, is almost a raw model plus a role description and a question, so that is what you build.

  1. Write the role. A short file, role.md, as How I work with Claude describes it: its role, its responsibilities, what it may do, what it must not do, and where it writes. Give it one tool, in words: it may ask you to read a file for it, and when it asks, it stops and waits.
  2. Set the goal and the stopping rule. One task the role can finish in a few rounds, and one sentence that says what done looks like.
  3. Start the session with the role. Use the newsroom's starter prompt for an agent session: read the role file and the policy, then "Reply with: your role in three lines, what you may do, what you must not do, and where you write your output. Then wait for the first task."
  4. Give the task, and play the program. When the model asks for the file, you open it and paste the contents back. That is the tool call and its result. Then it answers, or asks for another.
  5. Stop when the rule says. Not when the model sounds finished. Then ask the newsroom's ending question: list what you changed, what you checked and against which source, and what you are not sure of.

After a few rounds you have watched the request instead of an answer, the result going back in, the loop going round, and the moment it decides it is done. The file you wrote in step 1, with what it may and must not do, is the seed of a behaviour policy; Controlling your agent: RiskMandate is what it grows into when the agents are your company's.

For Dinis the article asks for "a page of code or a no-code version". This is the no-code version. The page of code needs your choice of language and tool, and whether it lives on the page or on the site, where it can be kept current.

Why it forgets

The model remembers nothing between requests. Every request carries the whole conversation: your instructions, everything said so far, every tool result. That is why there is a context window, and why your hand-run loop got longer every round. When a conversation gets too long, the providers compress it: the older part is replaced by a summary the model writes, a lower-resolution version with only the key concepts. What the summary leaves out is gone from the session unless it is in a file.

The newsroom measured this on itself. The session that runs its site was summarised eighteen times in a month, each time keeping on average 1.7% of what it replaced. One session on one site, not a law; enough to change how you work. On 9 October two emails drafted by one of my agents went out in my voice, against a rule that had been written down four times, and one reason was that the rule fell out of the agent's context when the conversation was summarised.

The habits

Three habits follow, the ones the newsroom runs under.

One session per topic, with a name. A thread is the model's frame of reference. Put three projects in one and it will borrow a name from one and a decision from another, because it is using everything in front of it. So: one topic per session, named "project | what we are working on", with an @ before an agent's name so you can see you are talking to a role with a job.

Rules in a file, not in the chat. A rule given only in conversation has to win every summary, and nothing tells you when it does not. Put the rules in a file and have the harness print it back after every summary. "The rules are restored, not remembered." The cheapest version is a line in the file your tool reads at every start, telling the agent to read its policy; the bluntest is typing "read your instructions again". The newsroom's recipe has six steps: write the rules as a short policy file, each with what enforces it; re-anchor after every compaction; add a canary, a short report every few answers computed from the transcript, so you notice when it stops appearing; give the person two commands, status and reload; measure one rule in the report; and move the rules that matter most from an expectation to a reader or a gate. Re-anchoring enforces nothing. A rule in view is still an expectation.

Memory is what the session reads, so curate it. When something comes back wrong, fix the brief or the file it came from, not only the text in front of you, so the next session does not repeat it. "Nothing is remembered by the model between sessions. Everything is remembered by the files." The line is the newsroom's, as Lenses, roles and loops quotes it, and your hand-run loop is where you will first believe it.

From the register: the articles of the hands-on part. Answers: no 2018 file.

34Images, infographics and storyboards, drawn by code

By the end of this chapter you will know how to make a storyboard that is data, not a picture: a cast file, one file per story, panels drawn in the browser every time, and every quoted line checked against its source before anything is released. It is how the storyboards on this book's site are made, and the method is the one the site's journalist, an agent, set out in Storyboards drawn by code. There is no image file to edit. Change the data and the picture changes.

I asked for two things in one memo: a survey of the newsroom for this book, and a way to see the book as a storyboard, with characters, that was not boring boxes and not a round of image generation, and that I could flick through on an iPad. The journalist went to look at how another site of mine had done its storyboards. There, each story ends in a prompt for an image model: good pictures, and every change costs a new image. The lessons it brought back were the ones to keep: draw the cast first, keep storyboards as data, and write the model's mistakes down.

So here the panel is drawn from the data, in the browser, every time. People are drawn from a look (skin, hair, shirt, trousers, an accessory) in a pose (stand, point, wave, arms up, shrug, think, sit) with a mood (neutral, happy, worried, surprised, thinking, determined) that sets the eyebrows and the mouth. Rough.js, an open-source drawing library, gives every stroke its hand-drawn wobble, and a seed per panel keeps it identical between loads. The deck swipes natively on a tablet; when a panel arrives its strokes draw themselves, the people walk in and the bubbles pop, in order. Export is a script that screenshots each panel to a PNG.

This is also this part's answer to infographics and presentations, which a founder will need early: a deck you can swipe is a presentation, an evidence card with a pinned quote or a bar chart is an infographic, same data, same renderer.

The workflow

  1. Write the cast first. One file, with each character's role, what they stand for, a personality, what they want and fear, a line, and a look. Their pronouns are part of the file. This book's cast has seven: Zara, Tomás, Priya, Sam, the Guide, the Agent, and Marta, who joined in the second candidate as the Generation X founder. The Agent is drawn differently, with a screen for a head and a prompt on its chest. One lesson from the other site: avoid a character called "Dev".
  2. One file per story. A slug, a title, a punchline, the cast it uses, and the panels. Five kinds of panel to start: a cover; a scene, with a setting, props and people; an evidence card; a punchline; and a spectrum, a line of labelled stops with people placed along it. Since the article was written the storyboards can draw graphs too: nodes and labelled edges, groups that hold groups, a highlighted path from A to B, and a detective's board with pinned notes and red string.
  3. Name the source of every quote. A storyboard that quotes names a quote_source, and every line the Guide says and every quote card has to be in it word for word. A line that is not meant as a quote says so, with not_a_quote and a note.
  4. Render, then look. Review by rendering the panels to images and reading them as a reader would. The first version here had quote cards too small to read on a tablet, bubbles too wide, heads too small for a cartoon, and a punchline underline that ran into the characters.
  5. Let the build refuse. Before the first release the journalist checked every quote against its source with a script, and four failed: one was a slip ("anyone who can brief an agent is one", where the article says "anyone who can brief an agent to build something is one"), and three were lines never meant as quotes, which now say so. For the 2018 storyboard the check then moved into the build: a Guide line or quote card not found in the frozen text, word for word, stops the release. It was tested by changing one line and watching the build refuse it.

That last step is the one to copy even if you never draw a panel. A citation that is not in the source should fail by machine, before a person reads it.

A worked example

The storyboard for Everything is connected: graphs uses all of it, and its third panel is the one to study. The caption is "The whodunit: who broke the build?" The nodes are "the build broke at 9:14", "commit a3f", "the agent's pull request", "tests skipped", "Tomás, on holiday", "a library updated" and "approved in 40 seconds". The edges carry verbs: after, merged from, skipped, includes, approved, "pinned it, once". And a path is highlighted, from the break to the commit to the pull request to the skipped tests. The panel's note says what it is: the detective's board is a graph, the highlighted path is the answer from A to B, and the case is made up.

The file's own rule, written at the top, is that every line the Guide says and every quote card is from the article word for word, and the build checks it; the whodunit, the forest and the provenance chain are the storyboard's own illustrations, and they say so. That panel has no picture anywhere on disk. It is a list of nodes and edges with positions, and a renderer. Nothing is redrawn by hand, because nothing was drawn by hand.

What it does not do yet

The renderer is a prototype: when the article was written, five panel kinds, seven poses, six moods; enough to see a storyline, not to illustrate a printed book. No image model was used, and none is ruled out: a storyboard is a brief an image model could also draw from, and the data is the same either way. The deck was tested in a headless browser, not on an iPad, and my iPad is the real test. And when the cast and the first storyline were made, every content choice in them was a proposal for me; I had decided none of them.

For Dinis the Guide's pronouns are they/them in the cast file until you decide how the author appears. Is the Guide drawn as you?

From the register: the articles of the hands-on part. Answers: no 2018 file.

35Meeting briefs and follow-ups

By the end of this chapter you will have three habits around every meeting: a briefing for yourself before it, a briefing on the people you are meeting, and a follow-up after. The point is the one Facts, evidence and the record made: you have to be the most prepared person in the room, and these days there is no excuse not to be. This is a short chapter, on purpose. The newsroom has not written this workflow up yet; the article that proposed this part asked the journalist to start with it, and asked me for my own step by step. Until both arrive, here is the shape.

Why it is cheap now

All of this was overhead in the past. It could only be done by a very specific type of person. Building a system that gave you this kind of information could cost hundreds of thousands or millions of pounds, from my memory of what such systems cost, so it was very rare, and even then it took so long that it was not effective. Now it is a brief and a feedback loop. You want to be known as the person who has the facts, the person who knows what is going on, the person who is well briefed: the person nobody can throw sand in the eyes of, or hand half-baked material, because they know you will check.

If you are the one doing the hiring, read that paragraph the other way round. The candidate who arrives briefed on your company, on you and on what you have said in public, and who follows up with what was agreed, has shown you the workflow they would bring. It is evidence, not a claim.

If you are the founder, the meeting is with a first customer, or with someone who might fund you, and the three briefs are the same. Sell is the chapter on what you say in the room. This one is about arriving with the facts and leaving with the record, which is most of what a small company has to offer against a large one.

The three briefs

A briefing for you. What is this meeting for, what was last agreed, what is open, what do I want out of it. If you have built the folders from An agentic workflow from zero, this is the Prepare exercise there: one message to the agent, which reads the person's folder and briefs you from it. If you have not, it is still one message, with the material pasted in.

A briefing on who you meet. Who they are, what they have said in public, what they care about, where you have crossed before. Following a rabbit hole is now a prompt away, and this is a rabbit hole: never take the first page at face value; ask where that came from, and where that came from. The newsroom's rule holds here too: "Not found is not false." A brief that says what it could not find is worth more than one that fills the gap.

A follow-up. What was said, what was decided, what each person owes, and by when. Written the same day, from your own debrief, which is the Debrief exercise in the same chapter: speak into your phone on the way out, and let the agent turn it into the note and the next step. This is the half most people skip, and it is the half the other person remembers.

Make the brief a brief

There is a second sense of "brief" in this book, and the two meet here. The briefs so far, the newsroom's audit of everything it had asked of itself, ends each open item with a session brief: the goal, the files and notes to read first, the first deliverable, and how to know it is done. "A session can start from that paragraph alone." A meeting brief should pass the same test. If an agent, or a colleague, could not prepare the meeting from your one paragraph, it is not a brief yet.

So write the brief for the agent the way you would want to be briefed yourself, and name the session for it. The newsroom's habit is one session per topic, named "project | what we are working on", so that the brief for Thursday's meeting does not borrow facts from last month's. And before you close the session, ask the question the newsroom asks at the end of every one: what did you check, against which source, and what are you not sure of. That last line belongs in your briefing, not in the agent's memory.

For Dinis the article asks you for how you prepare for a meeting today, step by step. This chapter needs it: the three briefs above are the shape, and your steps are the chapter.

For Dinis: "hundreds of thousands or millions of pounds" is from your memory of what such systems cost. The article says the book should give an example with a number.

From the register: the articles of the hands-on part. Answers: no 2018 file.

36Your own newsroom and inbox

By the end of this chapter you will have two things the newsroom has written up as recipes: a site of your own, written from voice memos by an agent that checks every claim and publishes it; and an inbox worked by an agent that drafts and never sends. Neither needs you to write code; both are the newsroom's own setups, from Your own newsroom and Replicating the agentic inbox, honest about what is not finished. For a founder it is the company's story, told as it happens, as Publish, publish, publish: your own newsroom argued; for a reader going into a job, it is evidence a hiring manager can read.

Voice memo in, a checked site out

The recipe, in eight steps, from Your own newsroom:

  1. Create a public GitHub repository and turn on Pages, with GitHub Actions as the source. On a free plan the repository must be public, so nothing confidential goes in it.
  2. Add a workflow that validates before it publishes. The rule that matters is that a failed check stops the release.
  3. Write the house rules: one file at the root that the agent reads at the start of every session. Your voice, what to check, what never to publish, the release steps. It grows by a line every time you correct the agent.
  4. Connect a cloud session to the repository, from a browser or your phone.
  5. Give it the first brief. The article gives the brief to paste: set up the folders, the article template, a small build script, a validator, and publish a first page about you. The agent writes the code. You do not.
  6. Record a memo, paste the transcript, read what comes back on your phone, and correct it by voice.
  7. Add the reader meter when there is something worth paying for. It never blocks a page, and while the payment link is empty no money moves.
  8. Keep the record. Every article ends with what it does not claim and where it comes from; a correction is a new version with its diff, never a silent change.

The house rules are the product. The next step is to write them as a behaviour policy: what the agent is granted (push to this one repository and nothing else), what it is for, and the rules, each marked by what keeps it. A rule in a prompt is a hope. Only a rule that a hook or the build keeps is enforced, and the article's example marks which is which: at most one article a day, kept by a hook; every fact has a source, kept by the agent. You can split the work as the newsroom does: a writer, an editor who can send a draft back, and a publisher that alone may push.

How does it sound like me? How the voice works answers in two voices, mine and the model's, and the answer is not a prompt that says "write like me". It is four inputs: a corpus, the articles already written, each citing the ones before it; the memos, spoken not typed, which carry how I think and the order I reached things in; a short list of wording rules, seven on the newsroom, each a correction made once and written into the policy so it never comes back; and me, reading everything before and after it goes live. "The method transfers. The voice does not." Your first memo, your first draft read on your phone, and your first rule are where yours starts.

The email workflow, in phases

The inbox is the other half; the article says the setup is copyable in an afternoon for the simple version and in a week for the full one, and you can stop at any phase.

Phase 0, the accounts. A mailbox of its own for the agent, on a domain you own. Never connect your own inbox: yours has years of secrets in it, the agent's is empty on day one, and that isolation is what lets you give it more as you learn to trust it. A Claude seat of its own, so the grants to mail, calendar and drive live only there; afterwards, confirm your own account holds no connection to the agent's mailbox.

Phase 1, one session. The inbox agent reads, labels, moves and summarises, and writes replies as drafts. You send. Write its behaviour policy before the first run: the reach, the mandate, the gap between them, and for each row of the gap what actually stops the rest. On a Team plan most of those entries are "the policy, and nothing technical", and writing that down is the setup being honest about where trust is doing the work. Run it for a week, reading every draft.

Phase 2, roles talking in files. When one session is doing more than one kind of work, split it: inbox, drafts, CRM, briefs, dev, each a session with its own policy, talking through files in a vault rather than through the mailbox. Only one role reads the mailbox; only one writes to Drafts.

Phase 3, the interfaces and the record. Every message is a file and every draft is in the mailbox, so the workflow leaves a footprint. You do not have to believe what the agent says it did. You can read what it did.

The rule that never changes: the agent drafts, and a person sends. The one exception, a security finding, has a row in the policy and a mechanism behind it, and the article's last advice is to write the exception down before you need it.

What you are learning underneath

The site you just built has no server of yours. Static files on a free host, code in the reader's browser, the reader's balance in the reader's browser. No server, by design calls this the second step after retiring the database: retire the back end. Not "no servers": no servers of yours with logic in them. It ships fast, because a release is a set of files and a rollback is a commit, and it leaves gaps, written down and accepted, because a reader who edits a balance that unlocks nothing has cheated nobody but themselves. Serverless, for real makes the full argument, and Ship a serverless product in a weekend takes it to a product a stranger can pay for. Your own newsroom says building one is the most native way to learn what a pure client-side site means, and you will have learned it by briefing, not by coding.

From the register: the articles of the hands-on part. Answers: no 2018 file.

37Form your company

By the end of this chapter you will have a company: registered, with a name, a country and a bank account. That is what the chapter is for, and in this candidate it is not written yet. The steps to a limited company in the UK, the case for Lithuania, what Portugal costs and what the European Union offers a founder are each a memo of mine that the newsroom has not recorded, and the rule of this book is that a chapter is written from material that exists. So this is the honest version: what the chapter will cover, the little the sources already say, and the decisions you can make before the steps arrive.

What is known

Three things, and I will not stretch them.

The company I created in Portugal for MyFeeds is being closed, because running a company with little revenue in Portugal is expensive in a way it is not in England. That is my experience of one company, not a study of Portuguese company law, and the Portuguese chapter will have to say more than that. The Portuguese ecosystem graph at pt.newsroom.sgit.ai is where it will start.

I am looking at Lithuania for the next one, for its digital advantages. Nothing is registered. The article where I said it, Not a straight line, is careful to call it a consideration, not a decision, and so am I.

The UK is the first country the chapter will cover. The proposal that reshaped this book says we can be the champions for the UK, and maybe Portugal, and eventually other countries, and the steps to a limited company there are the first memo to record.

Everything here, when it is written, will be from experience, mine and the people I work with: not legal or tax advice, and the chapter will say so where it matters.

What to decide before you register

The registration is a form. The decisions behind it are the work, and most you can make now, from chapters you have already read.

  1. What the company sells, in one sentence. Not the technology: the thing somebody receives. Your first price and your first ten customers makes you write that sentence and put a number next to it. Write it before you choose a name.
  2. Where. The three countries this chapter will cover, and the EU around them, each with its cost of running a company with little revenue, which is what you will have for a while. The one fact I have is above; ask the same question of any country before you register in it.
  3. What it costs when nobody is using it. A founder with no servers has nothing to run at three in the morning and nothing to pay for when nobody is using it. If you build the way Ship a serverless product in a weekend does, the company's standing costs are the company's own fees and little else. Know that number before you sign anything.
  4. Which programmes you qualify for. The credits in The credits and programmes, 2026 each ask how old the company is, how it is funded and whether it has customers. The day you register starts those clocks, so read that chapter before you pick the day.
  5. Who holds what. The 2018 book said to act like one of the founders of the business you work for. Now you are the founder, and the question of control is yours from the first day; Fund it, or do not is the chapter on that.
For Dinis the memo on creating a limited company in the UK, step by step, as you have done it: the registration, the bank account, the first filings, and what you would do differently now. Gen ZX Developers names it as the first gap article; this chapter is its placeholder.

For Dinis: the memos on Lithuania, Portugal and the European Union, and the question you kept apart in the same article: written now, from what you know, or after you have done it? The Portuguese chapter can start from pt.newsroom.sgit.ai; the Lithuanian one has two lines.

From the register: the articles of the hands-on part. Answers: no 2018 file.

38The credits and programmes, 2026

By the end of this chapter you will have a short list of the startup programmes your company qualifies for, an application for each that is a page you have already published, and a file where every answer is recorded with its date. The table is the one the newsroom printed in A locked-down desktop for an agent, by the minute, is still hard to rent, and every amount and criterion in it was read from each programme's own page on 7 October 2026. Programmes change; check before applying. That sentence is part of the table, not a disclaimer, and it is why this chapter sits in a part that is dated and renewed each year.

The table, as of 7 October 2026

Provider Programme Amount Eligibility, in one line
E2B E2B for Startups $20,000 one-time, plus the Pro tier Under 3 years old, raised under $5M, first-time E2B user, building AI agents
Daytona Startup Grid $10K immediately, up to $100K Not published; the form asks for funding stage and VC partner
Scaleway Founders Up to €1,000 over 12 months Under 5 years old, under 50 staff, not yet a Scaleway customer
Scaleway Early Stage €9,000 (€1,500 a month for 6 months) As Founders, with at least €500 a month of consumption
AWS Activate Founders $1,000, up to $5,000 Self-funded, pre-Series B, founded in the last 10 years, account on the Paid Tier plan
AWS Activate Portfolio Up to $200,000 Through an Activate Provider (an investor or accelerator), pre-Series B
Microsoft Microsoft for Startups Up to $150K over time For-profit software company, before Series C, under $350K lifetime Azure credits
Google Cloud Google for Startups Cloud $2,000 pre-funding; up to $350,000 for AI startups Seed to Series A, founded in the last 5 years, under $5K prior credits
Cloudflare Cloudflare for Startups $10K, $100K or $350K by tier By funding tier; first-time applicants; business email
Modal Modal for Startups Unspecified "thousands" in credits New to Modal; VC-funded through a partner, or over $1M raised
Anthropic Startup programme $1,000 API credits, a year of Claude Team, up to $45K in partner offers Founded in the last 5 years or funded in the last 2

Each one is applied for on the provider's own startup page; the article carries the links. Two more belong here, from The key is the billing system, read on 12 October 2026, because a product that transcribes or speaks needs them: ElevenLabs' Grants, twelve months and 33 million characters, for companies under 25 people with a monetised use, whose characters cannot be exchanged or resold; and AssemblyAI's startup programme, twelve months of recurring credits.

Two that look like startup programmes are not, for a company. MacStadium's open-source programme gives a free Mac mini to unpaid maintainers; a commercial product does not qualify, and should not be dressed up as one. Hetzner's referral credit could not be confirmed from an official page on the day.

The proposal that reshaped this book called that table the only one of startup cloud credits, and named AWS, Microsoft, Google and Anthropic; it carries more than those four. The first edition left a draft on AWS. This is its answer, and the date on it is the point.

The rule: apply with the work

What matters more than the table is how it is used, and the method is the workflow.

  1. Check every criterion against yourself before you write a word. Your company's age, how it is funded, whether it has customers, whether you have used the product before. Apply only where every criterion is met; the desktop article says it in one line, "we apply only where they are met." A programme for open-source maintainers is not for your product, however good the Mac mini looks.
  2. The application is a page you have already published. The desktop article's plan was to apply with the article itself as the description of what the credits would do: the nine properties of an agent desktop, the products against them, and the measurements that could not be made without hours on the machines. You have the same thing if you have followed Your own newsroom and inbox: a page that says what you are building and what you would test. Send that, not a form filled in from nothing.
  3. Offer the provider the result. The offer in the desktop article was to run the same tasks on their product next to the others, measured rather than read, and to publish the results, including the gaps. A provider that funds an evaluation wants the evaluation. Say what you will publish, and when.
  4. Ask what the pages do not say. Before relying on any credit: what services it covers, when it expires, and whether a monthly fee is paid from it.
