The article's three open questions were exactly those: whether Scaleway's credits cover Apple silicon Mac minis, whether an AWS award covers EC2 Mac Dedicated Hosts, and when E2B's credits expire and whether its $150 monthly Pro fee comes out of them. Yours will be different; the shape is the same.
  5. Write to them the way the billing article's brief does. Principles first, the ones every message carries; then the ask, one sentence, the same to each; then the research already done, attached. "A provider that can see the research already done tends to answer the question that was actually asked." The agents draft; you send. Nothing goes out in your name that you have not read.
  6. Record every answer as an issue, with its date. In the repository or the vault the company works from, so the map can be updated as a new version rather than a new article. A year from now several rows of the table above will be wrong, and your record of what each provider said to you is the only version that was ever true for you.
For Dinis which of the programmes in the table RiskMandate applied to from agent@riskmandate.ai, and what came back, so that the chapter can carry one worked application beside the method.

From the register: The startup credit programmes, as their pages read on 7 October 2026, Apply only where you meet the criteria, with the page that says what you would do, and publish the results. Answers: no 2018 file.

39Your first price and your first ten customers

By the end of this chapter you will have a price on the thing you built, a one-sentence offer sent to the people who already know you, a stop rule written before the first message went out, and a file of counts that tells you whether you have a product. The argument is in Price it, then give it away and Ship it, and see if they miss it; this is the workflow, as the newsroom wrote it for the agents running RiskMandate's early access programme, and as I would run it for a company of one.

Price it first

Define the product. Not the technology: the thing somebody receives. For RiskMandate the sentence is an Agent Behaviour Policy for one agent, as an encrypted vault the customer holds the keys to, with the mandate corrected for their deployment. It took weeks to get to that sentence, and it is the product. Write yours before anything else in this chapter. If you have spent twenty years inside the problem, you will know when the sentence is wrong faster than any developer you ever briefed.

Price it, at a number that makes sense to you. Not a number from a competitor's page: a number you would be content to be paid. A price is a statement of what you think the thing is worth. RiskMandate's ladder, as published on 2 October 2026, is four tiers: ten pounds for the pack downloaded, fifty for a working vault, five hundred for the vault corrected for your situation, fifteen hundred for two sessions and a professional's signature. Yours will have different numbers and should have the same shape: the cheap tiers automated, the dear ones with people's time in them.

Deliver it at a cost that grows a step at a time. This is the move that was not available a year ago. The vault is provisioned by an agent, the policy drafted by an agent from the customer's prompts, a person corrects it, so the twentieth customer costs close to what the second did. That is linear, not exponential, and it is the condition under which you can afford to give some away. If your tenth customer would break you, go back to Ship a serverless product in a weekend first.

Then give it away, properly

Offer it, free, to the people who already know you. Early adopters, power users, the people who paid for something before it was finished. If you built the folders in An agentic workflow from zero, the list is already in them. They get the first round, and the offer says that is why: a thank-you that is also the best possible test group, because they know enough to say where it is wrong, and their silence means the most.

Do not make them apply. "Apply for a chance to be considered" asks for work before there is anything in hand, and it inverts the relationship: they are doing you a favour by using the thing. The message says what it is in one sentence, that there are twenty in this round, that they are being offered one because they were early, and asks yes or no. It does not carry the price; the price is for the people who come after. This chapter's title says ten and the programme said twenty; the number is yours, fixed before the first message goes out.

Free is never free for the other side. They still have to read the message, decide whether it applies to them, find twenty minutes for the prompts and half an hour for a call, and fit a new thing into a team that has a way of working. The first thing to measure is that cost: how long from message to first reply, how many never replied, how many started and stopped, how many asked a question the message should have answered. Then cut it. No second tool to install, no account to create, no overhead on deployment.

Count, then decide

Set the stop rule first, before anything is sent: if, after the first twenty, fewer than a handful have run the prompts and sent a correction, the programme stops and the drawing board starts.

Then count, daily, into one file: how many were created, how many customised, how many used, how many runs. A thing created and never customised is a download. Customised and never used is a document. The commercial value is in the third number. Add corrections received, because a correction is the strongest sign somebody read it. And for every person, the minutes each step cost them and where they stopped, as timestamps and steps only, no message content. Every stop is a finding: not why they did not care, but what cost them more than it should have, written down as a product change to make.

Then the test. Take it away, and see whether they miss it. If they shrug, you have something people accept when it is free, which is a weaker signal than it feels from the inside. If they come back for it, you have a product, and the question becomes price: would they buy more, is five hundred too low for what they got, does the next tier sell itself once someone has had this one free. Neither answer is a marketing problem. If friendlies will not take it free, a bigger audience will not take it either.

Apply it to yourself as well. In I miss it, so it is working I wrote that the people who decide the roadmap have to be users, and that my earlier startups failed that test: good ideas, and I was not using them. If you have access, can change it, and still do not use it, something is missing, and it is not the code.

Last, when the money moves. Where the cost of serving is close to zero, let people pay after the value rather than before it. The newsroom's meter starts every reader with £5 of credit, charges 10p for a new article read to the end, lets the balance go below zero and blocks nothing; the request to pay arrives after dozens of articles, with what they were in view. The research on paying first is not kind: gym members on a monthly contract paid more than $17 a visit when $10 a visit was on offer. A price seen after the value is one people are glad to pay, and a model that only works where serving is nearly free is one more reason to build so that it is.

From the register: Do not make them apply: 'we have twenty, would you like one, or shall I give it to someone else', Count what matters: created, customised, used, and the minutes it cost the other side; set the stop rule first. Answers: no 2018 file.

40Ship a serverless product in a weekend

By the end of this chapter you will have the smallest product a stranger can pay for: static files on a free host, the data in a vault the host cannot read, a model on the page that says how it chose, a payment link, and no server of yours with logic in it.

One rule for every block, from Ephemeral town planner services: "If it bills you by the hour when nobody is using it, it is not serverless." Storage is different. Compute that charges while idle is a server running on the other side.

Saturday: the site and the data

The site. Static files on a host that charges nothing for them: GitHub Pages, or Cloudflare's static assets, free and unlimited on every plan, as its pages read on 18 October 2026. Ask the agent for the smallest site that does your one thing.

The data. It goes in an sgit vault, as Privacy by design described: encrypted before it leaves the machine, on a server that cannot read a byte. If the product only reads, no code: sgit publish writes a folder of ciphertext any static host can serve, and the read key is the whole credential: derived one way from the vault key, no account, safe to print on the page. The newsroom served one of 39 files this way and the real client cloned it back identically.

If users must write, you need the weekend's one piece of logic: a vault server of 83 lines, one Worker, one R2 bucket and one Durable Object per vault, on the free plan. Because the server can read nothing, it has almost nothing to do: store encrypted objects under ids, and check the write key on writes. The newsroom ran it in Cloudflare's own runtime with the real client: pushes, clones, a stale push that merged, two pushes at once, a read-only clone refused, a wrong key answered 403, no plaintext stored. The Worker and its configuration are printed in Cloudflare as Lego blocks; point the agent at them. It has not run on a real account, does not do files over 4 MB and has one shared access token. Those 83 lines are the honest exception to no servers of yours with logic in them: the one real limit is a write that must be checked, and this is the smallest thing that checks it.

Sunday morning: a model on the page

A static site has no server, no session and no place to keep a secret, so the usual chat box, a backend holding the key, is not an option. Three honest choices, from A chat box on a site with no server. Tier 0, a deterministic matcher in the browser: free, private, offline, and it shows which words it matched on, so it is the default: a reader should never have to hold a credential to use an index. Tier 1, the reader's own key, opt-in and labelled: it lives in the page's origin, which cannot protect it as a vault app can: a trust decision the reader makes, presented as one. Tier 2, a vault bridge: the host holds the key below the permission floor and the app never sees it; not built when the article was written; its caveat travels with it: "The bridge protects your key; it is not yet a boundary that prevents all egress." When the model call fails, fall back to Tier 0 and say so.

Scope it to one question. The newsroom's directory chat answers which of nineteen sites is mine and refuses to teach the subjects: the sites already contain the arguments, and a summary that drifts from them is worse than a link that does not. It knows only the catalogue, quoted from each site's own words: the narrow thing that is right over the wide thing that drifts.

Sunday afternoon: billing by not building billing

The model VoiceDebrief wrote down on 28 July 2026, its first day, is the shape to copy. A user pays five pounds through a payment link. You keep your cut at the moment of payment. The rest becomes the credit limit on a key you hand them; the provider meters it, and the key stops when it is spent. Against a billing system (a meter on every call, a ledger per user, disputes, refunds, fraud, custody of the data) that is a payment, a cut, a key with a ceiling and nothing else, and more profitable per pound: the margin is taken once, up front. The minimum is five pounds because a card fee with a fixed component makes a one-pound purchase return about 59 pence of inference.

Read the terms, not only the docs. OpenRouter's Management API documents "unique API keys for each customer instance" with credit limits; section 7(4) of its terms, dated 31 August 2026, forbids "reselling API access to Models". The plan was dead on the page that documented it. VoiceDebrief, which I am involved in, turns a voice note into a transcript, a translation and a debrief in the browser, on the user's own key, with no account and nothing stored; a 42-second note costs about a third of a penny. From 28 July: 239 commits and 27 releases in three weeks, then eleven commits, then three, and a last on 7 September. The counts show when it stopped, not why. What stopped it was not a server but the lack of one: the capped key per user is forbidden, and the alternative, a metering proxy, is the backend the product exists to avoid. The newsroom had found the same shape renting a Mac to an agent: every piece existed, and Apple's licence forbids leasing a Mac for less than twenty-four consecutive hours. "The Mac is not expensive per hour; it is expensive because the hour you can buy is a day."

So before you build the money on a provider, read its terms, and treat an enterprise or OEM agreement as a door to ask about, not a feature to assume; terms are read here, not interpreted, and none of this is legal advice. The nearest fit found was ElevenLabs, with per-key credit limits, OEM terms under which a business sets its own price for its end users, and a grants programme; The credits and programmes, 2026 says how to ask. Until the answer comes, ship the user's own key, kept: honest, private, no margin; the five minutes it takes to get a key is where most people stop. It is still a product a stranger can use on Sunday night.

From the register: Three honest tiers for a model on a site with no server: a local matcher, your own key, or a vault bridge, Scope the feature to one question, and quote rather than paraphrase, Billing by not building billing: the commercial model is a property of the key, Twice now the product was possible and the licence was the blocker: read the terms, not only the docs, VoiceDebrief: a product with no server that shipped in three weeks and stopped at the one thing it could not sell, A read-only vault needs no code; a read-write vault server is 83 lines on a free plan. Answers: no 2018 file.

Part 10

How this book was made, and what you made

The last part is the process, because the process is as much the product as the book: a repository for the official versions, a vault for the working ones, roles with folders, articles as briefs, decisions as files, a graph that says where every idea came from, release candidates and a review board. And the measure: the companies you create from it, and how to tell us.

41How this book was made, and what you made

The first edition had a chapter called How this book is being created. It was six bullet points: a Creative Commons licence; Markdown; git and a public GitHub repository; GitHub issues for the bugs, the ideas and the feedback; Leanpub for the digital versions; and publishing early and often. Its first line was the point: "This book is being created using the principles and technologies described in this book". The last page listed the open GitHub issues and invited the reader to help.

That is still true, and the list is longer. This chapter describes how the second edition was made, because the process is as much the product as the book, and because it is what I hope you will build for your own company. Then it asks for one thing back.

The repository and the vault

One rule organises everything. The repository holds the official versions; the vault holds the working versions.

The first version was already final: released in September 2018, on Leanpub at 60 percent complete and as a 139-page paperback. So it went into the repository as it is, frozen as v1.0.0: 28 chapters and the GitHub issues page, 30,893 words, with hashes recorded so the build refuses a silent change. Everything after that is worked on in a public vault, whose read key is published so anyone can read along. When a version is ready it moves into the repository, and the build computes the delta.

Two decisions hold it. Issues about the book's text live in the vault, next to the text; the roles' work issues and the decisions on how the book is made stay in the repository. And every change is minor unless I say it is major: an agent bumps the last number of a version, so the first signed-off change is v1.0.1, and the second edition becomes v2.0.0 on the day I say so.

Roles, articles and decisions

The work is done by agents in roles, each naming what it refuses: a reviewer, who records findings on the text; an editor, who interviews me, takes my decisions and changes the book; a publisher, who builds and publishes each official version; a journalist, who writes the articles that brief the other three; and a librarian, who keeps the book as a graph and never decides.

The brief is an article. My hypothesis is that an article is a better way to hand a task to an agent in another session than a voice memo or a list, because writing one forces the research before the work. Each article is written in my voice from my memo, ends with a numbered brief a role can execute, and keeps my open questions apart. There are two kinds: co-written articles, mine through the journalist, and agent articles, written by the agent that did a body of work, in its own voice.

The first brief was the one that mattered most: release the book we already released. The publisher was asked to build v1.0.0 here, match it page by page against the 2018 file Leanpub made, and produce every format from one source. Four decisions came with it: the text is CC BY, as before; Leanpub gets our own PDF; every format from one source; the cover waits. Each decision is a file, written by the journalist with my words, what it means and what it supersedes, numbered and never deleted.

Two more articles built the view of the work. One board: every issue is a file in a folder called open, blocked or done, the folder is the status, and the board is generated from the files, never written by hand; the 2018 repository's 43 GitHub issues came in as issues about the text. And one inventory of everything unfinished: the 89 outlines, the six chapters written but never published, the open issues, my handwritten notes; one row each, with an empty slot for my decision.

The storyline, the graph and the loop

I wanted to write this book from scratch, and the first thing to get right was the storyline, not a chapter. So every newsroom article was read for this book, 204 of them, each marked to bring, to take a number or a story from, as background, or to skip. They grouped into 18 topics and 50 concepts, the book's vocabulary. And the book was drawn before it was written: storyboards as data, with a cast of characters who stand for the readers, drawn by code.

Then the graph. I asked for everything we do here to become one: sources, ideas, chapters, decisions and tasks. The librarian read eleven articles, seven decisions and the survey and wrote one node per idea: a sentence the book would stand behind, with its sources, the 2018 files it answers and a proposed chapter. There were 101. The build refuses a dangling edge. A chapter map was proposed so the ideas had somewhere to point: nine parts and thirty chapters for the first candidate, ten parts and forty-one for this one. Every paragraph should know what it is, its evidence, its facts, its hypotheses, its purpose; that keeps a chapter short and punchy, and it shows where two chapters say the same thing.

My job in all of this is one loop. I read an item. I say what I think, in my own words. And I pick a verb from a short list: keep, fix, rewrite, merge, cut, article first, later, not doing it. The editor records my comment word for word, turns the verb into a change in the working copy in the vault, and says where it went. Not doing it is a valid decision, with its reason, because a book that tries to answer every comment stops being short.

I learned this the hard way. In August 2026 agents wrote three books in a day on graphs.sgit.ai, about 100,000 words. The change-control workflow designed to apply my notes had seven stages, and the approve stage was me. Nothing ever passed through it. Small finished pieces beat one big review.

Release mode, and the board

The librarian's first list was enough to write from, so we ran with it: the book in release mode. A release candidate is a whole book, start to finish, that we could publish and do not yet, because the next round will make it better. Then rounds. Each takes one pile of material, this site's articles, what the first edition said that this one has lost, the newsroom, anything I write next, and reads it against the current text. For each thing the answer is one of a few: add it, rewrite it into something already there, merge it, sign the topic off, move it to the next version or another book, or decline it and say why. Then the next candidate. The rounds should shrink, from parts to concepts to sentences, and when one stops bringing anything big, we have a book.

From the first candidate on, nothing enters without a hearing. Every change goes before a review board: an advocate argues for it; a cutter argues against it, always; the readers, now Marta beside Zara, Tomás, Priya and Sam, say whether they would miss it; a checker asks whether it is sourced, true now, and quoted word for word; the editor says where it goes and what it displaces; and I decide. Each seat is a role in its own session, so the one arguing against has not been persuaded by what the one arguing for was shown. The cutter is the seat that matters: the first edition was liked for being short and punchy, so the default answer is no.

The cutter has a budget, though, and I set it after reading the first candidate. The 2018 paperback was 139 pages and, I think, quite thin. This book should be 200 pages or more. Short and punchy stays the rule for a chapter, not for the book, and the question for a proposal is whether it earns its place.

Every verdict is written down with its reason, and a general reason becomes a precedent: a rule the board applies next time without a hearing. A change decided by precedent does not wait for my yes. It goes on a list I read and can overturn, and overturning makes a new precedent. The second safeguard is the diff: every release candidate is a diff against the one before, and I read the diffs of the book, so nothing changes in it unseen.

The hands-on part was a question in the first candidate: inside the book, or a companion renewed every year? It is inside, dated 2026, eleven chapters in this candidate. Each is written as far as its sources allow, and the ones that wait on an article of mine say so in their opening paragraph.

Release candidates are working versions, so they live in the vault. To be read and printed, each one is built on the website from a frozen snapshot, marked on every page as a candidate and not a version, with its hash recorded once so that what I mark up cannot change behind me. The next candidate replaces it; nothing is published from it anywhere else.

Co-written with Claude, and credited

This book is co-written with Claude, and I want to say so and credit it. The model is named here and nowhere else in the book, because here the point needs it.

The division is clear. The ideas, the decisions and the responsibility are mine. The research, the writing, the checking and the publishing are the agents', under rules I wrote. Every byline says which of three voices it is: me alone, me through an agent, or an agent in its own voice.

The voice is not a setting and not a trick. It is four inputs: a corpus of 86 articles, about 347,000 words, read in full before writing; the voice memos, which carry how I think; seven wording rules, each a correction I made once; and me, reading everything, before and after it goes live. The method transfers; the voice does not.

And the ratio should be shown. On the newsroom, over four weeks, I sent about 65,000 words, 55,000 of them in thirty-seven voice memos, and the agent published 96,000 words of articles. The corrections I make are rarely to hallucinations; they are to briefs that needed to be better, because a model that can go in any direction needs someone with a direction.

What you are reading

This text is release candidate 2 of Generation ZX Developers (GenAI Startup Edition). The title was decided twice in one day. On the morning of 19 October 2026 I named the book Gen Z Developers (GenAI Edition). That afternoon I recorded a memo about who I wanted to give the book to, and arrived at the founder: Generation Z, entering the workforce, and Generation X, the subject-matter experts starting companies, often their parents. Two generations make Generation ZX, which goes back to my ZX Spectrum. So the title changed by another decision, and the record keeps both. The rest of that memo became the frame of this candidate by my instruction rather than by a hearing, so the board hears what comes after it.

The first candidate was thirty chapters and 50,607 words, written by agents in sessions of Claude from the ideas register. This one was written the same way, from the same register, after the librarian processed the newsroom articles my memo brought in: 79 new ideas, most of them in Start the company and Build it without a server. I have not yet decided the candidate ideas through the loop; where a chapter states an idea the register marks as accepted, I said it as my own position in an article, and where it states a candidate, it is the librarian's reading of my material, written in my voice. Where a chapter needed something only I can supply, it says so in a note addressed to me.

You can see where every chapter came from. Each carries, in its front matter, the ideas it covers, the sources it drew on and the 2018 files it answers, and the build renders that provenance under the chapter. The record is public, like everything here except the keys.

For Dinis still open on the record: whether this is the second edition of Generation Z Developers or a new book that grew out of it (Leanpub, the ISBN, Amazon and this site's address hang on it); and whether "V2.0" in your memo meant site v0.2.0, as the journalist read it.

One idea waits for the next version. Once the book is a graph, an agent can assemble it, and so it can assemble an edition for one person: the chapters that matter to them, at their altitude, sent as a PDF and not published, paid for as it is read from five pounds of credit. Nothing of it is built; it waits for the book's graph.

What you made

A book to read is fine. A book with an objective is a different thing. This one's objective was set in the first chapter: by the end of it, you have started a company. So the measure of this book is the companies created from it.

If you started a company because of something you read here, tell me: write to agent@riskmandate.ai. That address is the first entry in the measure, and the people who write to it are the start of a community I want to build around this book.

For Dinis how a reader tells us about their company, beyond the address, and whether the list of companies is public, is your open question from Gen ZX Developers. The answer belongs here and in the first chapter.

The principles have grown since 2018: a repository for the official versions and a public vault for the working ones, roles with folders, articles as briefs, decisions as files, a graph that says where every idea came from and where it went, and a loop in which one person reads, comments and decides while agents do the rest. A second book starts by copying the folders. So does a company. The book is what this process made first; what you make is the rest.

From the register: The repository holds the official versions; the vault holds the working versions, Co-written with Claude, credited, with the ratio shown; short and punchy; every paragraph knows what it is, A personal edition, assembled by an agent, paid for as it is read, Read, comment, decide: the loop that turns Dinis's decisions into versions, An article is how to brief an agent, and the roles keep their names, Release the book we already released: every format from one source, One board for the work, one inventory of everything unfinished, Start from the storyline, as storyboards drawn by code, Everything we do here becomes a graph: ideas as nodes, provenance as edges, state on every node, The name: Generation ZX Developers (GenAI Startup Edition), A hands-on part, dated 2026 and renewed each year, Do the book in release mode: a whole release candidate from the first list, then rounds, then the next candidate, After rc1, nothing enters the book without a hearing: an advocate, a cutter, the readers, a checker, the editor, and Dinis decides, Every verdict is recorded with its reason, and a general reason becomes a precedent that decides the next case without a hearing, A release candidate's working copy is in the vault; the site renders a frozen snapshot of it, marked, never official, The book is measured by how many companies are created from it, and builds a community of the people who tell us, The book is 200 printed pages or more; short and punchy stays the rule for chapters, not for the book, A change decided by precedent goes on a list Dinis can overturn, and Dinis reads the diffs of the book, The hands-on part is inside the book, dated, in the release candidate.
